QUESTIONS PRESENTED a . 

1. Whether a federal prisoner who demonstrates that he is incarcerated for conduct that 
this Court conclusively determined does not constitute a federal crime has made “a substantial 
showing of the denial of a constitutional right” warranting issuance of a certificate of 
appealability under 28 U.S.C. § 2255. 

2. Whether a defendant's failure to raise an argument on direct appeal, when that 
argument was foreclosed by binding circuit precedent and had been uniformly rejected by other 
circuits, constitutes a knowing and intelligent "waiver," precluding any showing of “cause and 
prejudice" or other excuse for the procedural default in a collateral review proceeding. 

3. Whether a defendant who possesses a firearm and drugs in close proximity to each 
other, but neither bears the firearm on his person, nor otherwise transports it, commits the crime 


under 18 U.S.C. § 924(c) of “carr[ying]” a firearm “during and in relation to” the predicate 


offense of possession of the drugs with intent to distribute them.

PETITION FOR A WRIT OF CERTIORARI 


Amold F. Hohn respectfully petitions for a writ of certiorari to review the judgment of 

the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit in this case. 
OPINIONS BELOW 

The opinion of the court of appeals (Pet. App. la-7a) is reported at 99 F.3d 892. The 
order denying rehearing en banc (Pet. App. 21a) over the dissent of Judges Beam, Hansen, 
Loken, and McMillian is unreported. The opinion of the court of appeals affirming petitioner's 
conviction on direct appeal (Pet. App. 14a-20a) is reported at 8 F.3d 1301. 

JURISDICTION 

The order of the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit denying Hohn's 
petition for rehearing was entered on February 10, 1997. Pet. App. 2la. The jurisdiction of 
this Court is invoked under 28 U.S.C. § 1254(1). 

STATUTORY PROVISIONS INVOLVED 

Section 2253(c) of Title 28 of the United States Code provides, in pertinent part: “Unless 
a Circuit justice or judge issues a certificate of appealability, an appeal may not be taken to the 
court of appeals from * * * the final order in a proceeding under Section 2255. * * * A 
certificate of appealability may issue * * * only if the applicant has made a substantial showing 
of the denial of a constitutional right.” 

The Due Process Clause of the Fifth Amendment of the Constitution of the United States 
provides, in pertinent part: “No person shall * * * be deprived of * * * liberty * * * without 
due process of law * * *.” 

Section 924(c)(1) of Title 18 of the United States Code provides, in pertinent part: 


“Whoever, during and in relation to any crime of violence or drug trafficking crime * * * uses 


2 
or carries a firearm, shall, in addition to the punishment provided for such crime * * *, be 
sentenced to imprisonment for five years * * *, nor shall the term of imprisonment imposed 
under this subsection run concurrently with any other term of imprisonment * * *.” 
STATEMENT 

Arnold Hohn was convicted and is presently incarcerated for conduct — purportedly 
“using” a firearm by merely having it “available” to him during his possession of a controlled 
substance — which this Court held in Bailey v. United States, 116 S. Ct. 501 (1995), is not a 
crime. After learning of the Bailey decision, Hohn filed a motion secking collateral relief 
pursuant to 18 U.S.C. § 2255 from his unlawful incarceration. 

The district court denied Hohn’s collateral challenge, finding that he had “waived” it by 
failing to prognosticate that this Court, several years after Hohn’s conviction had become final, 
would reverse well-settled precedent and redefine the meaning of the criminal statute under 
which he was charged. Pet. App. 8a-9a. The court of appeals refused to accept Hohn's appeal, 
relying on a provision of the new Anti-Terrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act (AEDPA), 
28 U.S.C. 2253(c), which allows an appeal to be taken from the denial of a Section 2255 motion 
only upon the issuance of a “certificate of appealability” (which in turn requires the movant to 
make “a substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right”). 28 U.S.C. § 2253(c). In 
the court of appeal’s view, Hohn’s claim that he was incarcerated for conduct that was not a 
crime did not meet that standard because Hohn was asserting the denial of a “statutory” rather 
than a “constitutional” right. 

1. On June 20, 1990, police armed with a no-knock warrant searched Hohn’s home after 


a confidential informant told them that Hohn was selling methamphetamine. Pet. App. 15a. 


act 


™ 
~~ 


3 
They testified that they found three firearms near methamphetamine in the kitchen (Tr. 163-168), 
two firearms in a box in the kitchen (Tr. 418-420), and one firearm a few feet away from 
methamphetamine in the bedroom (Tr. 212). In Hohn’s bedroom, the police also observed a 
wall-mounted gun case containing a collection of rifles and shotguns (Tr. 508-509). When they 
arrested him in his living room, Hohn was not carrying any weapon and no weapons were found 
in the living room. Pet. App. 3a; Tr. 367-368. 

At trial, no witnesses testified that they had observed Hohn use or carry any weapon 
during and in relation to his predicate offense. Hohn admitted possession of the methamphet- 
amine and firearms but testified that he owned the firearms because he is an avid hunter and 
gunsmith, and because his house had been burglarized and vandalized repeatedly. Pet. App. 3a; 
Tr. 1107-1110. Only one witness, Cindy Vandry, testified to having purchased, or witnessed 
a purchase, of drugs from Hohn. Vandry admitted that she had never seen Hohn use or carry 
any weapon (Tr. 454, 456), and that the only weapons she had observed when she visited his 
home were located in a closed, wall-mounted gun case (Tr. 454-455). 

Consistent with then-controlling Eighth Circuit precedent and Eighth Circuit Model Jury 
Instructions (see note 1, infra), the trial court gave the following instruction to the jury on the 
meaning of the terms “use” and “carry” in 18 U.S.C. § 924(c): 

The phrase ‘used a firearm’ means having a firearm available to aid in the commission 

of possession of Methamphetamine with intent to distribute. Similarly, the phrase 

‘carried a firearm’ does not require proof of actual possession of a firearm or use of it 

in any affirmative manner, but does require proof beyond a reasonable doubt that the 


firearm was available to provide protection in connection with the possession of 
Methamphetamine with intent to distribute or to facilitate success. 


4 


Pet. App. 31a; see also id. at 29a-30a. The trial judge gave the instruction over the objection 
of Hohn’s attorney, who argued that it improperly “allowed the jury to find that merely having 
a firearm available” nearby during a drug offense constitutes “use.” Pet. App. 28a-30a. 

Hohn was convicted of drug possession, firearm possession, and “using or carrying” a 
firearm in violation of Section 924(c). On July 8, 1992, Hohn was sentenced to 90 months — 
30 months for other crimes and 60 months (to be served consecutively) for the Section 924(c) 
conviction. Hohn has been incarcerated for the last three years solely based on his conviction 
for “using or carrying” a firearm in violation of Section 924(c). 

2. Hohn appealed to the Eighth ¢ « cuit, but did not challenge the “use or carry” jury 
instruction or his conviction under Section 924(c). At the time of his appeal, the Eighth Circuit 
— like most other courts — had repeatedly interpreted the term “use” in Section 924(c) 
expansively to include the “mere presence and availability” of a firearm in a house in which 
drugs were discovered.' Thus, the jury instruction given at Hohn’s trial was consistent with 
controlling, well-established Eighth Circuit law, and any argument to the contrary would have 
been rejected, if not dismissed as frivolous. Hohn’s conviction was affirmed. Pet. App. 14a- 
20a. 

3. Several years after Hohn’s conviction was affirmed, this Court issued its decision in 


Bailey, substantially narrowing the range of conduct the lower courts had deemed criminal under 


'  E.g., United States v. Boykin, 986 F.2d 270, 274 (8th Cir. 1993) (“The government does not need to 
show that the defendant was in actual physical possession of the firearm, or that he brandished it or discharged 
it.”); United States v. Sykes, 977 F.2d 1242, 1248 (8th Cir. 1992) (“It is inconsequential that the gun was 
located in a different room from where the drug transactions occurred.”); United States v. Drew, 894 F.2d 
965, 968 (8th Cir. 1990) (“the mere presence and ready availability of a firearm at a house where drugs [have 
been] dealt” is sufficient to support a 924(c) conviction); EIGHTH CIRCUIT MANUAL OF MODEL JURY 
INSTRUCTIONS (CRIMINAL) § 6.18.924, at 197-199 & n.9 (1994). 


5 

Section 924(c)’s “use” prong. The Court held that, to “use” a firearm within the meaning of 
Section 924(c), a person must “active[ly] employ{]” it by “brandishing, displaying, bartering, 
striking with, * * * firing or attempting to fire” the weapon. Jd. at 508-509. The Court 
explicitly overruled those circuit court opinions, like the Eighth Circuit's, in which the only 
nexus required between a firearm and drugs was the “availability,” “accessibility,” or 
“proximity” of the firearm to the defendant during the predicate offense. Jd. at 506, 508. 

Because Hohn had neither “used” a firearm as this Court interpreted that term in Bailey, 
nor “carried” one, and hence was incarcerated for conduct that Section 924(c) does not 
criminalize, he moved to vacate, set aside, or correct his sentence pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 2255. 
The government conceded in its response to Hohn’s Section 2255 motion that the jury instruction 
on firearm “use” contravened this Court's decision in Bailey. Pet. App. 23a. Nevertheless, the 
district court dismissed Hohn’s petition because, in its view, Hohn had knowingly and 
intelligently “waived” his right to relief by failing to “raise his Bailey claim” on direct appeal, 
several years prior to the date on which Bailey was decided. Pet. App. 8a (citing United States 
v. McKinney, 79 F.3d 105, 109 (8th Cir. 1996)). In dismissing Hohn’s Section 2255 motion, 
the district court neithe explored the applicability of doctrines (such as cause and prejudice) 
under which a “waiver” (or procedural default) has traditionally been excused, nor examined 
the merits of Hohn’s Bailey claim. 

4. Hohn then applied to the Eighth Circuit for a certificate of appealability. Under 
Section 2253(c) of the recently enacted the AEDPA, federal and state prisoners who have 
received an adverse ruling on a Section 2254 or 2255 motion in district court must obtain a 


“certificate of appealability” as a precondition to appeal. 28 U.S.C. § 2253(c). A certificate 


6 
may issue only upon a “substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right.” Jd. at § 
2253(c)(2). 

Over the vigorous dissent of Judge McMillian, the panel denied Hohn's request for a 
certificate of appealability. The majority explained that the AEDPA’s requirement of a 
“substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right” (ibid.) had not been satisfied 
because, in its view, “petitioner is not making a constitutional claim,” but rather is “making a 
claim [only] to a federal statutory right.” Pet. App. la-2a. In support of that proposition, the 
majority cited the fact that this Court in Bailey “did no more than interpret a statute.” Jd. at 
2a. The majority rejected the argument that the Constitution forbids the government from 
incarcerating an individual for conduct that is not a crime. It reasoned: “The Constitution does 
not guarantee that judges will always be right.” Jbid. 

In dissent, Judge McMillian argued that Hohn’s claim for relief from incarceration is 
clearly rooted in the Fifth Amendment’s Due Process Clause. Jd. at Sa-7a. He explained that 
the Due Process Clause forbids continued incarceration of a person based on a conviction for 
ceaduct that this Court has determined is not criminal. Jd. at Sa. The panel majority, however, 
found unpersuasive the series of Supreme Court and appellate cases, cited by Judge McMillian, 
in which post-conviction relief routinely has been granted on the basis of subsequent 
interpretations narrowing the reach of substantive criminal law. Id. at Sa-6a.’ 

Hohn petitioned for rehearing, arguing that his claim was premised on a denial of his due 


process right not to be incarcerated for conduct that this Court has decided does not constitute 


° One month later, the Eighth Circuit decided United States v. Apker, 101 F.3d 75 (1996), addressing the 
same issue. Judge Henley wrote a concurrence to criticize the Hohn decision, which he acknowledged was 


— and to recommend that the Eighth Circuit consider taking Apker or Hohn for en banc review. Id. 
at 76.

a crime. See Pet. for Reh’g. at 7-9. He also challenged (id. at 13-15) the draconian “waiver” 
rule the district court had applied to bar his claim, pointing out that the decision on which the 
doctrine is based, McKinney, 79 F.3d at 109, has only tenuous support even in the Eighth 
Circuit: Chief Judge Arnold and Judges Hansen, Loken, McMillian, and Murphy all voted to

suggestion for rehearing en banc on February 10, 1997. Pet. App. 21a. Judges Beam, Hansen, 
Loken, and McMillian all dissented from the denial of en banc rehearing. 
REASONS FOR GRANTING THE PETITION 

I, THE QUESTION WHETHER A CERTIFICATE OF APPEALABILITY SHOULD 

ISSUE UPON A SECTION 2255 PETITIONER’S SUBSTANTIAL SHOWING 

THAT HE IS INCARCERATED FOR CONDUCT THIS COURT HAS HELD IS 

NOT A CRIME IS IMPORTANT AND RECURRING 

A. The Proper Interpretation of Section 2253(c)’s Appealability Requirement of 

a “Substantial Showing of the Denial of a Constitutional Right” Is a Question 
of Great Importance and Will Affect Countless Cases 

Section 2253(c)(2) of the AEDPA is the first limitation Congress has imposed on the 
availability of appellate review of federal prisoners’ collateral challenges to their convictions 
under Section 2255 since that statute was adopted almost fifty years ago. The proper scope of 
this new limitation on appellate review is an important and recurring issue of federal law that 
will significantly affect the proper administration of justice in the federal courts. 

It cannot seriously be disputed that the federal courts will frequently be required to 
interpret and apply Section 2253(c)(2). As this Court is well aware, innumerable Section 2255 
petitions are filed every year. In every Section 2255 case in which relief is denied, the 


petitioner will have an incentive to take an appeal; and, under the AEDPA, no appeal will lie 


unless a certificate of appealability is issued. The question presented in this case — whether 


Section 2253(c)’s reference to “constitutional rights” restricts the type of cases that may be 
appealed, and in particular forecloses an appeal such as petitioner's — therefore can be expected 
to arise with great regularity in the federal courts. 

This Court often renders substantive law decisions narrowing the perceived reach of 
criminal statutes.’ Each such decision calls into question the validity of hundreds of convictions 
premised on the lower courts’ erroneously broad constructions.* Section 2255 motions are the 
primary avenue of relief for such wrongfully incarcerated persons. Literally hundreds of Section 
2255 challenges have been filed as a consequence of this Court's decision in Bailey alone. Thus, 
the question whether Section 2253(c) forecloses appeal as an avenue of relief for persons who 
are incarcerated for engaging in lawful conduct, as the court below held, will arise often. The 
same question of appealability presented here also will arise in al/ unsuccessful collateral 
challenges premised on statutory grounds. 

In addition to arising with great frequency, the proper meaning of Section 2253(c) is an 
issue of great importance. Limitation of appellate review in large numbers of federal post- 
conviction cases will substantially restrict its salutary and unifying effect, leading to intracircuit 


differences in how district courts resolve issues in Section 2255 cases. By the same token, a 


* See, e.g., Bailey, 116 S. Ct. at 508; Ratzlaf v. United States, 510 U.S. 135 (1994) (requiring, for a 
conviction under 31 U.S.C. §§ 5322(a) and 5324(3), that “the jury had to find [defendant] knew the [cash 
transactions] structuring in which he engaged was unlawful” — an element of the crime several courts of 
appeals had not previously required); McNally v. United States, 483 U.S. 350 (1987) (rejecting the widely 
accepted interpretation in the courts of appeals of the mail-fraud statute, 18 U.S.C. §§ 1341, 1343, as 
prohibiting schemes to deprive citizens of their intangible right to the honest and loyal services of a public 
official). 


* See, e.g., Criminal Use of Guns: Hearing Before the Senate Comm. on the Judiciary, \04th Cong., 2d 
Sess. (May 8, 1997), 1997 WL 10571065 (Statement of T. Hungar) (“As a result [of Bailey], countless 
convictions under Section 924(c) have been reversed or vacated * * *.”) (citing cases); DOJ Moves to Counter 
Impact of Ratzlaf, 4 No. 2 DOJ ALERT 10 (Feb. 7, 1994) (“The ruling [of the Supreme Court in Ratzlaf] 
undermined an estimated 200 past corruption convictions based on mail fraud * * *.”). 


) 

prohibition on appellate review will decrease the opportunies for error correction. For federal 
prisoners convicted of conduct this Court later determines is not a crime, the district court will 
be the first, last, and only forum for Section 2255 relief from incarceration.’ If the district court 
fails to grant relief — as did the district court below, on a misguided “waiver” theory (see in/ra, 
Section II) — an individual convicted only of lawful conduct may languish in prison without 
recourse. The court of appeals’ interpretation of Section 2253(c) similarly would preclude any 
appeal from the denial of “statutory” claims (however meritorious) that a prisoner's sentence 
exceeds the maximum permitted by law, or that the convicting court lacked personal or subject 
matter jurisdiction, notwithstanding that Section 2255 expressly authorizes (see 28 U.S.C. § 
2255), and courts historically have granted (see Ex Parte Lange, 85 U.S. (18 Wall.) 163, 178 
(1874); Ex Parte Watkins, 28 U.S. (3 Pet.) 193, 202 (1830)), habeas relief for such claims. 
Thus, the Eighth Circuit's interpretation of Section 2253(c) will, unless corrected, inflict on 
many federal prisoners a profound injustice. 

B. The Eighth Circuit's Analysis is Incorrect 

Section 2253(c)(2) requires that a Section 2255 petitioner make “a substantial showing 
of the denial of a constitutional right” as a prerequisite to appeal. The court below interpreted 
that requirement to bar appeal ip «ses involving an alleged violation of a “federal statutory 
right” — a category it deemed to include a prisoner's claim that he is incarcerated based on a 


conviction for conduct that is not criminal. The decision is patently incorrect for two reasons. 


* Although a federal prisoner may seek a writ of habeas corpus in the district of confinement pursuant to 
18 U.S.C. § 2241, such relief is available only when Section 2255 is “inadequate or ineffective to test the 
legality of his detention” (28 U.S.C. § 2255) — a standard that is not often met, even when the Section 2255 
court has incorrectly refused to grant relief. See LARRY W. YACKLE, POSTCONVICTION REMEDIES § 30, at 
154 (1981). 


10 


First, it is difficult to conceive of a more egregious denial of the constitutional right to due 
process than incarceration for conduct that this Court hes conclusively decided is not a crime. 
Hohn’s claim therefore plainly falls within the scope of Section 2253(c). Second, even if the 
“statutory” label the court beiow attached to Hohn's claim were accurate, Section 2253(c) cannot 
properly be interpreted to bar his appeal. A reading of Section 2253(c) in comtex* with other 
relevant provisions of the AEDPA makes apparent that Congress used the term “constitutional 
right” interchangeably with the term “federal right,” and did not intend Section 2253(c) to sever 
the appeal rights of federal prisoners on their statutory claims. 

1. The court below decided not to issue a certificate of appealability solely becuase it 
deemed Hohn’'s claim — that he is incarcerated for conduct this Court has determined to be 
lawful — to be “a claim to a federal statutory [rather than constitutional] right.” Pet. App. 2a. 
That was plainly wrong. As Judge Posner has correctedly explained, “If you argue that Statute 
X does not make your act a crime, you are making a statutory argument, but in support of a due 
process claim.” Johnson v. United States, 805 F.2d 1284, 1288 (7th Cir. 1987) (Posner, J.) 
(emphasis added). Hohn need not “claim” that the “using” provision of Section 924(c) does not 
prohibit the conduct of which he was convicted — this Court conclusively decided that point in 
Bailey. Instead, Hohn claims that because the Court has decided that the conduct of which he 
was convicted is not criminal, his continuing incarceration is unlawful. That claim is 
constitutional because “punishing a person for engaging in conduct that the polity has not made 
criminal” violates the constitutional guarantee against a deprivation of liberty without due 


process of law. Ibid. 


1 

a. Bailey applies retroactively, making the conduct of which Hohn was convicted lawful. 
This Court has held that decisions like Bailey, which narrow the construction lower courts have 
given to substantive criminal statutes (unlike most decisions creating new procedural rules, see, 
e.g., Teague v. Lane, 489 U.S. 288 (1989)), are retroactive. See Davis v. United States, 417 
U.S. 333, 346-347 (1974).° “There can be no room for doubt” that “conviction and punishment 
*** for an act” the Court has subsequently determined “the law does not make criminal * * 
* ‘inherently results in a complete miscarriage of justice’” and requires retroactive application 
of the clarifying judicial decision. Jbid. (citation omitted). See also United States v. Johnson, 
457 U.S. 537, 550 (1982) (“[F]ull retroactivity [i]s a necessary adjunct to a ruling that a trial 
court lacked authority to convict or punish a criminal defendant in the first place.”).’ Not 
surprisingly, in the year and a half since this Court issued its decision in Bailey, the lower courts 
uniformly have concluded that the decision must be applied retroactively to persons convicted 


prior to the Court's pronouncement. See, e.g., Bousley v. Brooks, 97 F.3d 284 (8th Cir. 1996), 


* The Court has applied a different rule to “new constitutional rules of criminal procedure,” generally 
barring their retroactive application on collateral review. Teague v. Lane, 489 U.S. 288, 310 (1989). Unlike 
substantive law changes, which reveal beyond doubt that a defendant has been wrongfully convicted of conduct 
that is not a crime, procedural law changes do not always implicate the accuracy of the conviction and 
sentence. Procedural law changes accordingly are retroactive only when they are so “fundamentally unfair™ 
as to “seriously diminish{]” “the likelihood of an accurate conviction.” /d. at 290. As the Court explained in 
Teague, few “components of basic due process” that are “so central to an accurate determination of innocence 
or guilt” have not yet emerged, and thus retroactive application of new procedural rules is rarely required. 
Id. at 313. In contrast, all substantive law determinations that the conduct of which a defendant was convicted 
is not criminal are “central to an accurate determination of innocence or guilt.” See United States v. McKie, 
73 F.3d 1149, 1152-1153 (D.C. Cir. 1996). 


’ This principle of retroactivity of decisions that narrow the perceived scope of a criminal prohibition is 
compelled by the common-sense proposition that “[a] judicial constructior of a statute is an authoritative 
statement of what the statute meant before as well as after the decision of the case giving rise to that 
construction.” Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc. , 511 U.S. 298, 312-313 (1994). 


12 


pet. for cert. filed (U.S. Mar. 18, 1997) (No. 96-8516); United States v. Cota-Loiaiza, 936 F. 
Supp. 751, 753-754 (D. Colo. 1996) (listing cases). 

b. Incarceration for lawful conduct is a core violation of the constitutional right to Due 
Process of law. The Due Process Clause commands that “[n]o person shall * * * be deprived 
of life, liberty, or property, without due process of law.” U.S. CONST. amend. V. This Court 
has often recognized that there are two components to that clause. “Substantive due process” 
prevents the government from engaging in conduct that “shocks the conscience,” Rochin v. 
California, 342 U.S. 165, 172 (1952), or interferes with rights “implicit in the concept of 
ordered liberty,” Palko v. Connecticut, 302 U.S. 319, 325 (1937). And “procedural due 
process” requires that “[w}hen government action depriving a person of life, liberty, or property 
survives substantive due process scrutiny, it must still be implemented in a fair manner.” United 
States v. Salerno, 481 U.S. 739, 746 (1987). 

The government's continuing incarceration of Hohn for conduct this Court has concluded 
is not a crime violates both components of due process. It is difficult to imagine a right more 
fundamental or “implicit in the concept of ordered liberty” than the right of a person to be free 
from punishment for conduct that is not proscribed by any law. See Bordenkircher v. Hayes, 434 
U.S. 357, 363 (1978) (“To punish a person because he has done what the law plainly allows him 
to do is a due process violation of the most basic sort.”); North Carolina v. Pearce, 395 U.S. 
711, 738 (1969) (Opinion of Black, J.); cf. BMW v. Gore, 116 S. Ct. 1589, 1598 & n. 19 
(1996) (holding that a state does not have the power to punish an individual for lawful conduct). 
This Court already has held that it is beyond dispute that “conviction and punishment” of a 
person for an act that the Court later concluded is not criminal “inherently results in a complete 


13 
miscarriage of justice.” Davis, 417 U.S. at 346-347. Several courts of appeals, in reliance on 
Davis, have recognized that continued incarceration for conduct this Court has declared not to 
be criminal is a violation of due process. See, e.g., id.; United States v. Loschiavo, 531 F.2d 
659, 666 (2d Cir. 1976); see also Apker, 101 F.3d at 76 (Henley, J., concurring). 

Hohn's continuing incarceration violates the most basic of his procedural due process 
rights: the government has failed both to prove each element of his purported crime beyond a 
reasonable doubt. See Jn re Winship, 397 U.S. 358, 363 (1970); Jackson v. Virginia, 443 U.S. 
307, 315 (1979). The government has never offered any evidence, let alone proven, that Hohn 
“actively employed” or “carried” a firearm. Pet. App. 4a-Sa (McMillian, J., dissenting). 

2. Even if the lower court's dubious conclusion (Pet. App. 2a) that Hohn is claiming a 
denial of his “statutory” (rather than constitutional) rights were correct, the court nevertheless 
erred in holding that Congress had abolished all appeals of such “statutory” claims. Although 
Section 2253(c)(2) authorizes a certificate of appealability to be issued only upon a “substantial 
showing of the denial of a constitutional right,” the statute must be read as a whole and in light 
of its legislative history. See Robinson v. Shell Oil Co., 117 S. Ct. 843 (1997). Such a reading 
clarifies that this term encompasses substantial claims of denial of statutory rights. The lower 
court's holding to the contrary would radically truncate the availability of appellate review of 
Section 2255 petitions, relegating many federal prisoners to one solitary opportunity in the 
district court to have even the most meritorious statutory claims heard — a drastic change in the 
law that no evidence suggests Congress intended. 

Congress used the terms “constitutional right” and “federal right” interchangeably in the 


AEDPA and appears to have regarded them as synonymous. Section 2253(c)(2) authorizes 


14 


appeal upon a “substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right.” (emphasis added). 
In another section of the amended statute, however, Congress referred to precisely the same 
authorization to appeal as requiring a “substantial showing of the denial of a Federal right.” 
28 U.S.C. § 2262(b)(3) (emphasis added).* Further, Sections 2254 and 2255 continue to 
authorize a writ of habeas corpus to be granted if a person is held “in violation of the 
Constitution or laws of the United States,” thus expressly authorizing relief from incarceration 
that violates only statutory rights. 28 U.S.C. §§ 2254, 2255 (emphasis added). 

The legislative history and an examination of the purposes of the statutory habeas scheme 
resolve the ambiguity created by the statute's inconsistent terms. Cf Robinson, 117 S. Ct. at 
848. Congress modeled the “certificate of appealability” requirement, which applies equally to 
state and federal prisoners, on the former requirement that state prisoners obtain a “certificate 
of probable cause” indicating that a prisoner's claims were sufficiently substantial to warrant 
apellate review. See H.R. REP. No. 23, 104th Cong., Ist Sess. 9 (1995) (“The bill thus enacts 
the standard of Barefoot v. Estelle, 463 U.S. 880 (1983)"). In Barefoot, 463 U.S. 881, 893 
n.4, this Court held that a state prisoner meets the prerequisites for a certificate of probable 
cause by making a “substantial showing of the denial of a federal right.” Id. at 881 (emphasis 
added). Several courts of appeals have correctly concluded that the AEDPA was meant to 
codify the Barefoot “federal right” standard for entitlement to an appeal. See, ¢.g., Santana v. 
United States, 98 F.3d 752, 757 (3d Cir. 1996), pet. for cert. filed (U.S. Mar. 19, 1997) (No. 


96-1443). 


* That section provides that a stay of execution shall be issued in a capital case upon application by the 
prisoner to any court for habeas relief; but mandates that the stay “shall expire if * * * a State prisoner * * 
* fails to make a substantial showing of the denial of a Federal right or is denied relief in the district court 
or at any subsequent stage of review.” 28 U.S.C. § 2262(b)(3) (emphasis added). 


15 


The Barefoot “federal right” standard did not preclude appeal of a claim based on a 
federal statutory right, as the court below contends the certificate of appealability standard 
should be interpreted to do. Instead, it merely ensured that, regardless from which source of 
law (Constitution, federal statute, treaty) a habeas claim derived, it would proceed to appeal only 
if it presented a question of substance. See Barefoot, 463 U.S. at 893 n.4, 894 (holding that a 
claim is appealable if it is “‘debatable among jurists of reason’” or is not “squarely foreclosed 
by statute, rule or authoritative court decision”) (emphasis added) (citation omitted). “Although 
virtually all habeas corpus petitions” by state prisoners — and hence appeals from their denial 
— “raise federal constitutional claims” (POSTCONVICTION REMEDIES, supra, § 91, at 360 
(emphasis in original)), Section 2254 has always authorized state prisoners’ claims that their 
incarceration violates the “Constitution or laws or treaties of the United States.” 28 U.S.C. § 
2254(a) (emphasis added). This Court recently recognized that a state prisoner's claim of a 
serious violation of federal statutory law can warrant federal habeas relief under the same 
circumstances as would a federal prisoner's statutory claim. See Reed vy. Farley, 114 S. Ct. 
2291, 2299-2300 (1994). See also POSTCONVICTION REMEDIES, supra, § 91, at 360-362. 
Nothing in Barefoot suggested an intent to bar state prisoners from appealing such claims. 

Because the overwhelming majority of habeas petitions and appeals by state prisoners 
invoke constitutional rights (see id.), Congress appears to have assumed that the Barefoot 
requirement that petitioners make a substantial showing of denial of a “federal right” is 


synonymous with the requirement that they make such a showing of a “constitutional right.” 


16 


Particularly in the context of federal Section 2255 motions for relief (which often assert 
violations of statutory rights), however, that characterization is incorrect.° 

That Congress gave no thought to this difference in extending the certificate of probable 
cause requirement to federal prisoners is apparent. In its amendment of Federal Rule of 
Appellate Procedure 22 to provide procedures for the new certificate of appealability 
requirement, Congress referred exclusively to State prisoners’ petitions for relief. The amended 
Rule suggests that only State prisoners are required to obtain certificates of appealability and 
provides judicial procedures for addressing only Stare prisoners’ appeals. See Fed. R. App. P. 
22(b). 

The lower court's interpretation of Section 2253(c) as eliminating federal prisoners’ right 
to appeal from denial of even highly meritorious statutory claims would severely unde,mine the 
purpose of the statutory collateral challenge scheme. Section 2255 is, “‘[a]s a remedy, * * * 
intended to be as broad as [the common law remedy of] habeas corpus.’” United States v. 
Hayman, 342 U.S. 205, 217 (1952) (quoting Statement by Judicial Conference Committee on 
Habeas Corpus Procedures). We have found nothing in the legislative history of the AEDPA 
suggesting that Congress intended to change that by eliminating a critical appellate avenue of 
relief for all movants asserting statutory rights. Instead, it is plain that Congress intended the 
appealability requirement merely to eliminate frivolous appeals by federal prisoners, regardless 
whether they are based on statutory or constitutional rights — just as the certificate of probable 


cause requirement had eliminated frivolous appeals by state prisoners. See H.R. REP. No. 23, 


“[G]iven that federal criminal prosecutions involve far more federal non-constitutional law than do state 
prosecutions, in practice the inclusion of federal ‘laws’ is far more significant in the § 2255 setting.” 
RICHARD H. FALLON, DANIEL J. MELTZER, DAVID L. SHAPIRO, HART & WECHSLER’S THE FEDERAL COURTS 
AND THE FEDERAL SYSTEM 1465 (4th ed. 1996). 


17 
supra, at 9 (“Since federal prisoners, like state prisoners, generate a high volume of meritless 
applications for collateral relief, it is appropriate to require that appeals of habeas corpus 
petitions meet a threshold probable cause standard before such an appeal will be heard by an 
appellate panel.”). 

The Eighth Circuit's view that Section 2253(c) should be read otherwise, to make district 
court decisions final for even the most important federal statutory Section 2255 claims “would 
produce silly” — and shocking — “results.” LARRY W. YACKLE, Federal Evidentiary Hearings 
Under the New Habeas Corpus Statute, 6 B.U. PuB. INT. L.J. 135, 146-147 n.43 (Fall 1996). 
Persons who are wrongfully incarcerated absent any statutory authority, like Hohn, may remain 
in prison without appropriate relief. This type of claim has historically been given the utmost 
solicitude, and indeed has roots in the earliest decisions of this Court granting habeas corpus 
relief. See, e.g., Ex Parte Lange, 85 U.S. at 178 (habeas corpus is appropriate remedy where 
convicting court issued a punishment that exceeded its statutory authority). There is simply no 
reason why Congress would have singled out such “statutory” claims for a special burden under 
the AEDPA. See Apker, 101 F.3d at 76 (Henley, J., concurring) (“it is doubtful Congress 
intended in its 1996 habeas revisions to bar appeals of claims that a conviction violates federal 
law”). 


c. The Courts of Appeals Are Deeply Divided Over Whether Section 2253(c) 
Permits Appeal of Meritorious Federal Statutory Claims 


“There is a difference of opinion” among the federal courts of appeal on the question 
presented here: “whether § 2253(c), which provides that a certificate of appealability may issue 
‘only if the applicant has made a substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right,’ 


modifies preexisting law, which defined the required showing as, a ‘substantial showing of the 


denial of [a] federal right.” Hunter v. Bailey, 101 F.3d 1565, 1571 (11th Cir. 1996). “It is 
undecided” among the federal courts “whether Congress intended to change the standard, along 
with the name, for granting a certificate of appealability,” thereby foreclosing appeals by persons 
who claim to be held in violation of federal statutory rights. Santana, 98 F.3d at 757. 

The Ninth Circuit has concluded that the prerequisite showing of a “denial of a 
constitutional right” in Section 2253(c) is “more demanding” than the “federal right” standard 
this Court set forth in Barefoot. Williams v. Calderon, 83 F.3d 281, 286 (9th Cir. 1996). In 
contrast, the Second, Fifth, and Tenth Circuits have concluded that Congress intended to codify, 
and did not alter, the Barefoot “federal right” standard. See Drinkard v. Johnson, 97 F.3d 752, 
756 (Sth Cir. 1996), cert. denied, 117 S. Ct. 1114 (1997); Reyes v. Keane, 90 F.3d 676, 679- 
680 (2d Cir. 1996); Lennox v. Evans, 87 F.3d 431, 434 (10th Cir. 1996), cert. denied, 117 S. 
Ct. 746 (1997). Each of those circuits, however, appears to interpret that standard as 
foreclosing appeals of statutory claims. See Drinkard, 97 F.3d at 756; Reyes, 90 F.3d at 679- 
680; Lennox, 87 F.3d at 434. The Third Circuit has suggested an entirely different 
interpretation, directly at odds with the Eighth Circuit's decision in Hohn: that Congress’ use 
of the term “constitutional right” is attributable to “imperfect draftsmanship,” and that Section 
2253(c) may properly be read to permit appeals of statutory claims. Santana, 98 F.3d at 757. 

The Eighth Circuit's decision that the new “constitutional right” standard forecloses 
Hohn’s claim that he is incarcerated for “using” a firearm in a manner this Court held in Bailey 
does not violate Section 924(c), also directly conflicts with three decisions by the Tenth Circuit 
granting certificates of appealability under Section 2253(c) to hear virtually identical post-Bailey 


claims. In each case, the Tenth Circuit granted a certificate to decide an appeal of a federal 


19 

prisoner's claim that he was incarcerated for conduct this Court had determined in Bailey not 
to constitute “using” a firearm within the meaning of 18 U.S.C. § 924(c). See United States 
v. Gorecki, No. 96-4092, 1997 WL 74 (10th Cir. Apr. 1, 1997), pet. for cert. filed (Apr. 18, 
1997) (No. 96-8702); United States v. David, No. 96-1391, 1997 WL 26581 (10th Cir. Jan. 24, 
1997}; United States v. Deases, No. 96-3152, 1996 WL 557661 (10th Cir. Oct. 1, 1996), cert. 
deniea, 117 S. Ct. 746 (1997)). Like the Eighth Circuit, the Tenth Circuit interprets Section 
2253(c) as requiring “a colorable showing of [a] constitutional error.” Lennox, 87 F.3d at 434 
(emphasis added). The Tenth Circuit's decisions in Gorecki, David, and Deases make clear, 
however, that it rejects the Eighth Circuit's view that a claim of incarceration for “using” a 
firearm in a manner this Court held in Bailey to be lawful is not a substantial claim of 
“constitutional” error. '° 

The Eighth Circuit's crabbed view of the protections of the Due Process Clause also 
conflicts with decisions in the Seventh and Second Circuits, recognizing (in reliance on this 
Court's decision in Davis v. United States, supra) that 2 prisoner's claim that he is incarcerated 
for lawful conduct asserts a denial of the constitutional right to due process. See Johnson, 805 
F.2d at 1288; Loschiavo, 531 F.2d at 666; see also Apker, 101 F.3d at 76 (Henley, J., 


concurring). "' 


‘° It is evident from the fact that the Tenth Circuit has repeatedly interpreted Section 2253(c) to authorize 
certificates of appealability to hear such claims that this is the law of the Circuit, notwithstanding that the 
decisions are unpublished. This Court recently granted certiorari to resolve a conflict presented in part by 
an unpublished decision of a court of appeals denying a certificate of probable cause (the predecessor of the 
certificate of appealability) in a habeas challenge. See Lynce v. Mathis, 117 S. Ct. 891, 893 (1997). 


‘' Notably, the decision below has stimulated substantial controversy even within the Eighth Circuit itself 
on the question of the standards for appealability of habeas claims. Judges Beam, Loken, Hansen and 
McMillian all voted to grant Hohn’s petition for en banc review. And in Apker, a case presenting a post- 
Bailey challenge identical to Hohn’s, Judge Henley wrote separately to criticize the majority decision in Hohn.
