Il. THE EIGHTH CIRCUIT’S NOVEL WAIVER DOCTRINE CONFLICTS WITH 

THE DECISIONS OF OTHER CIRCUITS AND IS INCOMPATIBLE WITH THIS 

COURT’S PROCEDURAL DEFAULT CASES 

If the Court grants review to address the Eighth Circuit's construction of the AEDPA, 
it should also decide whether a Section 2255 movant's procedural default — failure to raise on 
direct appeal a legal challenge that at the time was squarely foreclosed by binding circuit 
precedent and had been uniformly rejected in other circuits — can be excused because of an 
intervening change in controlling precedent, which establishes that the underlying conduct does 
not constitute a crime. This Court's decisions establish that such procedurally defaulted claims 
may be raised in collateral review proceedings if the claimant establishes “cause and prejudice” 
or “actual innocence.” In denying Hohn’s Section 2255 motion, however, the district court 
made no mention of the cause-and-prejudice standard but instead applied a broad, categorical 
waiver rule announced by the Eighth Circuit in United States vy. McKinney, 79 F.3d 105, pet. 
for cert. filed (U.S. Oct. 11, 1996) (No. 96-6348). Pet. App. 8a-9a.'? Under the McKinney 
standard, a defendant can be deemed to have knowingly and intelligently “waived” a claim by 
failing to anticipate an unforeseeable change in binding circuit precedent. As we explain below, 
the Eighth Circuit's waiver rule is at odds with the decisions of other circuits and is patently 


inconsistent with the jurisprudence of this Court. Further review of this important issue of 


federal law is warranted.’ 


‘2 The magistrate judge who initially reviewed Hohn’s Section 2255 motion noted that Hohn had not raised 
a Bailey claim on direct appeal but found that the cause-and-prejudice standard for procedurally defaulted 
claims was satisfied. Pet. App. |la-12a. “Because Bailey was not decided at the time of the direct appeal,” 
the magistrate judge explained, “there is adequate cause for defendant's failure to raise it”; furthermore, Hohn 
had established prejudice because his case appeared to fall “within the purview of Bailey.” Jd. at 12a. 


'’ The court of appeals in this case did not reach the question of procedural default because it concluded that 
it was without jurisdiction to hear Hohn’s appeal under the AEDPA. But because the district court denied 


21 
A. The Eighth Circuit’s Decision Conflicts With The Approach of Other Circuits 
McKinney involved a defendant who had been convicted under 18 U.S.C. § 924(c) for 

“using” a firearm. After McKinney's trial and the filing of his opening brief on direct appeal, 

this Court decided Bailey. On the basis of these events, a panel of the Eighth Circuit held (in 

two sentences and without citation) that McKinney's failure to raise the Bailey argument at trial 
or in his initial appeal brief amounted to a “waive[r]” of the argument. McKinney, 79 F.3d at 

109. Judge Heaney dissented, pointing out that “it would have been useless for McKinney to 

challenge the correctness of [the Eighth Circuit's] previous cases” before Bailey was decided. 

Id. at 109 (Heaney, J., dissenting). He added that “a waiver must be knowing and intentional” 

(ibid.) and that those requirements could not be satisfied when the “waived” argument was not 

available because this Court's decision had not yet been issued. The Eighth Circuit declined to 

rehear the case en banc, although five judges voted to do so. /d. at 105 n.*. In Bousley v. 

Brooks, 97 F.3d at 287-288, the Eighth Circuit applied the McKinney rule to a Section 2255 

petitioner raising a Bailey claim. 

The Eighth Circuit's waiver doctrine clearly conflicts with decisions of the Sixth, 
Seventh, and Tenth Circuits. Mizen v. United States, No. 96-3478, 1997 WL 117037, at **1 
(6th Cir. Mar. 13, 1997); Lee v. United States, ___ F.3d ___, No. 96-3323, 1997 WL 213972, 
*2-*3 (7th Cir. Apr. 30, 1997); United States v. Barnhardt, 93 F.3d 706, 708-709 (10th Cir. 
1996). The conflict is sharpest with Barnhardt, which held that a Section 2255 petitioner who 


failed to raise a Bailey argument (because he entered a guilty plea) was not precluded from 


Hohn’'s Section 2255 petition solely on the strength of the Eighth Circuit's waiver rule, and because Hohn 
presented the waiver argument to the court of appeals (see Pet. for Reh’g, at 5-7, 13-15), it is properly before 
this Court for decision. See United States v. Williams, 504 U.S. 36, 41 (1992) (Court may review a question 
if it was “pressed or passed upon” in the court below). 


22 
mounting a collateral attack on his Section 924(c) conviction. Barnhardt, 93 F.3d at 708-709. 
In Bousley, the Eighth Circuit, relying on the waiver doctrine announced in McKinney, expressly 
“declined to follow” Barnhardt’s holding. 97 F.3d at 288 n.4. 

The rule of McKinney is also at odds with post-Bailey decisions of the Sixth and Seventh 
Circuits in cases involving collateral challenges to Section 924(c) convictions. In Lee, the 
Seventh Circuit, like the Tenth, rejected the government's argument that a defendant's guilty 
plea “conclusively establishe[d] the elements of the offense and the material facts necessary to 
support the conviction, foreclosing any challenge to the conviction.” 1997 WL 213972, *2, 
(internal quotation marks omitted). The Seventh Circuit reasoned that, although the facts 
admitted in the guilty plea “at that time were thought to constitute a crime under § 924(c),” 
Bailey later held that those facts (amounting to simple possession of a gun) “do[] not a § 924(c) 
crime make.” Ibid. The concept of waiver, the Seventh Circuit explained, was simply 
inapposite under these circumstances: 

[H]ow can Lee be held to have waived his right to challenge whether those facts 

constituted a crime when at the time they did, and after Bailey they may not? He could 

not possibly be viewed as having voluntarily waived what turns out to be a Bailey 
challenge when Bailey did not exist at the time he pled guilty. 
Ibid. (emphasis added). The court of appeals also noted that the same reasoning applies whether 
the facts (which are later determined not to constitute a crime) were “found by a jury or 
admitted by a defendant.” Jd. at *3. 

Similarly, the Sixth Circuit in Mizen, 1997 WL 117037, permitted a Section 2255 petition 
to raise a Bailey challenge that had been procedurally defaulted. Applying well-established 
principles, the Sixth Circuit explained that the defendant's failure to raise the Bailey issue on 


direct appeal required him to “demonstrate cause and actual prejudice to excuse his failure to 


23 
do so.” 1997 WL 117037, at *1. The court then went on to hold that cause was established 
because “Mizen's § 924(c) [Bailey] claim relies on a significant, intervening change in the law.” 
Ibid. The court also found prejudice because the evidence appeared insufficient to satisfy the 
Bailey standard. Ibid. 

In cases raising similar claims following this Court's decision in McNally, the courts of 
appeals recognized that cause existed for failing to raise an issue that was not viable before an 
intervening decision of this Court changed the interpretation of the underlying statute. See, e.g., 
Callanan v. United States, 88\ F.2d 229, 231 (6th Cir. 1989) (“the fact that the point appeared 
to be settled constituted cause for not raising it”); United States v. Shelton, 848 F.2d 1485, 1490 
(10th Cir. 1988) (McNally “presents the quintessential circumstance in which a defaulted claim 
had so little basis in existing law that a habeas petitioner had cause for failing to raise it”). 
Although other circuits have not directly decided the question in post-Bailey claims, no court of 
appeals has shown any predisposition to agree with the Eighth Circuit.'* As we next explain, 
the Eighth Circuit's waiver rule is also in severe tension with established law concerning the 
cause-and-prejudice standard and the exception. 

B. The Eighth Circuit’s Waiver Rule Creates An End Run Around The 


Established Framework For Deciding Whether Collateral Relief Is Available 
for Procedurally Defaulted Claims 


' The Third, Fourth, and Ninth Circuits have all heard appeals (on other grounds) from district court orders 
that vacated convictions or guilty pleas after Bailey; not one of those courts suggested that waiver or 
procedural default posed any barrier to collateral relief based on Bailey. See, ¢.g., United States v. Davis, 
___ F.3d ___, No. 96-1721, 1997 WL 195397, *1 (3d Cir. Apr. 23, 1997); United States v. Hillary, 106 
F.3d 1170, 1170-1171 (4th Cir. 1997); United States v. Handa, 110 F.3d 42, 43 (9th Cir. 1997). Two other 
circuits, the First and Fifth, have expressly reserved the question whether Bailey can be invoked in collateral 
challenges, but neither court suggested any inclination to agree with the Eighth Circuit's lonely view. See 
United States v. Joseph, \O9 F.3d 34, 36 (ist Cir. 1997); United States v. Rocha, \09 F.3d 225, 229 n.5 (Sth 
Cir. 1997); see also United States v. Andrade, 83 F.3d 729, 730 n.1 (Sth Cir. 1996). 


24 

The Eighth Circuit's waiver rule is also incompatible with this Court's cases dealing with 
the “cause-and-prejudice” test for excusing procedural defaults. Under established principles, 
a prisoner who seeks collateral relief on a ground not raised at trial or on direct appeal from 
conviction is said to have suffered a “procedural default.” See, e.g., United States v. Frady, 
456 U.S. 152, 168 (1982). But procedural default does not necessarily preclude collateral relief 
on the defaulted theory. This Court has established two exceptions that allow collateral review 
in spite of a procedural default: where the prisoner (1) can “demonstrate cause for the default 
and actual prejudice as a result” of the underlying error, or (2) can show “that failure to 
consider the claims will result in a fundamental miscarriage of justice.” Coleman v. Thompson, 
501 U.S. 722, 750 (1991); see also Frady, 456 U.S. at 167-168. Although Hohn satisfies both 
exceptions and thus is entitled to relief on his procedurally defaulted Bailey claim, the Eighth 
Circuit’s waiver rule denies him the benefit of these exceptions. 

1. Cause and Prejudice. To demonstrate “cause, a claimaint must show “the existence 
of * * * some objective factor external to the defense [which] impeded counsel{]” and effectively 
prevented the defendant from raising the argument during trial. Murray v. Carrier, 477 U.S. 
478, 488 (1986). The fact that an argument is precluded by binding precedent (and is 
sufficiently novel that it appears frivolous) is just such an objective, external factor that excuses 


counsel's failure to raise the issue.’ 


'* As we show below (see Section III), Hohn was obviously prejudiced by the trial court’s error because he 
was convicted under § 924(c) for conduct that does not constitute a crime after Bailey. See Callanan, 881 
F.2d at 231 (“[t}he prejudice requirement boils down to a question of whether the Callanans could properly 
have been convicted” under the proper standard); Dalton v. United States, 862 F.2d 1307, 1310 (8th Cir. 
1988) (prejudice requires showing that prisoner “was convicted for conduct that is not proscribed by the 
[relevant] statute”). 


25 
This Court has specifically held that where a claim “is so novel that its legal basis is not 
reasonably available to counsel, a defendant has cause for his failure to raise the claim.” Reed 
v. Ross, 468 U.S. 1, 16 (1984). In Reed, the Court categorized the kinds of novel claims that 
might establish cause, identifying two categories that “[b]y definition” demonstrate cause 
because there was no reasonable basis for raising the claim earlier: 
First, a decision of this Court may explicitly overrule one of [its own] precedents. 


Second, a decision may overturn a longstanding and widespread practice to which this 
Court has not spoken, but which a near-unanimous body of lower court authority has 


expressly approved. 

Id. at 17 (internal citations and quotation marks omitted). The Bailey argument, on which Hohn 
here relies, falls squarely within the second category. In 1992, when Hohn was tried and 
convicted under Section 924(c), the Eighth Circuit (like every other circuit) had construed the 
term “use” quite broadly (and erroneously, as it turned out). Hohn’'s trial counsel cannot be 
faulted for lacking the prescience to believe that a decision of this Court some three years after 
his conviction would fundamentally alter the landscape of Section 924(c)(1). 

The Sixth Circuit, like the magistrate judge in this case (Pet. App. 12a), has recognized 
that Bailey's substantial change in the substantive interpretation of Section 924(c)(1) constitutes 
cause. Mizen, 1997 WL 117037, at **1 (“Mizen’s § 924(c) [Bailey] claim relies on a 
significamt, intervening change in the law”). Moreover, the courts of appeals recognized that 
this Court's decision in McNally — a case that, like Bailey, narrowed the scope of a criminal 


statute — provided cause for failing to raise the identical issue on direct appeal. See, ¢.g.. 


26 


Callanan, 881 F.2d at 231; Shelton, 848 F.2d at 1490."° Where cause and prejudice exist, as 
they do here, procedural default is excused. 

2. Miscarriage of Justice. This Court has permitted the assertion of procedurally 
defaulted claims in order to avoid a “miscarriage of justice” or where a claim asserts “actual 
innocence.” Sawyer v. Whitley, 505 U.S. 333, 339 (1992)."" In a collateral review case 
involving a similar claim, this Court has noted that “conviction and punishment [] for an act that 
the law does not make criminal * * * inherently results in a complete miscarriage of justice 
*** ” Davis v. United States, 417 U.S. 333, 346 (1974) (internal quotation marks omitted).'* 
The courts of appeals have likewise recognized that a claim such as Hohn’s -- pointing to a later 
decision of this Court that demonstrates the prisoner has been convicted for conduct that is not 
criminal -- shows that there has been a miscarriage of justice and obviates any need to show 
cause for the default. See, e.g., Shelton, 848 F.2d at 1490 n.4 (recognizing “a compelling 
argument [that a McNally claim is available on collateral review] without regard for whether the 
petitioner had cause for any default”); Callanan, 881 F.2d at 231 (“There is a strong argument 


* * * that cause need not be shown at all in these unusual circumstances.”) (citing Shelton); see 


‘© In Dalton, the Eighth Circuit correctly applied the cause-and-prejudice test in holding that a defendant 
challenging his conviction for mail fraud after McNally was not precluded from obtaining relief because of 
procedural default. See Dalton, 862 F.2d at 1310 (stating that “a petitioner for post-conviction relief has cause 
* * * for failing to raise a claim ‘for which there was no reasonable basis in existing law’”) (quoting Reed, 
468 U.S. at 15). The Court in McKinney did not cite or distinguish Dalton. In this case, the Eighth Circuit 
was asked to grant rehearing en banc in part to resolve the tension between Dalton and McKinney, but 
declined to do so. 


'’ Although Sawyer addressed the “miscarriage of justice” exception in the context of successive claims, the 
Court noted that the same standard also applies to allow consideration of the merits of procedurally defaulted 
claims. /bid. (citing Murray, 477 U.S. at 496). 


'* Davis itself did not involve a procedural default, but the Court there expressed unshakeable certainty 
(“[t}here can be no room for doubt,” ibid.) that conviction for conduct that is not a crime constitutes a 
miscarriage of justice. 


27 

also United States v. McKie, 73 F.3d 1149, 1154 (D.C. Cir. 1996) (remanding to determine 
whether § 2254 movant was actually innocent under new interpretation of criminal statute); 
United States v. Sood, 969 F.2d 774, 776 (9th Cir. 1992) (“The doors of collateral review are 
open for petitioners who can show, through an intervening change in the law, that they could 
not have committed the substantive offense with which they were charged,” reviewing cases 
applying McNally). Because Hohn is actually innocent of the crime defined by Section 924(c), 
the actual innocence exception should excuse his procedural default. See also Section II, 
infra.** 

The Eighth Circuit's waiver doctrine eviscerates these well-established exceptions to 
procedural default and denies Hohn the opportunity for collateral review that he is properly 


entitled to. 


Cc. The Issue Is Important And The Eighth Circuit's Analysis Is Seriously 
Flawed 


The Eighth Circuit's novel waiver rule raises an important issue of federal law that 
warrants this Court's attention. At the same time, the Eighth Circuit's rule is erroneous and in 
need of correction by this Court. For those reasons as well, review should be granted. 

The Eighth Circuit's waiver doctrine is important for at least four reasons. First, it arises 
with great regularity. Second, it threatens to undermine the scheme that this Court has 


established for evaluating procedural defaults. Third, the Eighth Circuit's waiver rule leads to 


We note, moreover, that many cases decided in the wake of McNally and Bailey have recognized, without 
engaging in a specific analysis of cause or actual innocence, that an intervening change in the interpretation 
of the underlying criminal statute is a proper ground for collateral relief. See, ¢.¢., Lee, 1997 WL 213972, 
at *2-*3; Barnhardt, 93 F.3d at 709; Ingber v. Enzor, 841 F.2d 450, 454 (2d Cir. 1988) (“retroactive 
application [of McNally) is necessary to avoid an unfair result”); Magnuson v. United States, 861 F.2d 166, 
167 (7th Cir. 1988). 


28 


untenable results, permitting federal prisoners to languish in federal prisons for conduct that this 
Court has held is not criminal. And fourth, the Eighth Circuit's rule would effectively require 
defendants to raise claims that are admittedly frivolous, merely to preserve arguments for 
collateral review in the hope that the substantive law might later be interpreted more narrowly. 
Besides the sheer impossibility of that task, such a requirement would multiply the number of 
frivolous issues in criminal cases, needlessly burdening the limited resources of federal courts 
and serving no useful purpose. This Court has (without dissent) rejected a requirement that a 
defendant “make a long and virtually useless laundry list of objections to rulings that were 
plainly supported by existing precedent.” Johnson v. United States, No. 96-203, slip op. 6-7 
(U.S. May 12, 1997). Indeed, such a requirement would directly conflict with counsel's 
obligation not to advance frivolous arguments before the court. See, e.g., United States v. 
Gomez, 24 F.3d 924, 926 (7th Cir. 1994). 

The Eighth Circuit's waiver rule is also egregiously incorrect. As Judge Heaney 
correctly noted, “a waiver must be knowing and intentional.” McKinney, 79 F.3d at 109 
(Heaney, J., dissenting). A defendant who does not know (and cannot reasonably predict) that 
this Court will issue a ground-breaking decision three years after his conviction cannot be said 
to have waived an argument that was foreclosed before that decision. The Seventh Circuit has 
made much the same point, recognizing that a defendant “could not possibly be viewed as 
having voluntarily waived” an argument that “did not exist at the time he pled guilty.” Lee, 
1997 WL 213972, at *2 (emphasis added). Similarly, the Ninth Circuit pointed out that a pre- 
Bailey guilty plea was not “knowing and voluntary” in the light of Bailey because “the facts [the 
defendant] admitted committing do not now constitute a crime.” United States v. McJoy, No. 


29 

95-15565, 1996 WL 528422, **1 (9th Cir. Sept. 13, 1996); see also id. at **2 (noting 

“fundamental unfairness of allowing the government to keep the benefit of a bargain McJoy did 

not contemplate”). 

Il. THE PROPER DEFINITION OF THE CRIME OF “CARR[YING] A FIREARM” 
UNDER 18 U.S.C. § 924(C) IS AN IMPORTANT AND RECURRING ISSUE OF 
FEDERAL LAW THAT WOULD BENEFIT FROM CLARIFICATION BY THIS 
COURT 
If this Court concludes that review should be granted on the first two questions presented, 

then it should also grant review to address the government's contention below (replayed in 

countless post-Bailey cases across the country) that petitioner's conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 

924(c) can be sustained because he “carrie[d] a firearm.” As we explain below, the proper 

scope of “carrying” liability under Section 924(c) is a critically important question of federal law 

as to which the courts of appeals are in disarray. Although the lower courts did not reach the 

“carry” issue, it was properly raised below and is therefore presented for decision. See note 

12, supra. Moreover, resolution of the issue is not dependent upon any factual findings but 

rather on an analysis of the text, structure, and legislative history of the statute as well as on 

very limited materials from the record (such as the jury instructions and limited portions of the 


trial testimony).*” As we explain below, the government completely failed to prove that 


petitioner “carried” a firearm, much less that he carried it “during” the predicate offense. And, 


* The government acknowledged in the district court that petitioner's challenge to his Section 924(c) 
conviction raises “an issue of law” that “can be resolved on the basis of the record and files before the 
Court.” Pet. App. 1Sa. 


30 

at a minimum, petitioner is entitled to a new trial because the jury instr ctions plainly misstated 
the proper scope of “carrying” liability.” 

A. The Scope of “Carrying” Liability Is An Important And Recurring Issue 

Section 924(c) is one of the most frequently deployed criminal provisions in the federal 
prosecutor's arsenal. According to data collected by the U.S. Sentencing Commission, in the 
fiscal year ending September 30, 1995 alone, 1,973 defendants were sentenced for violations of 
Section 924(c)(1). MURRAY, Mandatory 5-year Sentence for Gun Use Curbed By Court, Wash. 
Times. Dec. 7, 1995, at A6. Moreover, since this Court's decision in Bailey, which worked 
a substantial narrowing of the scope of “use” liability under Section 924(c), the federal courts 
increasingly have been called upon to determine the nature of alternative liability under Section 
924(c) for “carr[ying] a firearm.” See United States vy. Cooke, No. 96-1852, 1997 U.S. App. 
LEXIS 6645, at “16 n.7 (7th Cir. April 9, 1997) (“[NJow that the Bailey decision has 
dramatically constricted the range of conduct constituting ‘use’ of a firearm, delineation of the 
parameters of the term ‘carries’ takes on heightened significance”). In the year and a half since 
Bailey was decided, there have been literally hundreds of reported decisions by the district courts 
and courts of appeals discussing this subject. Not surprisingly, the Solicitor General has 
acknowledged that Section 924(c) “is an important and widely used statute.” No. 95-1605 


Petition for Certiorari, United States v. Gonzales, at 7. The scope of criminal liability under 


2! Because one of the two alternative theories of Section 924(c) liability has (as the government concedes) 
been rendered legally invalid by Bailey, Hohn is entitled to have the verdict set aside. As the Eighth Circuit 
has explained, “a verdict based on jury instructions that provided alternative bases for a finding, one of which 
is legally inadequate, is presumed to have rested on the insufficient basis.” United States v. Ryan, 41 F.3d 
361, 365 (1994) (citing Griffin v. United States, 502 U.S. 46 (1991)). The jury in this case was not asked 
to specify on the verdict form whether Hohn “used” or “carried” a firearm. It is therefore impossible to 
determine from the record on which ground the jury based the conviction (Stromberg v. California, 283 U.S. 
359, 368 (1931)), and the verdict must be set aside if either ground is improper. 


31 


Section 924(c) is especially important, moreover, because of the severe penalties that Congress 
has attached to it. 


B. The Courts Of Appeal Are In Disarray Over What It Means To “Carry” A 
Firearm And Would Benefit From Guidance From This Court 


As the Seventh Circuit recently noted, “[t)he circuits are split” in cases involving 
firearms found in vehicles over what it means to “carry” a firearm under Section 924(c). 
Cooke, 1997 U.S. App. LEXIS 6645, at *25. There is a sharp conflict over “whether a weapon 
that is aboard * * * must be immediately accessible to the defendant in order to suppport a 
‘carrying’ conviction.” Ibid. Accord United States v. Cleveland, 106 F.3d 1056, 1065, 1066- 
68 (Ist Cir. 1997) (noting 4-3 circuit split on this issue), pet. for cert. filed, Apr. 30, 1997 (No. 
96-8837). The First, Fourth, and Tenth Circuits have all held that “a gun may be ‘carried’ 
in a vehicle for purposes of § 924(c)(1) without necessarily being immediately accessible to the 
defendant while it is being transported.” Cleveland, 106 F.3d at 1066; see United States v. 
Miller, 84 F.3d 1244, 1259 (10th Cir.), cert. denied, 117 S. Ct. 443 (1996); United States v. 
Mitchell. 104 F.3d 649, 653-54 (4th Cir. 1997). In contrast, the Second, Sixth and Ninth 
Circuits have held that a gun may be carried in a vehicle only if it is immediately accessible to 
the defendant. See United States v. Giraldo, 80 F.3d 667, 677-78 (2d Cir.), cert. denied, 117 
S. Ct. 135 (1996); United States v. Riasco-Suarez, 73 F.3d 616, 623 (6th Cir.), cert. denied, 
117 S. Ct. 136 (1996); United States v. Staples, 85 F.3d 461, 464 (9th Cir.), cert. denied, 117 
S. Ct. 318 (1996). The dissenting judges in the D.C. Circuit's en banc court in Bailey, whose 


interpretation of “use” was adopted by this Court, were of the view that “carry” does not extend 


2 See also No. 96-8837 Petition for Certiorari, Cleveland v. United States, at 7-17 (describing conflict); 
No. 96-1654 Petition for Certiorari, Muscarello v. United States, at 6-11 (filed April 18, 1997) (same). 


32 

to cases where a firearm that is not immediately accessible to the defendant is transported in a 
vehicle. See 36 F.3d 106, 125 (D.C. Cir. 1994) (Williams, joined by Silberman & Buckley, 
JJ., dissenting) (“The effect would be to have § 924(c) embrace virtually every instance where 
a drug trafficker transports a weapon; in view of Congress's provision of a separate penalty in 
an adjacent section for anyone who “transports” a weapon * * * , 18 U.S.C. § 924(b), that 
seems an improbable duplication.”), rev'd, 116 S. Ct. 501 (1995). At least one commentator 
has noted this “split among the circuit courts.” KESSELMAN, Firearm Possession: Excuse Me, 
Are You “Using” That Gun? The United States Supreme Court Examines 18 U.S.C. 924(c)(1) 
in Bailey v. United States, 30 CREIGHTON L. REV. 513, 540 (1997). Thus the courts of appeal 
are sharply divided on the question whether immediate accessibility is a necessary prerequisite 
to “carrying.” 

Although the circuits are hopelessly confused over the meaning of “carry” in vehicle 
cases, we are aware of no circuit decision since Bailey that has held that a defendant “carries 
a firearm” merely by having it nearby or accessible in a house, apartment or other stationary 
setting (i.e., not in a moving vehicle). Two circuits have expressly concluded that “carrying” 
does not cover such a scenario. Thus, the Seventh Circuit in Cooke recently held that immediate 
accessibility to a firearm does nor suffice to show “carrying” in a nonvehicle case (where the 
firearm is not transported). 1997 U.S. App. LEXIS 6645, at *22-*25 (7th Cir. April 9, 1997) 
(defendant's presence in passenger compartment of pickup truck at same time garment bag 
containing firearm was on truck’s bed did not constitute “carrying,” where “the truck never 
moved the weapon”); accord United States v. Jackson, 103 F.3d 561, 569 (7th Cir. 1996). 


Similarly, the Sixth Circuit vacated a Section 924(c) where the defendant had been apprehended 


33 


while sleeping in his bedroom (with four guns “strewn about the bedroom”). United States v. 
Moore, 76 F.2d 111, 113 (6th Cir. 1996). The Sixth Circuit explained that “[i]mmediate 
availability is * * * a necessary, but not sufficient, determinant” of liability for carrying, and 
noted that “if Congress had meant Section 924(c)(1) to implicate any individual who happens 
to be within arm's reach of a firearm, surely it would have selected a more accurate term than 
‘carry."” Ibid. (emphasis added).** Nevertheless, at least one district court has accepted this 
untenably broad definition of “carry,” see also United States v. Canady, 920 F. Supp. 402, 405- 
408 (W.D.N.Y. 1996), and the government can be counted on to press that theory in other 
cases. Under these circumstances, further guidance from this Court would be tremendously 
valuable to the lower courts. 


Cc. The Government Failed To Prove That Petitioner “Use[d] or Carrie[d] A 
Firearm” 


As we explain below, there was no evidence — none — that Hohn ever “used” a firearm 
as that term was explained in Bailey v. United States, 116 S. Ct. 501 (1995). Nor did the 
government offer any evidence that would prove that Hohn “carried” the weapon “during and 
in relation” to the predicate offense. 

1. “Use” Liability. The evidence supporting Hohn’s § 924(c) conviction was succinctly 
summarized by Judge McMillian in his dissent from the panel opinion. He explained that police 
found firearms in the kitchen and bedroom, but not in the living room, where Hohn was 


arrested. Pet. App. 3a (McMillian, J., dissenting). At trial, a government witness, Cindy 


2) The handful of pre-Bailey decisions that endorsed such an expansive concept of “carrying” (see | ited 
States v. Evans, 888 F.2d 891, 895 (D.C. Cir. 1989), cert. denied, 494 U.S. 1019 (1990); United States v. 
Anderson, 881 F.2d 1128, 1140-41 (D.C. Cir. 1989); United States v. Stewart, 779 F.2d 538 (9th Cir. 1985)) 
have been effectively overruled by this Court’s rejection, in Bailey, of the government's “possession for 
protection” theory of “use.” 


34 

Vandry, testified that she had twice purchased methamphetamine from Hohn in his house. Jbid. 
She admitted that on those occasions she had observed “the rifles in the gun case but no 
handguns.” /d. at Sa. Testifying in his own defense, Hohn “admitted possession of some of 
the methamphetamine, admitted he owned the firearms as an avid gunsmith and hunter, [and] 
disputed the police version of the location of some of the firearms * * * .” Jd. at 3a. 

In Bailey, this Court held that, to “use” a firearm within the meaning of Section 924(c), 
a person must “active[ly] employ[]” it by “brandishing, displaying, bartering, striking with, 
* * * firing or attempting to fire” the weapon. 116 S. Ct. at 508-509. In so holding, the Court 
rejected the government's argument that mere possession of a firearm for intended or potential 
use during a predicate offense qualifies as “use.” In this case, there was simply no evidence 
that Hohn brandished, displayed, fired, pointed, or otherwise actively employed any of the 
firearms found in his home “during and in relation to” the predicate offense of possession of 
methamphetamine with intent to distribute it. Indeed, in opposition to Hohn’s 2255 motion, the 
government did not argue that there was any evidence that Hohn “used” the firearms found in 
his home. Instead, it argued that there was sufficient evidence that Hohn carried a firearm. In 
short, the government failed completely to prove that Hohn committed the crime of “us[ing]” 
a firearm under Section 924(c).™ 

2. “Carrying” Liability. The government also failed to prove that Hohn“carrie[d]” a 
firearm. As we explain below, the text, structure, and legislative history of Section 924(c) all 


demonstrate that the crime of “caning” a firearm occurs only when a defendant has or bears 


** The government conceded, moreover, that the jury instruction on “use” was erroneous under Bailey. Pet. 
App. 16a. It further “agree{d] that it would be plain error had the instructions only given the jury the option 
of convicting the defendant under the ‘use’ prong of 18 U.S.C. 924(c), and the evidence only supported that 
option.” Pet. App. 17a (emphasis added). 


35 
a weapon on his person. There is no evidence in this case, however, that Hohn “carried” any 
of the firearms found in his home, and certainly no evidence that he carried them “during” as 
well as “in relation to” the predicate possessory offense of possession of methamphetamine with 
intent to distribute it. The government accordingly failed to prove the crime of “carrying” a 
firearm in a predicate offense under Section 924(c) beyond a reasonable doubt, and Hohn is 
entitled to be have his sentence vacated. 

As this Court has explained, the operative language of Section 924(c) “must be given its 
‘ordinary or natural’ meaning.” Bailey, 116 S. Ct. at 506 (quoting Smith v. United States, 113 
S. Ct. 2050, 2054 (1993)). The ordinary meaning of the word “carry” — especially when used 
in conjunction with the direct object “firearm” or “weapon” — is to have or bear on one’s 
person. This meaning is amply confirmed by standard dictionaries. See, e.g., WEBSTER’S II 
NEW RIVERSIDE UNIVERSITY DICTIONARY 233 (1988) (defining “carry” as “[t]o have or keep 
on one’s person (carry a gun)”) (emphasis altered); BLACK’S LAW DICTIONARY 194 (Sth ed. 
1981) (defining “carry” as “[t]o have or bear upon one’s person, as a watch or weapon; 
locomotion not being essential.”); ibid. (defining “carry arms or weapon” as “[t]o wear, bear, 
or carry them upon the person or in the clothing or in a pocket, for the purpose of use, or for 
the purpose of being armed and ready for offensive or defensive action in case of a conflict with 
another person.”). Accord Cleveland, 106 F.3d at 1067 (acknowledging that the “Black’s 
definition of ‘carry arms or weapons’ limits ‘carrying’ to the defendant's person” but declining 
to adopt that definition). Ordinary experience confirms that to carry a firearm mean to have or 
bear it on one’s person. If asked the question, “Are you carrying a firearm?”, an ordinary 


speaker of the English language would not answer “yes” unless he had a firearm on his person. 


36 
The government's answer to Hohn’s Section 2255 petition in the district court suggests 
that it too understands the phrase “carries a firearm” according to its ordinary meaning. The 
government there argued: 


Evidence was presented to the jury that Hohn carried a firearm. * * * It is true that Hohn 

did not have a firearm on his person when the Omaha Police * * * execut[ed] [the] 

search warrant. However, a reasonable inference can be drawn from the evidence that 

Hohn was carrying a firearm prior to the officers’ entry based upon the items that were 

located, i.e., drugs, guns and ammunition, and where they were located. * * * The .380 

caliber semi-automatic pistol and the Colt revolver found on the kitchen counter within 

arms reach of the methamphetamine, packaged for redistribution, were found in holsters. 

The obvious inference to be drawn from this evidence is that Hohn, at some time prior 

to the execution of the search warrant, entered his residence and placed his belongings, 

which included the methamphetamine and firearms, onto the counter. Therefore, at some 
point prior to the execution of the search warrant, he obviously carried the firearms 
during his drug trafficking activities. 
Pet. App. 17a-19a (emphasis added). As the highlighted language makes clear, even the 
government understands that to “carry a firearm” is to bear it upon one’s person. 

The ordinary meaning of the phrase “carries a firearm” is further confirmed by the 
structure and legislative history of Section 924. Section 924(a)(5)(B)(ii) penalizes the sale, 
delivery, or other transfer of a handgun or ammunition by an adult to a juvenile if the adult 
knows or has reasonable cause to know that the juvenile “intended to carry or otherwise possess 
or discharge or otherwise use the handgun or ammunition in the commission of a crime of 
violence.” 18 U.S.C. § 924(a)(5)(B)(ii) (emphasis added). This provision reflects Congress's 
understanding of “carrying” as a specific kind of possession (bearing on one’s person). In 
addition, Section 924(b) makes it a separate crime to “transport” a firearm with the knowledge 
or “intent to commit therewith an offense punishable by imprisonment for a term exceeding one 
year.” 18 U.S.C. § 924(b). Thus Congress plainly knows how to reach situations where a 


firearm is caused to be moved by a defendant in a vehicle. Had Congress thought such conduct 


37 

was already encompassed within “carrying” liability, there would have been no need to enact 
Section 924(b). 

What is more, every reference to “carrying” a firearm in the legislative history of Section 
924(c) of which we are aware is fully consistent with the ordinary meaning of that term. See, 
e.g., S. Rep. No. 225, at 314 n.10, 1984 U.S.C.C.A.N. at 3492 n.10 (“Evidence that the 
defendant had a gun in his pocket but did not display it, or refer to it, could nevertheless support 
a conviction for “carrying” a firearm in relation to the crime if from the circumstances or 
otherwise it could be found that the defendant intended to use the gun if a contingency arose or 
to make his escape.”) (citation omitted) (emphasis added). 

Moreover, this Court's unanimous decision in Bailey strongly confirms that “carrying” 
a firearm encompasses only bearing the weapon on one's person. Tellingly, the Court in Bailey 
said: “Under the interpretation we enunciate today, a firearm can be used without being carried, 
e.g., when an offender has a gun on display during a transaction, or barters with a firearm 
without handling it, and a firearm can be carried without being used, e.g., when an offender 
keeps a gun hidden in his clothing throughout a drug transaction.” Bailey, 116 S. Ct. at 507 
(emphasis added). As the highlighted language makes clear, this Court in Bailey had in mind 
the ordinary meaning of “carrying”; and the opinion’s clear intimation that a firearm that is 
bartered for drugs would not be carried if it is not handled, confirms that having a firearm in 
one’s hands or one’s person is a necessary precondition to carrying it. Also significant is this 
Court's rejection in Bailey of the government's contention that there could be liability under 
Section 924(c) merely by virtue of a defendant's “placement of a firearm to provide a sense of 


security or to embolden.” 116 S. Ct. at 508. The Court explained: 


38 


It follows, according to this argument, that a gun placed in a closet is “used,” because 
its mere presence emboldens or protects its owner. We disagree. Under this argument, 
mere possession of a firearm by a drug offender, at or near the site of a drug crime or 
its proceeds or paraphernalia, is a “use” by the offender, because its availability for 
intimidation, attack, or defense would always, presumably, embolden or comfort the 
offender. But the mere inert presence of a firearm, without more, is not enough to 
trigger § 924(c)(1). Perhaps the nonactive nature of this asserted “use” is clearer if a 
synonym is used: storage. A defendant cannot be charged under § 924(c)(1) merely for 
storing a weapon near drugs or drug proceeds. Storage of a fiream, without its more 
active employment, is not reasonably distinguishable from possession. 
Ibid. (emphasis added). Although the foregoing discussion was directed at “use” liability, there 
is no reason why a similarly broad reading of “carrying” liability — to include mere possession 
of a firearm in proximity to drugs — would “be reasonably distinguishable from possession” 
either. 

Finally, even if the Court were to conclude that the language, structure, and legislative 
history of Section 924(c)(1) do not compel the interpretation of “carry” suggested above, there 
can be no serious doubt that our interpretation is a reasonable one, and that the statute is 
therefore ar least ambiguous on this score. Under the rule of lenity, “ambiguity concerning the 
ambit of criminal statutes should be resolved in favor of lenity.” Rewis v. United States, 401 
U.S. 808, 812 (1971) (citing Bell v. United States, 349 U.S. 81, 83 (1955)). That principle is 
dispositive here. 

In sum, the crime of “carrying” a firearm under Section 924(c) requires that a defendant 
bear the weapon on his person. There was no evidence in this case that Hohn “carried” the 
firearms in this sense, much less that he carried them “during” the predicate possessory offense. 
Although the government argued below that a fair inference of such conduct could have been 
drawn from the facts, that submission was simply incorrect. There was no evidence presented 


that Hohn ever carried the firearms located in his kitchen and in his bedroom drawers. In any 


39 
event, even if there were proof of carrying at some time by Hohn, there was no proof (much 
less proof beyond a reasonable doubt) that Hohn carried the firearms during the predicate 
offense. See United States v. Harrison, 103 F.3d 986, 990-92 (D.C. Cir. 1997). For all we 
know, any carrying that occurred could well have preceded Hohn’s coming into possession of 
the methamphetamine he was charged with possessing with intent to distribute. If so, then Hohn 
plainly did not violate Section 924(c) — under any view of the evidence. 


D. Because The Jury Instruction Grossly Misstated The Law, Petitioner Is 
Entitled, At A Minimum, To A New Trial 


As previously explained, Hohn is entitled to have his conviction set aside on the Section 
924(c) count because of this Court's decision in Bailey. But even if that were not the case, and 
the government had presented sufficient evidence from which the jury could conclude beyond 
a reasonable doubt that Hohn “carried” a firearm in violation of Section 924(c), he would be 
entitled to a new trial because of fundamental defects in the jury instructions. The jury was 
charged that 

[t}he phrase “used a firearm” means having a firearm available to aid in the commission 

of possession of Methamphetamine with intent to distribute. Similarly, the phrase 

“carried a firearm” does not require proof of actual possession of a firearm or use of it 

in any affirmative manner, but does require proof beyond a reasonable doubt that the 

firearm was available to provide protection in connection with the possession of 

Methamphetamine with intent to distribute or facilitate success. 
Pet. App. 3la (emphasis added). As previously noted (at note 23, supra), the government 
acknowledged that the part of the chagge, solating to firearm “use” was erroneous and would 
have qualified as “plain error” if use were the only basis for liability charged to the jury. 

The jury's instruction on the meaning of “carrying” liability, however, suffers from the 


same flaw as the “use” instruction. Indeed, “use” and “carry” are defined in the Eighth 


40 
Circuit's model instruction in a virtually identical fashion: both permit a finding of liability based 
solely on whether the firearm is “available” to assist the defendant during the predicate act. 
That theory of liability, however, was categorically rejected by this Court in Bailey. See 116 
S. Ct. at 508 (explaining that government's “possession for protection” theory “is not reasonably 
distinguishable from possession,” which Section 924(c) does not reach). Nor does the Eighth 
Circuit's instruction for “carrying” even remotely resemble the ordinary meaning of that term, 
when used in conjunction with weapons. Hohn could easily have “carried” the firearms within 
the meaning of the Eighth Circuit's instruction without bearing them on his person, without 
transporting them within immediate reach in a vehicle, and even without transporting them in 
an inaccessible place in a vehicle.” Indeed, the jury instruction did not even require a finding 
that Hohn posessed the firearms for a finding of “carrying.” At a minimum, petitioner is 
entitled to a new trial. 
CONCLUSION 
The petition for a writ of certiorari should be granted. j

»* The government's suggestion that the jury could properly have found that Hohn carried the firearms by 
bearing them on his person at some previous point in time (but still during his predicate possessory offense) 
is wrong not only because there was no evidence to support such a finding, but also because the jury 
instruction failed completely to present that issue for the jury’s determination.

MEMORANDUM AND ORDER 


ARNOLD F. HOEN, 


Defendant. 


Defendant Hohn has filed a motion to vacate, set aside or 
correct his sentence pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 2255. (See filing 
17S). Liberally construing the allegations of the motion, Haines 
v. Kerner, 404 U.S. $19, 520-21 (1972), I conclude that the 
government should respond to defendant’s clain. 


BACKGROUND 


Defendant was convicted on one count of possession with 
intent to distribute methamphetamine near a school, in violation 
of 21 U.S.C. §§ 841(a), 845(a); ome count of carrying a firearm 
in relation to a drug trafficking crime, in violation of 18 
U.S.C. § 924(c) (1); and one count of being a felon in possession 
of a firearm, in violation of 18 U.S.C. §§ 922(g). Defendant was 
sentenced to serve a term of 90 months in prison. He appealed 
the sentence and the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Eighth Circuit affirmed. 


Defendant now attacks his firearm conviction under 18 U.S.C. 
§ 924(¢c)(1) om the ground that the government failed to present 


10a 


ee 


~ .e 


evidence sufficient so estaSlish that he “used” a firearm within 


the meaning that statutory section. As support f9Fr that clain 
defendant cites Bailey v. United States, 116 S.Ct. 5091, 13 


L.£d.2¢ 472 (1995).' 


DISCUSSION 


A section 2255 motion is not a substitute for direct appeal. 

r : tates, 25 F.3d 704, 706 (8th Cir. 1994); Poor 
Thunder v. United States, #10 F.2d 817, 823 (8th Cir. 1987). As 
such, a claim which could have been presented on direct appeal 
generally will not be considered in a section 2255 proceeding 
absent a showing of “cause” for the failure to raise the issue 
and “actual prejudice” resulting from that failure. Ramey v. 
United States, 8 F.3d 1313, 1314 (8th Cir. 1993); Reid v. United 
States, 976 F.2d 446, 447-48 (8th Cir. 1992), cert. denied, 113 
S. Ct. 1351 (1992). To show cause that would excuse such a 
procedural default, a defendant must show that some objective 


' In Bailey, the United States Supreme Court held that, to 
obtain a conviction for use of a firearm under 138 U.S.C. § 
924(c) (1), the government must prove “an active employment of the 
firearm by the defendant, a use that makes the firearm an operative 
factor in relation to the predicate offense." Bailey, i116 S.Ct. at 
505. In so ruling, the Supreme Court overruled prior authority 
holding that a defendant could be convicted under § 924(c) (1) based 
on evidence that the firearm was physically available to the 
defendant or that the defendant intended to use it if the need 
arose. See e.c., United States v. Jones, 990 F.2d 1047, 1050 (8th 
Cir. 1993) ("‘use’ may be inferred from the availability and 


presence of the firearm, and actual possession is not necessary 


provided the firearm is under the defendant’s control"), cert. 
ied, 114 S.Ct. 699 (1994), denial of habeas corpus afs’d, 29 
F.3d 1184 (8th Cir. 1994). 

The Bailey decision applies retroactively. Bel) _v. United 
States, 917 F.Supp. 681, 683-84 (E.D. Mo. 1996); Sanabria v. United 
States, 916 F.Supp. 106, 109-14 (D. P.R. 1996); United States v. 
Turner, 914 F.Supp. 48, 49-50 (W.D. N.¥. 1996); Abreu _v. United 
States, 911 F.Supp. 203, 206-07 (E.D. Va. 1996). 


lla 


r

0. DISCUSSION 
A. The Speedy Trial Act Claim 


The Speedy Trial Act, 18 U.S.C. §§ 3161. 
3174 (1988), requires that a criminal 
defendant be tried within seventy days of the 
indictment or the date of first appearance 
before a judicial officer, whichever is later. Id. 


Copr. © West 1997 No claim to orig. U.S. govt. works 


16a 


Page 3 


§ 3161(cX1). The statute allows, however, 
certain delays to be excluded for purposes of 
calculating the seventy-day time limit. See id. 
§ 3161(h). If the government fails to comply 
with the terms of the Speedy Trial Act, a 
defendant may move for dismissal either with 
or without prejudice. Id § 3162(a). The 
district court's legal findings are reviewed de 
novo, but factual findings are reviewed under 
the clearly erroneous standard. United States 
v. Hoslett, 998 F.2d 648, 652 (9th Cir. 1993). 


Hohn argues that the court incorrectly 
excluded two periods of delay from its 
calculation for Speedy Trial Act purposes: (1) 
the period from October 20 through November 
9 (twenty-one days), during which the court 
considered pretrial services’ petition (FN3); 
and (2) the period from December 21 through 
January 13, 1991 (twenty-four days), during 
which Hohn was treated as an inpatient at St. 
Gabriel's Alcohol and Drug Treatment Center. 
(FN4] The district court [FN5) excluded both 
these periods of time from its calculation of 
the seventy-day limit because it found that the 
first period was excludable pursuant to § 
31610hX1XF) as a pretrial motion and the 
second period of time was excludable pursuant 
to either § 3161(hX1XF) or, in the alternative, 
§ 3161(hX4). We find that for purposes of 
determining whether the Speedy °1304 Trial 
Act seventy-day limit had lapsed, both periods 
of time were properly excluded from the 
district court's calculation of time. 


FN3. Pretnal services’ petition was filed on October 
1. However, the period of time up to and including 
October 19 is not in dispute. 


FN4. The status of the other days is not in dispute. 
The parties agree that the period from August 31, 
1990, through September 18, 1990, counts towards 
the 70-day limit (a total of 19 days) The parties 
likewise agree that September 19 through October 
19 is excludable pursuant to the stipulated 
agreement. The parties agree that the period of 
time from November 10 through December 9 
counts towards the 70-day limit (bringing the tou! 
to 49 undisputed days). The parties also agree that 
December 10 through December 20 is excludable 
due to the mouons that were filed before the court. 


8 F.3d 1301 
(Cite as: 8 F.3d 1301, °1304) 


FNS. Magistrate Judge Richard G. Kopf made these 
findings. The district court, after a de novo review, 
sdopted the magistrate judge's report wn its entirety. 
The magistrate judge's findings subsequently 
adopted by the district court will be referred to as 
the distnet court findings.

1990

pursuant to § 3161(hX1XF) (subsection (F)) as a

[3] Motions excludable under subsection (F) 
include any pretrial motion and are not 
limited to those motions enumerated in 
Federal Rule Criminal Procedure 12(bX2). 
United States v. Wilson, 835 F.2d 1440, 1443 
(D.C.Cir. 1987). Pretrial motions are 
excludable under subsection (F) because these 
motions require both court and party time. Id.

pretrial detention, from the date of filing until

are both a service to the court and an

(5) Hohn'’s argument that the district court 
erred when it failed to make a § 3161(hX8XA) 
(FN8) statutory finding is without merit. 
Section 3161(8XA) allows a court to grant 
continuances should that court find it is in the 
interests of justice to do so after weighing the 
best interests of the defendant and the public 
to a speedy trial. The court under § 3161(8XA) 
is required to make explicit findings; the 
court is not required to make explicit findings 
for § 31610hX1) delays. Section 3161(8XA) 
provides an additional basis, in addition to the 
(hX1) bases, for the court to grant a delay 
which will toll the Speedy Trial Act clock. For 
example, § 3161(8XA) is frequently used by 
the court to grant continuances which will toll 
the Speedy Trial Act clock when, due to 
complexities in the case, counsel requires 
additional time to prepare. See United States 
v. Driver, 945 F.2d 1410, 1414 (8th Cir.1991), 
cert. denied, 502 U.S. 1109, 112 S.Ct. 1209, 
117 L.Ed.2d 448 (1992); United States v. 
Fogarty, 692 F.2d 542, 546 (8th Cir.1982), 
cert. denied, 460 U.S. 1040, 103 S.Ct. 1434, 75 
L.Ed.2d 792 (1983). The district court did not 
err when it allowed the hearing to be 
continued without making findings; here, the 
excluded pursuant to 
subsection (F), not § 3161(8XA). 


FNS. 18 U.S.C. § 3161(h)S)A) states in pertinent 
part: Any period of delay resulting from a 
continuance granted by any judge ... if the judge 
granted such continuance on the basis of his 
findings that the ends of justice served by taking 
such acuon outweigh the best interest of the public 
and the defendant in a speedy tral. No such penod 
of delay resultang from a contanuance granted by the 
court m accordance with this paragraph shall be 
excludable under this subsection uniess the court 
sets forth ... its reasons for finding that the ends of 
justice served by the granting of such continuance

(Cite as: 8 F.3d 1301, *1305) 


outweigh the best interests of the public and the 
defendant in a speedy tral.

[6] We further find that the period of time 
from December 21, 1990, through January 
*1306 13, 1991, was properly excluded for 
Speedy Trial Act purposes pursuant to 
subsection (F). [FN9] Hohn filed a motion to 
reconsider detention on December 18. [FN10] 
The district court requested the advice of 
pretrial services. After a hearing on 
December 20, 1990, and upon the advice of 
pretrial services, the court entered an order 
conditionally releasing Hohn on December 20 
in order that Hohn could be evaluated at St. 
Gabriel's Alcohol and Drug Treatment Center. 
If Hohn was not accepted into drug treatment, 
he was to be returned to the custody of the 
United States Marshal. If Hohn failed to 
successfully complete drug treatment, he was 
to be returned to the custody of the Marshal. 
Only upon successful completion of the drug 
treatment program would the district court 
then consider granting a release order with 
specific terms and conditions. 


FN9. The district court found this period of time 
was also excludable pursuant to § 3161(h)\(4); that 
is, the district court found that Hohn while in drug 
treatment was physically unable to stand trial. 
Because we find this period of time was properly 
excluded pursuant to subsection (F), we need not 
reach the issue of Hohn's physica! availability for 
tnal while in drug treatment. 


FN10. Hohn concedes that subsection (F) excludes 
the period of time from December 18 through 
December 20 and is therefore only contending that 
the period from December 21 through January 13 
was unproperty excluded. 


States v. Noone, 913 F.2d 20, 27 (let 
Cir.1990), cert. denied, 500 U.S. 906, 111 S.Ct. 
1686, 114 L.Ed 2d 81 (1991). Thus, when 
Hohn filed his motion to reconsider detention 
on December 18, subsection (F) tolled the 


Copr. © West 1997 No claim to orig. U.S. govt. works 


19a 


ro 


Page 6 


Speedy Trial Act clock until all information 
necessary to that motion’s determination was 
before the court. Subsection (F) excludes the 
period of time after a motion is submitted but 
before it is under advisement. Henderson, 476 
U.S. at 331, 106 S.Ct. at 1877. That is, 
subsection (F) excludes the period of time after 
a hearing in which the court record may 
remain open to receive further information 
Here, the district court did not receive all the 
information necessary to determine the 
motion to reconsider detention until January 
23, 1991, when Hohn completed drug 
treatment. The district court’s order setting 
conditions of release was not issued until 
January 23, 1991. Consequently, during the 
entire time period at issue, from December 21 
until January 13, 1991, the Speedy Trial Act 
clock was tolled pursuant to subsection (F).

B. Other Claims 


Hohn makes two other claims: (1) that the 
district court erred when it denied his motion 


(8) Hohn asserts that evidence obtained from 


8 F.3d 1301 
(Cite as: 8 F.3d 1301, *1306) 


See United States v. Moore, 956 
P50 068 Gh Cee.18n. The issue is not 
whether the °1307 warrant complied with § 
3109 but whether the state officials complied 
with Nebraska law and the Fourth 
Amendment. See id. at 848. We find that the 
district court properly denied the motion to 
suppress the evidence obtained from this 
warrant because it was supported by probable 
cause and complied with Nebraska law and 
the Fourth Amendment. 


FN11. in Nebraska no-knock warrants are 
expressly allowed. Neb.Rev Stat. § 29-411 (1989). 


FN12. 18 U.S.C. § 3109 provides: The officer may 
break open any outer or inner door or window of a 
house, or any part of a house, or anything therein, 
to execute a search warrant, if, afler notice of his 
authority and purpose, he w refused admiitance or 
when necessary to liberate himself or a person 
aiding him in the execution of the warrant. 


_ (9) Hohn also contends that Count I of the 


1988). Hohn’s residence was located within 


remodeling at the time of the arrest that it 
was not a school as a matter of law. 


FN13. Hohn was charged in Count | with « 
violation of 21 U.S.C. § 845e; however, this 
statute has been transferred to 21 U.S.C. § 860. 


requires that the defendant have an intent to 
distribute (or manufacture) a controlled 
substance and at the time be within 1000 feet 
of a school. See 21 U.S.C. § 860. Nothing in 
the statute requires that school be in session 
or that children be near or around the school 
at the time of the offense. See United States 


Copr. © West 1997 No claim to orig. U.S. govt. works 


Page 7 


v. Jones, 779 F.2d 121, 123 (2d Cir. 1985), cert. 
denied, 475 U.S. 1031, 106 S.Ct. 1236, 89 
L.Ed.2d 344 (1986). In Jones, the defendant 
argued that the schoolyard statute did not 
apply to his distribution of drugs because the 
offense occurred at night without any 
potential for affecting children. Id. The Jones 
court rejected this argument finding, as we do, 
that the language in the statute is 
unambiguous and does not require that a 
school be open at the time of the offense. Id. 


The magistrate judge found in an opinion 
adopted by the district court that as a 
preliminary matter there was sufficient 
evidence to find that Central Park was a 
school. The final determination as to whether 
Central Park met the statutory definition of 
school was determined by the magistrate 
judge to be a question of fact appropriate for 
the jury. See also United States v. Brookins, 
919 F.2d 281, 284 (5th Cir.1991) (holding that 
there was sufficient evidence before the jury 
for it to find by any common definition that 
the institution in question was a school). We 
agree. The Omaha public school system 
continued to carry Central Park on its books 
as a school. The children continued to use the 
school grounds. A school need not be in 
session to trigger the schoolyard statute's 
penalties. The district court did not err when 
it denied Hohn’s motion to dismiss Count I. 


i. CONCLUSION 


Accordingly, we find that the district court 
properly denied Hohn’s motion to dismiss on 
Speedy Trial Act grounds, his motion to 
suppress evidence, and his motion to dismiss 
Count I of the indictment. We affirm the 
judgment of the district court.

Pursuant to Rule 5, Rules Governing Proceedings in the United 
States District Courts Under Section 2255 of Title 28, United 
States Code, and this Courc's Order of May 1, 1996, the United 

tates answers Filing #175, Defendant's Motion to Vacate, Set Aside 
or Correct Sentence, as follows: 

1. The United States denies that Arnold F. Hohn is entitled 
to any post-conviction relief. No evidentiary hearing has been 
accorded regarding the post-conviction motion. An evidentiary 
hearing will not be needed as this matter can be resolved on the 
basis of the record and files before the Court. The United States 
respectfully submits that this is an issue of law that must be 
developed from the transcript of the record at trial and post- 
conviction pleadings and briefs. 

2. Hohn claims that his Section 924(c) conviction should be 
vacated because of the United States Supreme Court's recent 
decision in Bailev v. United States, 116 S.Ct. 501, 123 L.Ed.24 472 
(1995). The United States denies that the Supreme Court's decision 
in Bailey would grant relief to Hohn. The United States would 


submit that sufficient evidence was presented to support the jury's 


verdict and Hohn's conviction for carrying a firearn. 


22a 


c r 
3. The United States acknowledses that the jury LnStruction 
provicec does not comport with the status of law since the Supreme 


Court's decision in Bailey. 

Under Federal Rule cf Criminal Procedure 30, in order to 
Claim error in a jury instruction, an objection is required which 
specifies the grounds of the objection. In the present case, the 
charge to the jury with regard to the 924(c) violstion is found in 
instruction #7. Although Hohn objected to instruction £7, his 
objection did not go to whether one must affirmatively brandish a 
firearm but, rather, objected to the wording of the particular 


instruction. Specifically, Hohn asked the Court to drop the words 


"to aid in the commission of" and substitute the words "for the 


purpose of aiding in the commission cf." (Tr. at 1332-34). 


Therefore, only if there is plain error, may this the Court vacate 


oo 


the conviction. 


In United States v. caldwell], 954 F.2d 496 (8th Cir. 


1992), the Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals said 


"the Supreme Court has explained that the 
plain-error doctrine ‘authorizes the Court of 
Appeals to correct only ‘particularly 
egregious errors’. .. that ‘seriously affect 
the fairness, integrity or public reputation 
of judicial proceedings.'" United States v. 
Young, 470 U.S. 1, 15 (1985) (quoting United 


States v. Frady, 456 U.S. 152, 163 (1982) and 
United States v. Atkinson, 297 U.S. 157, 160 
(1936)). 


The plain-error rule is to be "‘used sparingly,'" and 
only when there has been a fundamental error so basic and 
prejudicial that justice could not have been done, or when the 


error deprives the accused of a fundamental right. Young, 470 U.S. 


23a 


——_— 
© . eee 


: Pr 


az iS (quoting Eerady, 456 U.S. at 63); & 
F.2d 1413, 1422 (7th Cir. 1990). za. $00-S01. 

The governmen= agrees that it would be plain-error haa 
the instructions only given the jury the option of convicting the 
defendan= under the “use” srong of 18 U.S.C. § 924(C), and the 
evidence only supported “hat option. However, in this case, as in 
Bailey, the jury was instructed that the defendant could be 
convicted if he used or carried a firearn. 

4. The question remains as to the appropriate standard of 
review. The Third Circuit, in a case involving a Bailey question, 
has cbserved: 


“we must now determine whether the district 
court's error in instructing the jury was 
harmless or requires reversal of Mr. Price's 
conviction on the Section 924(¢)(1) count. 
The error was one of statutory interpretation, 
not constitutional in nature. "We upheld that 
non-constitutional error is harmless when ‘it 
is highly probable that the error did not 
contribute to the judgement.'" .. . citations 
omitted. Because we conclude that the error 
was harmless, we will affirm. . 


United States v. Price, 1996 W.L. 67398 (3rd Cir. (Pa.)), at p. 4. 

If the Court determines that the error was constitutional 
in nature, the standard of review would be whether the error was 
harmless, beyond a reasonable doubt. See United States v. Davis, 
417 U.S. 333, 346 (1974); Chapman v. Galifornia, 386 U.S. 11 


(1967). 


5. Evidence was presented to the jury that Hohn carried a 
firearm. That evidence is recited in the prosecution version and 


can be found at the transcript of trial. It is true that Hohn dic 


24a 


¢ re 

mot have a firearm Om Als serscn when che Omaha Police Division's
