QUESTION PRESENTED 

Whether petitioner's motion under 28 U.S.C. 2255, which 
alleged that his conviction under 18 U.S.C. 924(c) for using and 
carrying a firearm during and relation to a drug trafficking crime 
could not stand under this Court's later decision narrowing the 
"use" element in Bailey v. United States, 116 S. Ct. 501 (1995), 
made a purely statutory claim, or made a "substantial showing of 
the denial of a constitutional right" as required to obtain a 


certificate of appealability under 28 U.S.C. 2253(c) (2). 


(I) 


IN THE SUPREME COURT OF THE UNITED STATES 
OCTOBER TERM, 1996 


No. 96-8986 
ARNOLD F. HOHN, PETITIONER 
Vv. 


UNITED STATES OF AMERICA 


ON PETITION FOR A WRIT OF CERTIORARI 
TO THE UNITED STATES COURT OF APPEALS 
FOR THE EIGHTH CIRCUIT 


BRIEF FOR THE UNITED STATES 


OPINION BELOW 
The opinion of the court of appeals (Pet. App. la-7a) is 
reported at 99 F.3d 892 (1996). 
JURISDICTION 
The judgment of the court of appeals was entered on November 
4, 1996. A petition for rehearing was denied on February 10, 1997. 
The petition for a writ of certiorari was filed on May 12, 1997. 
The jurisdiction of this Court is invoked under 28 U.S.C. 1254(1). 
STATEMENT 
Following a jury trial in the United States District Court for 
th District of Nebraska, petitioner was convicted of possession of 


2thamphetamine within 1,000 feet of a public school with intent to 


2 
distribute it, in violation of 21 U.S.C. 845(a); using and carrying 
a firearm during and in relation to a drug trafficking offense, in 
violation of 18 U.S.C. 924(c) (1); and being a felon in possession 
of a firearm, in violation of 18 U.S.C. 922(g) and 924(a) (2). 
Petitioner appealed his sentence and the court of appeals affirmed. 
United States v. Hohn, 8 F.3d 1301 (8th Cir. 1993). 

Petitioner then filed a motion under 28 U.S.C. 2255, 
challenging his conviction under 18 U.S.C. 924(c) (1). The district 
court dismissed the motion, Pet. App. 8a-9a, and the court of 
appeals denied petitioner's request for a certificate of 
appealability. Id. at la-7a. 

1. The evidence at trial showed that, on June 20, 1990, 
police officers executed a search warrant at petitioner's house in 
Omaha, Nebraska. As a result of their search, police recovered 
approximately 16 grams of methamphetamine, including one gram 
recovered from petitioner's person. The police discovered in a 
brown case on the kitchen counter 12 grams of methamphetamine that 
was packaged for redistribution. Within arm's reach of the 
methamphetamine, the police found a loaded .380 caliber semi- 
automatic pistol, a loaded Colt revolver, and a loaded Ruger .357 
pistol. The .380 caliber semi-automatic pistol and the Colt 
revolver were in holsters. The police also found in the kitchen a 
grinder bearing methamphetamine residue, a Ruger .22 caliber semi- 
automatic pistol, and an Excam .22 caliber revolver. On top of a 
dz-sser in petitioner's bedroom, police discovered a cigarette pack 

ontaining three grams of methamphetamine, a loaded handgun and 


3 
$293 in currency. Pet. App. 25a; Gov't Br. in Opp. to Pet. for 
Rehearing ("Gov't Rehearing Brief") at 2; see also Pet. App. 15a. 

2. Petitioner appealed, but did not challenge the sufficiency 
of the evidence to support his conviction under 18 U.S.C. 
924(c) (1). The court of appeals affirmed. United States v. Hohn, 
8 F.3d 1301 (8th Cir. 1993). 

- On December 6, 1995, this Court held, in Bailey v. United 
States, 116 S. Ct. 501 (1995), that a conviction for use of a 
firearm under Section 924(c) "requires evidence sufficient to show 
an active employment of the firearm by the defendant." Id. at 505. 
Active employment includes uses such as "brandishing, displaying, 
bartering, striking with, and most obviously, firing or attempting 
to fire" the weapon. Jd. at 508. By contrast, possession of a 
firearm -- without more -- does not constitute a "use," nor does 
"placement of a firearm to provide a sense of security or to 
embolden[.}]" JIbid. 

4. Petitioner filed a pro se motion under 28 U.S.C. 2255 
making two claims. First, he contended that, under Bailey, the 
facts established at his trial were insufficient to support a 
conviction for violating Section 924(c)(1). Petitioner acknowl- 
edged that he had not raised such an insufficiency claim on direct 
appeal, but argued that his default should be excused because his 
conduct “was mot a crime” and, therefore, it would be a 
"fundamental miscarriage of justice” not to review his claim. 
Mc-ion to Vacate, Set Aside or Correct Sentence Pursuant to 28 


S.C. § 2255, at 8. Second, petitioner challenged the jury 


4 
instructions at his trial. He argued that the jury instructions 
did not properly define the phrase “use a firearm" to require proof 
of active use, and that correct jury instructions would likely have 
produced an acquittal. Jg. at 11. 

In response, the government contended that petitioner was not 
entitled to relief under Bailey because the evidence at trial was 
sufficient to show that petitioner had "carried" a firearm. Noting 
that the police found weapons in holsters within arm's reach of 
methamphetamine in petitioner's kitchen, the government argued that 
the jury could infer that petitioner had “entered his residence and 
placed his belongings, which included the methamphetamine and the 
firearms, onto the counter." Pet. App. 25a-26a. The government 
acknowledged that, under Bailey, the jury instructions at 
petitioner's trial erroneously defined the word "use," but it 
contended that the error was harmless because "(t]he evidence 
obviously establishes ‘carrying.'" Jd. at 22a-26a. 

The district court held that petitioner had waived his Bailey 
claim by failing to raise it on direct appeal. Pet. App. 8a-9a. 
In so holding, the court relied on United States v. McKinney, 79 
F.3d 105, 109 (8th Cir. 1996), in which the Eighth Circuit held 
that a defendant who had not raised a Bailey claim at trial or in 
his initial brief on direct appeal had waived it. This Court 
recently vacated and remanded McKinney for further consideration in 
light of Johnson v. Wnited States, 117 S. Ct. 1544 (1997). 


— 


5 
McKinney v. United States, 117 S. Ct. 1816 (1997) .? 

5. a. Om April 24, 1996, while petitioner's Section 2255 
motion was pending in the district court, the President signed into 
law the Anti-Terrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996, 
Pub. L. No. 104-132, 110 Stat. 1214 ("the Act" or "the AEDPA"). 
Title I of the Act made changes to the law governing motions for 
collateral relief for federal prisoners under 28 U.S.C. 2255. 
Section: 102 of the Act amended 28 U.S.C. 2253 to require a prisoner 
to obtain a “certificate of appealability” before appealing from a 
district court's denial of his Section 2255 motion. The new 
provision states that "unless a circuit justice or judge issues a 
certificate of appealability, an appeal may not be taken to the 
court of appeals from * * * the final order in a proceeding under 
section 2255." 28 U.S.C. 2253 (c) (1) (B). A certificate of 
appealability may be issued "only if the applicant has made a 
substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right." 28 
U.S.C. 2253(c) (2). 

b. On July 29, 1996, petitioner sought to appeal from the 
district court's decision denying his motion. See Gov't Rehearing 
Brief 4. The court of appeals, however, denied him a certificate 
of appealability, concluding that he had not made a "substantial 
showing of the denial of a constitutional right." Pet. App. la-2a. 


The court noted tl.at petitioner had argued in the district court in 


2 On remand, the Eighth Circuit reached the merits of 
McKinney's Bailey claim and vacated his conviction. The court held 
‘hat evidence that McKinney kept weapons in a drug fortress did not 

that he had used under Section 924(c). United States 
. McKinney, No. 95-2132, 1997 WL 395262 (8th Cir. July 16, 1997). 


6 

his motion for collateral relief that the jury instructions at his 
trial were inconsistent with Bailey. As a result, the court of 
appeals concluded, "petitioner is not making a constitutional 
Claim. He is making a claim to a federal statutory right." Jbid. 
Because "Bailey did no more than interpret a statute, and an 
incorrect application of a statute by a district court, or any 
other court, does not violate the Constitution," the court of 
appeals denied a certificate of appealability. Jd. at 2a. 

Judge McMillian dissented. Jd. at 2a-7a. After noting that 
courts have granted post-conviction relief following a change in 
the law, the dissent stated that "'a judgment in a criminal case in 
which the prosecution has offered and the record discloses no proof 
whatever of various elements of the crime charged has a fatal 
constitutional taint for lack of due process of law.'" Pet. App. 
6a (quoting United States v. Liguori, 438 F.2d 663, 669 (2d Cir. 
1971)). The dissent also found that petitioner had "cause" for 
failing to challenge the jury instructions on appeal "([b] ecause 
Bailey represents such a clear break with precedent regarding the 
definition of ‘use' under § 924(c)(1)." Jd. at 7a. Accordingly, 
the dissent concluded that this case presented a "substantial 
showing of the denial of a constitutional right." Jbid. 

Petitioner sought rehearing, with a suggestion for rehearing 
en banc. In opposing further review, the government took the 
position that the panel's decision was correct. The full court of 
appeals denied rehearing en banc, with Judges McMillian, Beam, 
‘oken, and Hansen dissenting. Pet. App. 2la. 


- 
DISCUSSION 
Petitioner seeks review of the court of appeals' holding that 


he was not entitled to a certificate of appealability because his 


Claim -- that he is presently incarcerated for conduct that, under 
Bailey, is not criminal -- raises a statutory, not a constitu- 
tional, issue. We agree that claims of the kind raised by 


petitioner in his motion under Section 2255 may fairly be 
characterized as "constitutional," and that the court of appeals 
erred in concluding otherwise. Accordingly, we believe that the 
judgment of the court of appeals should be vacated and the case 
remanded for further consideration of whether a certificate of 
appealability should be granted here. 

1. In Bailey, this Court "clarif[ied) the meaning of ‘use’ 
under § 924(c)(1)" by looking to "the language of the statute" and 
"consider[ing) not only the bare meaning of the word ['use'] but 
also its placement and purpose in the statutory scheme." 116 S. 
Ct. at 506. That holding resolved a question of statutory 
construction. The Court did not purport to apply or construe the 
Constitution, but rather "determine[d)]) the meaning Congress 
intended." Id. at 505-506. Thus, "Bailey did not establish a new 
rule of constitutional law, but simply interpreted a substantive 
criminal statute." In re Dorsainvil, No. 96-8074, 1997 WL 409442 
De (3d Cir. July 23, 1997) (citing cases from the Fourth, 
Seventh, Tenth, and Eleventh Circuits agreeing with that 
proposition). 


The fact that petitioner's Section 2255 motion invoked a 


8 
statutory holding of this Court, however, does not mean that his 
claim is purely a statutory one. In bringing a collateral 
challenge to the sufficiency of the evidence and the adequacy of 


the jury instructions, a movant under Section 2255 is entitled to 


rely on the statutory meaning of the word "use" as later : 


interpreted by this Court in Bailey.* A contention that, in light 
of Bailey, the evidence was insufficient to support a conviction 
under Section 924(c)(1) may state a claim under the Due Process 
Clause. See Jackson v. Virginia, 443 U.S. 307, 313-316 (1979) (Due 
Process Clause requires that a criminal conviction rest on evidence 
sufficient to support a conclusion by a rational trier of fact that 
every element of the offense has been established beyond a 
reasonable doubt). A contention that the jury instructions failed 
to require the jury to make findings on every essential element of 
the offense under Section 924(c), as construed in Bailey, may state 
a claim under the Fifth and Sixth Amendments. See United States v. 
Gaudin, 115 S. Ct. 2310, 2320 (1995) ("The Constitution gives a 
criminal defendant the right to have a jury determine, beyond a 
reasonable doubt, his guilt of every element of the crime with 
which he is charged."). Petitioner raised claims of both varieties 


2 Several courts of appeals have so held in onsidering 
motions under Section 2255 relying on Bailey. See, e.g., Stanback 
v. United States, 113 F.3d 651, 654 & n.2 (7th Cir. 1997); United 


States v. McPhail, 112 F.3d 197, 199 (Sth Cir. 1997); United States 
v. Barnhardt, 93 F.3d 706, 708-709 (10th Cir. 1996). Courts have 


reached the same conclusion in considering other new decisions 
narrowing the substantive scope of a criminal statute. See United 
Stares v. McKie, 73 F.3d 1149, 1153 (D.C. Cir. 1996); United States 
-. , 52 F.3d 298, 299 (10th Cir. 1995); United States v. 
290d, 969 F.2d 774 (9th Cir. 1992). 


9 
in his pro ge motion for collateral relief under 28 U.S.C. 2255. 
See pp. 3-4, supra.’ 

Claims of the sort that petitioner raised do not automatically 
satisfy Section 2253(c) (2)'s requirement of a "gubstantial showing 
of the denial of a constitutional right." For example, a claim 
under Section 924(c) that the evidence was insufficient to show a 
"use" of a firearm in light of Bailey would not constitute a 
substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right absent 
an indication that the evidence was insufficient to show a 
"carrying." See United States v. Eyer, 113 F.3d 470, 475-476 (3d 
Cir. 1997) (Section 924(c) conviction can be sustained based on 
evidence of "carry" without regard to "“use”). But Section 
2253(c)(3) does not require certainty that the collateral 
petitioner would prevail. See Barefoot v. Estelle, 463 U.S. 880, 
893 n.4 (1983) (to make a substantial showing, a petitioner must 
demonstrate that the issues are "'debatable among jurists of 
reason; that a court could resolve the issues [in a different 
manner); or that the questions are adequate to deserve 
encouragement to proceed further.'") (quoting Gordon v. Willis, 516 
F. Supp. 911, 913 (N.D. Ga. 1980) (emphasis in original; internal 


quotation marks omitted)). The court of appeals did not evaluate 


3 In Davis v. United States, 417 U.S. 333 (1974), this Court 
considered a Section 2255 motion claiming that the defendant had 
been convicted for an act that the law did not make criminal, as 
established by an intervening decision interpreting substantive 
law. The Court held that such a claim was cognizable under Section 
2255 as a statutory claim. 417 U.S. at 342-347. Davis, however, 
dic not address whether a narrowing construction of a substantive 
criminal statute might support a constitutional claim on collateral 
attack as well. 


10 
the substantiality of petitioner's showing in this case, because it 
held that petitioner's reliance on Bailey failed to raise a 
constitutional claim. In our view, that basis for the court of 
appeals' decision is mistaken. 

2. Although we believe that a remand is required to 
reconsider whether petitioner can make a "substantial showing of 
the denial of a constitutional right," no remand is needed to 
reconsider the threshold issue of whether petitioner was required 
to obtain a certificate of appealability. The petition does not 
ask this Court to review whether the certificate-of -appealability 
requirement applies in this case. In Lindh v. Murphy, 117 S. Ct. 
2059 (1997), however, which was decided after the petition was 
filed, this Court concluded that Congress generally intended "to 
apply the [AEDPA]) amendments to chapter 153 only to such cases as 
were filed after the statute's enactment." Id. at 2063, see id. at 
2068. In so holding, the Court reasoned that the express provision 
that the Act's amendments to Chapter 154 of Title 28 "shall apply 
to cases pending," AEDPA § 107(c), implied that the Act's 
amendments to Chapter 153 of Title 28 should not apply to pending 
cases. Id. at 2063. 

The Act's provisicn requiring a certificate of appealability 
for federal prisoners is an amendment to Chapter 153 of Title 28; 
accordingly, under Lindh, that provision does not apply to "cases 
pending" on April 24, 1996, the effective date of the Act. The 
facts of Lindh, however, did not involve a petition for post- 


conviction relief in which the appeal was filed after the effective 


11 

date of the Act. See id. at 2061-2062. And the Court did not 
address whether, for purposes of the applicability of the 
certificate-of-appealability requirement, the "case" that has to be 
pending on the effective date of the Act is the "case" on appeal or 
the original "case" filed in the district court. In this instance, 
petitioner's motion for relief was filed in the district court 
before the effective date of the Act, but his case on appeal was 
filed after the effective date of the Act. We believe that such a 
case is subject to the Act's certificate of appealability 
requirement because the appeal -- the relevant case -- was neither 
"filed" nor "pending" on April 24, 1996.4 

In Tiedemann v. Benson, No. 96-3977, 1997 WL 437181 (August 6, 
1997), the Eighth Circuit recently agreed that a certificate of 
appealability is required whenever the notice of appeal was filed 
after the effective date of the Act, and that Lindh does not 
require otherwise. Tiedeman attempted to appeal from a district 
court order denying his petition for habeas corpus. Before the 
court of appeals, Tiedeman contended that Section 2253(c)(1)'s 
certificate-of-appealability requirement did not apply in his case 


because he had filed his petition for habeas corpus in the district 


4 The Court has denied certiorari in five cases in which 
courts of appeals applied the certificate of appealability 
requirement to cases (like this one) that were pending in the 
district court on the effective date of the Act, but in which a 
notice of appeal was not filed until after that date. Matthews v. 
United States, No. 96-8310, cert. denied, 117 S. Ct. 2485 (1997); 
Zarnes v. United States, No. 96-1324, cert. denied, 117 S. Ct. 1842 
(1997); Durham v. United States, No. 96-7329, cert. denied, 117 S. 
Ct. 1699 (1997); England v. United States, No. 96-6826, cert. 


denied, 117 S. Ct. 969 (1997); Travis v. United States, No. 96- 
5585, cert. denied, 117 S. Ct. 968 (1997). 


12 
court before the enactment of the AEDPA.* 1997 WL 437181 at *3. 
The court of appeals disagreed. It distinguished Lindh on the 
ground that the particular provision at issue in that case involved 
"Substantive standards for review of state-court judgments by 
habeas courts." JIbid. Moreover, the court continued, "we can 
think of no reason why a new provision exclusively directed towards 
appeal procedures would depend for its effective date on the filing 
of a case in a trial court, instead of on the filing of a notice of 
appeal or similar document." Ibid. Accordingly, the court of 
appeals concluded * at the certificate-of-appealability requirement 
applied to Tiedeman because his notice of appeal was filed after 
the Act's effective date. Ibid. Because the Eighth Circuit has 
already concluded that the certificate-of-appealability requirement 
applies where, as here, the notice of appeal was filed after the 
effective date of the Act, no remand is required for 
reconsideration in light of Lindh. 

We recognize that three courts of appeals have held, since 
Lindh, that the certificate-of-appealability requirement does not 
apply to petitions for post-conviction relief that were filed in 
the district court before the effective date of the Act. United 
States v. Carter, 117 F.3d 262, 264 (Sth Cir. 1997) (holding that 
"(f)ollowing Lindh, * * * § 2255 appellants are not subject to this 


[certificate-of-appealability] requirement unless their § 2255 


5 28 U.S.C. 2253(c)(1)(A) requires a certificate of 
appealability to appeal from "the final order in a habeas corpus 
proceeding in which the detention complained of arises out of 
~rocess issued by a State court." 


13 

petitions were filed in the district court after the AEDPA's 
effective date of April 24, 1996."); Nelson v. Walker, No. 96-2354, 
1997 WL 442686 (2d Cir. August 7, 1997) (holding that the 
requirement in 28 U.S.C. 2253(c)(3) (as amended) that the 
certificate of appealability identify which issue(s) merit appeal 
under Section 2253(c) (2) does not apply to an appeal from denial of 
a habeas petition filed before the Act's effective date); Arredondo 
v. United States, Nos. 96-2126/2583, 1997 WL 459716 (6th Cir. 
August 13, 1997) (holding that appellant whose Section 2255 motion 
was pending on the effective date of the Act is not required to 
obtain a certificate of appealability); but cf. Letter dated July 
17, 1997 from Leonard Green, Clerk, to United States, Handy 7 
United States, No. 96-2594 (6th Cir.) (directing the government to 
respond to a petition for rehearing en banc on the issue whether 
"§ 2255 petitioners are subject to the certificate of appealability 
requirement of the [AEDPA] if their § 2255 petitions were filed in 
the district court before April 24, 1996, when the Act became 
effective."). That disagreement, however, concerns an issue that 
is of rapidly diminishing importance, will affect the outcome in 
only a handful of cases, and is unlikely, in our view, to be 
determinative of the appeal in this case. Accordingly, this 
Court's review of the applicability of Lindh to a case in the 
procedural posture presented here is not warranted. 

3. Because this case should be remanded, this Court need not 
address any of the additional issues identified by petitioner. 


First, this Court need not consider at this juncture petitioner's 


14 
claim that the language in Section 2253(c)(2), requiring a 
"Substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right," 
should be read to permit appeals of statutory claims. Pet. 13-19. 


Second, petitioner's challenge to the district court's 


conclusion that petitioner had "waived" his claim by failing to. 


raise it on direct appeal is not ripe. The court of appeals did 
not address that holding in its order denying a certificate of 
appealability. The court of appeals may reach that claim on 
remand, however, because a defendant whose constitutional claim is 
procedurally barred cannot prevail on the merits or make a 
"substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right." 
Tucker v. Johnson, No. 97-20101, 1997 WL 367348 at *4 (Sth Cir. 
1997) (denying certificate of probable cause where petitioner 
cannot show that she can overcome her procedural default) .°® 
Third, certiorari is not appropriate to consider whether 
petitioner's conduct constituted a "carrying" under Section 924(c) 
or whether the jury instructions at his trial misstated the law. 


Pet. 29-39, 39-40. Those fact-bound issues were not considered by 


6 The district court based its waiver holding on a decision 
that was subsequently vacated and remanded by this Court, and in 
which the court of appeals granted relief on remand. See pp. 4-5, 
Supra. Moreover, the government recently filed a confession of 
error in this Court in Bousley v. Brooks, 97 F.3d 284 (8th Cir. 
1996), petition for cert. pending, No. 96-8516. In that case, the 
court of appeals held that a challenge under Bailey was barred by 
procedural default rules. In our view, the court of appeals erred 
by concluding its analysis without considering whether the 
defendant could make a sufficient showing of "actual innocence." 
Brief for the United States in Bousley v. Brooks, No. 96-8516, at 
9-11. On remand, the court of appeals would be free to consider 
those developments in reviewing the district court's waiver 
holding. 


15 
either the district court or the court of appeals, and they 
accordingly do not warrant review by this Court in the first 
instance. 
CONCLUSION 

The petition for a writ of certiorari should be granted, the 
judgment of the court of appeals vacated, and the case remanded to 
the court of appeals for further consideration in light of the 
position asserted in this brief. 


Respectfully submitted. 


WALTER DELLINGER

REPLY BRIEF FOR PETITIONER 


The parties in this case are now in agreement: petitioner’s claim that he is incarcerated 
for conduct that does not constitute a crime “may fairly be characterized as ‘constitutional,’ and 
* * * the court of appeals erred in concluding otherwise.” U.S. Br. 7. After confessing error 
on the primary point in dispute, however, the government suggests a remand to determine 
whether petitioner Hohn can make a “substantial showing” within the meaning of the Anti- 
Terrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act (AEDPA), 28 U.S.C. § 2253(c)(2), concerning his 
admittedly constitutional claim. For three reasons, a remand for that purpose is unnecessary and 
the Court should enter an order summarily reversing the decision below. 

First, with respect to one of the two constitutional grounds for relief advanced by Hohn, 
the government fails to provide any reason (and there is none) why the extensive showing 
already made in Hohn’s petition for certiorari might fail to qualify as “substantial.” Second, 
although the government mentions a theory under which claimants who advance arguments 
analogous to Hohn’s second constitutional challenge might fail to make the “substantial showing” 
required to obtain a certificate of appealability, it cannot bring itself to suggest that Hohn himself 
falls within this category (and he does not). And finally, after Hohn’s petition for certiorari was 
filed, this Court issued its decision in Lindh v. Murphy, 117 S. Ct. 2059, 2063 (1997), which 
conclusively establishes that AEDPA’s certificate-of-appealability requirement may not be 
applied retroactively to this case. For all of these reasons, this Court, in light of the 
government’s confession of error, should summarily reverse the Eighth Circuit’s decision and 


remand with instructions to the court of appeals to proceed to the merits of petitioner’s appeal. 


I 

As explained in the petition (at 33-34), Hohn is presently incarcerated for conduct that 
this Court unanimously held ia Bailey v. United States, 116 S. Ct. 501 (1995), is not a federal 
crime. The government admits that Hohn’s continued incarceration could violate the 
Constitution in at least two different ways. First, “[a] contention that, in light of Bailey, the 
evidence was insufficient to support a conviction under Section 924(c)(1) may state a claim under 
the Due Process Clause.” See U.S. Br. 8 (citing Jackson v. Virginia, 443 U.S. 307, 313-16 
(1979)). Second, “[a] contention that the jury instructions failed to require the jury to make 
findings on every essential element of the offense under Section 924(c), as construed in Bailey, 
may state a claim under the Fifth and Sixth Amendments.” U.S. Br. 8 (citing United States v. 
Gaudin, 115 S. Ct. 2310, 2320 (1995)). The government acknowledges that Hohn has “raised 
claims of both varieties.” Jbid. 

These concessions should definitively resolve in petitioner's favor the first question 
presented in the petition for certiorari. The government professes to harbor doubts, however, 
as to whether “[c]laims of the sort that petitioner raised * * * setisfy Section 2253(c)(2)'s 
requirement of a ‘substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right."” U.S. Br. 9 
(quoting 28 U.S.C. § 2253(c)(2)) (emphasis in government's brief). Of course, in order to 
justify a remand for consideration of the question of substantiality, the government would need 
to show that some doubt exists on the substantiality question with respect to both constitutional 
violations. The government, however, has not even attempted to make such a showing. 

A. This Court has recently reaffirmed the principle that “[{t}he Constitution gives a 


criminal defendant the right to have a jury determine, beyond a reasonable doubt, his guilt of 


every element of the crime with which he is charged.” United States v. Gaudin, 115 S. Ct. 
2310, 2320 (1995). As we have shown (Pet. 29-30 & n.21, 39-40), the jury instructions on the 
18 U.S.C. § 924(c) count at Hohn’s trial grossly misstated the law, and Hohn was accordingly 
deprived of his constitutional right to have a jury determine his guilt beyond a reasonable doubt 
of every element of that crime. The jury was charged that 
[t}he phrase “used a firearm” means having a firearm available to aid in the commission 
of possession of Methamphetamine with intent to distribute. Similarly, the phrase 
“carried a firearm” does not require proof of actual possession of a firearm or use of it 
in any affirmative manner, but does require proof beyond a reasonable doubt that the 
firearm was available to provide protection in connection with the possession of 
Methamphetamine with intent to distribute or facilitate success. 
Pet. App. 31a (emphasis added). The government itself conceded below (see Pet. 5, 30 n.21, 
34 n.24, 39; Pet. App. 23a) that this instruction allowed the jury to convict Hohn of “using a 
firearm” under a definition of that crime that is manifestly erroneous in light of Bailey. 
Although we contend that the jury instructions’ definition of “carrying a firearm” is 
similarly unsupportable (Pet. 39-40), it is important to bear in mind that Hohn’s constitutional 
rights would be violated even if the “carrying” instruction were entirely proper. As the Eighth 
Circuit, sitting en banc, has explained, “a verdict based on jury instructions that provided 
alternative bases for a finding, one of which is legally inadequate, is presumed to have rested 
on the insufficient basis.” United States v. Ryan, 41 F.3d 361, 365 (1994). Indeed, that is the 
holding of this Court's decisions in Yates v. United States, 354 U.S. 298, 312 (1957), and 
Stromberg v. California, 283 U.S. 359, 368 (1931). Not surprisingly, the government makes 
no attempt to dispute this controlling principle of law, or to suggest that it is inapplicable here. 
Thus, under established law it must be presumed that Hohn is currently incarcerated for “using 


a firearm” even though no jury has ever found beyond a reasonable doubt that his conduct 
violated 18 U.S.C. § 924(c), as construed by this Court in Bailey. 

The government concedes (U.S. Br. 8) that Hohn’s claim based on the erroneous jury 
instruction for “using a firearm” qualifies as a claim under the Fifth and Sixth Amendments. 
Significantly, though, the government offers not the slightest hint of a reason why Hohn has 
failed to make a “substantial showing” with respect to this constitutional claim. The reason is 
simple: Hohn’s unanswered and undisputed arguments in the petition for certiorari easily satisfy 
the “substantial showing” standard, which, as the government itself is constrained to admit (U.S. 
Br. 9), requires no more than a demonstration that the issues are “debatable among jurists of 
reason; that a court could resolve the issues [in a different manner]; or that the questions are 
adequate to deserve encouragement to proceed further.” Barefoot v. Estelle, 463 U.S. 880, 893 
n.4 (1983) (internal quotation marks omitted) (emphasis added). Hohn’s arguments based on 
the concededly erroneous “use” instruction and the Stromberg- Yates principle amount to much 
more: they conclusively demonstrate that Hohn is entitled to relief. 

Although this should be enough to warrant a summary reversal, there is more. The 
government also pointedly fails to suggest that Hohn has not made a “substantial showing” that 
the “carrying” instruction given at his trial was legally defective. Here again, the reason is 
straightforward: the petition for certiorari (Pet. 39-40) clearly demonstrates that the “carrying” 


instruction largely mirrored the “use” instruction and thus was invalid under Bailey. There 


being no dispute about the substantiality of Hohn’s showing concerning either of his Fifth and 
Sixth Amendment claims, summary reversal is warranted. ' 

B. As we previously explained (Pet. 34-39), there is a second, independent ground 
for Hohn’s constitutional challenge to his continued incarceration: the evidence presented at 
his trial was insufficient to support a conviction under Section 924(c)(1). The government 
acknowledges (U.S. Br. 8) that this shortcoming gives rise to a claim under the Due Process 
Clause. With respect to this constitutional challenge, the government does at least articulate a 
reason why — in the government's carefully chosen words — “[c]laims of the sort that petitioner 
raised do not automatically satisfy” the “substantial showing” requirement of Section 2253(c)(2). 
U.S. Br. 9 (emphasis added). The government states that “a claim under Section 924(c) that the 
evidence was insufficient to show a ‘use’ of a firearm in light of Bailey would not constitute a 
substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right absent an indication that the evidence 
was insufficient to show a ‘carrying.’” U.S. Br. 9. 

Whatever the truth of these generalizations, they have no bearing on this case. It may 
well be that “[c]laims of the sort that petitioner raised” concerning the sufficiency of evidence 
in his case “do not automatically satisfy” the “substantial showing” requirement. But the issue 
here is whether there is any reason to think that Hohn’s claim is not supported by a “substantial 
showing.” Tellingly, the government cannot even bring itself to say that Hohn’s sufficiency 


argument, as set forth in detail in our certiorari petition, is not “debatable among jurists of 


' The government's statement that the retroactivity of AEDPA’s certificate-of-appealability requirement 
is “unlikely * * * to be determinative of the appeal in this case” (U.S. Br. 13) is highly illuminating. 
It indicates that the government harbors no real doubt that Hohn will easily qualify for a certificate of 
appealability on remand, if he is required by this Court to secure one from the court of appeals. 


5 


reason” and does not present “questions [that] are adequate to deserve encouragement to proceed 
further.” Barefoot, 463 U.S. at 893 n.4. In the absence of even an assertion by the government 
that Hohn has failed to make a substantial showing concerning his sufficiency argument, there 
is no good reason to remand this issue to the court of appeals. 

The government's reluctance to assert that Hohn has failed to make such a “substantial 
showing” is unsurprising, as such an assertion would be wholly implausible. As we explained 
at length in the ~etition for certiorari (at 35-41), the evidence was woefully insufficient to show 
beyond a reasonable doubt that Hohn violated Section 924(c) by “carrying” a firearm “during” 
the predicate offense. Indeed, we are aware of no post-Bailey decision of any court of appeals 
under which the facts of this case would support a conviction for “carrying” a firearm under 
Section 924(c). 

Any doubt about the outcome in the Eighth Circuit, moreover, has been erased by that 
court’s recent decision in United States v. McKinney, 1997 U.S. App. LEXIS 17912 (8th Cir. 
July 16, 1997), on remand, 117 S. Ct. 1544 (1997).? In McKinney, the defendant had been 
convicted of using or carrying a firearm under Section 924(c) based on the following facts: 

McKinney kept several guns in his heavily fortified compound in a room from which he 

monitored video cameras that were placed strategically so as to allow him to observe 

anyone approaching his building. As the government puts it in its supplemental brief, 

“the four assault weapons from the control room formed the basis for defendant's 


conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)” because he was operating “an armed drug 
fortress” on the day that he was raided. 


? Not only does McKinney establish that there was insufficient evidence that Hohn “carried” a firearm, 
it also repudiates the Eighth Circuit's misguided waiver doctrine (upon which the district court relied in 
denying Hohn relief). See Pet. 20-27. Thus, after McKinney there can be no serious question both that 
Hohn is actually innocent of the federal crime for which he is currently serving time and that the district 
court’s reasons for denying him relief were legally erroneous. 


6 


1997 U.S. App. LEXIS 17912, at *3. The Eighth Circuit concluded that these facts did not 
support a conviction under Section 924(c) either for “using” or for “carrying” a firearm. In 
fact, the court of appeals stated that it was plain error for the trial court to submit the case to 
the jury on the “carrying” theory, because “[hjaving a gun available for use, even an immediate 
use, is simply not the equivalent of carrying it.” Jd. at *5; see also id. at *4-*5S (“In all of the 
cases in which we have upheld convictions for carrying guns during or in relation to a drug- 
trafficking offense, the defendant has had the weapon either in a car’s hatchback, in the 
passenger compartment of a car, or on his person, and was thus carrying the offending weapons 
in an obvious, literal way.”). If it was plain error for the district court to submit a “carrying” 
charge to the jury in McKinney, the same is true a fortiori in this case. 

Because Hohn has easily made a “substantial showing” with respect to his Due Process 
Clause claim concerning the insufficiency of evidence that he “carried” a firearm, a remand to 
the court of appeals for a resolution of that issue is unnecessary.’ For this reason as well, 


summary reversal is warranted.‘ 


* Of course, summary reversal on the appealability issue would not preclude the government from raising 
the “carrying” point at the merits stage of petitioner's appeal. 


* In urging the Court not to grant plenary review on the waiver issue presented in Hohn’s petition (Pet. 
21-30), the government states in passing that “a defendant whose constitutional claim is procedurally 
barred cannot prevail on the merits or make a substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right.” 
U.S. Br. 14 (internal quotation marks omitted). Here again, the government does not come right out 
and say that Hohn would fail to make a “substantial showing” with respect to his constitutional claims 
because those claims have been waived. In any event, that argument would be completely untenable 
because, as previously explained (at note 2), the Eighth Circuit in McKinney has repudiated the 
indefensible waiver doctrine on which the district court based its erroneous decision to deny Hohn relief. 


7 


Il 

After the petition for certiorari was filed, this Court issued its decision in Lindh v. 
Murphy, 117 S. Ct. 2059 (1997). Lindh held that AEDPA’s amendments to Chapter 154 of Title 
28 (which includes Section 2253(c), the provision requiring certificates of appealability) do not 
apply to cases that were pending on the date AEDPA was signed into law. 117 S. Ct. at 2063. 
The government acknowledges, as it must, that this is the clear holding of Lindh. See U.S. Br. 
10 (“[U}nder Lindh, that provision [requiring certificates of appealability] does not apply to 
‘cases pending’ on April 24, 1996, the effective date of the Act.”). The government further 
admits that “petitioner's Section 2255 motion was pending in the district court” on “April 24, 
1996.” U.S. Br. 5 (emphasis added). Rather than draw from these two concessions the 
inexorable conclusion that AEDPA has no application to this case, Louse, the government 
maintains that Lindh is not controlling and, even more remarkably, urges this Court to limit 
Hohn’s ability to argue otherwise on remand to the Eighth Circuit. The government's request 
for a limitation on any remand order should be rejected out of hand. Indeed, this Court's 
decision in Lindh provides yet another reason why summary reversal is appropriate. 

According to the government (U.S. Br. 10-11), this Court’s holding in Lindh that 
AEDPA'’s amendments to Chapter 154 of Title 28 apply only to “cases pending” on the date 
AEDPA was enacted is not controlling here because the appellate phase of the proceedings below 
qualifies as a different “case” from the trial phase. Since what the government calls the “case 
on appeal” was commenced only after the passage of AEDPA (with the filing by Hohn of a 
notice of appeal), it does not matter, says the government, that Hohn’s request for Section 2255 


relief was pending in the district court before the statute’s enactment. The government cites no 


authority to support the novel proposition that the appellate phase of litigation constitutes a 
separate “case,” and our research has uncovered none. Indeed, the only circuit that has adopted 
the government's position — the Eighth Circuit in Tiedman v. Benson, 1997 WL 437181 (Aug. 
6, 1997) — did so not because the appellate phase constitutes a different “case” but because it 
could “think of no reason why a new provision exclusively directed towards appeal procedures 
would depend for its effective date on the filing of a case in a trial court.” Jd. at *6. The 
dispositive “reason,” of course, is supplied by this Court’s decision in Lindh. Not surprisingly, 
at least six circuits have concluded that no certificate of appealability is necessary under Lindh 
if the Section 2255 petition was filed in the district court prior to AEDPA’s enactment. See 
United States v. Carter, 117 F.5d 262, 264 (Sth Cir. 1997); United States v. Gutierrez, 1997 
U.S. App. LEXIS 17430, at *2-*3 & n.1 (10th Cir. July 11, 1997) (unpublished opinion); 
Nelson v. Walker, 1997 WL 442686, at *14 (2d Cir. Aug. 7, 1997); Arredondo v. United States, 
1997 WL 459716, at *2 (6th Cir. Aug. 13, 1997); Koo v. McBride, 1997 WL 549557, at *1-*2 
& n.3 (7th Cir. Sept. 3, 1997); Hardwick v. Singletary, 1997 WL 555715, at *1-*2 (11th Cir. 
Sept. 4, 1997). 

To correct the Eighth Circuit’s obvious misreading of Lindh, and to bring it into line with 


the unanimous conclusion of other circuits, summary reversal is also warranted.° 


>’ At a minimum, if the Court decides to vacate and remand for a determination of substantiality, it should 
not preclude Hohn from raising the Lindh argument. Although the government is correct in observing 
that the petition for certiorari “does not ask this Court to review whether the certificate-of-appealability 
issue applied in this case” (U.S. Br. 10), it is clear that the retroactivity question, which is jurisdictional, 
arose only after the certiorari petition was filed when this Court issued its decision in Lindh. See also 
S. Ct. Rule 14.1(a) (issues “fairly included” in questions presented may be considered). 


9 


CONCLUSION 


The petition for a writ of certiorari should be granted, and the decision below summarily 
reversed and remanded with instructions to consider petitioner’s appeal on the merits. 


Respectfully submitted.

INTEREST OF AMICUS* 


The National Association of Criminal Defense Lawyers 
(NACDL) is a non-profit corporation with a membership of 
more than 9,000 attorneys and 28,000 affiliate members in 50 
states. The American Bar Association recognizes NACDL as 
an affiliate organization and awards it full representation in its 
House of Delegates. 


NACDL was founded over 35 years ago to promote 
study and research in the field of criminal law, to disseminate 
and advance knowledge of the law in the area of criminal 
practice; and to encourage the integrity, independence, and 
expertise of defense lawyers in criminal cases. NACDL seeks 
to defend individual liberties guaranteed by the Bill of Rights 
and has a keen interest in the correctness and fairness of 
criminal convictions. Among NACDL’s objectives are to 
promote the proper administration of justice and to ensure that 
Statutes are construed and applied in accordance with due 
process. 


Letters from the parties to this case expressing their 
consent to the filing of this brief are on file with the clerk. 


*Counsel for neither party authored this amicus curiae 
brief, in whole or in part. No person or entity, other than the 
amicus curiae, has made a monetary contribution to the 
preparation or submission of this brief. 


QUESTIONS PRESENTED 


1. Whether a federal prisoner who demonstrates that he 
is incarcerated for conduct that this Court conclusively 
determined does not constitute a federal crime has made “a 
substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right” 
warranting issuance of a certificate of appealability under 28 
U.S.C. §2255. 


3. Whether a defendant who possesses a firearm and 
drugs in close proximity to each other, but neither bears the 
firearm on his person, nor otherwise transports it, commits the 
crime under 18 U.S.C. §924(c) of “carr[ying]” a firearm 
“during and in relation to” the predicate offense of possession 
of the drugs with intent to distribute them. 


STATEMENT OF THE CASE 
circuit judgment this Court is asked to review. 
ARGUMENT 


This case is certworthy not only for the reasons the 
petitioner ably asserts, but for two other reasons as well. First, 


2 . 


the circuit court’s construction of the adjective “constitutional” 
in §2253(c)\(2), if accepted, would undermine the correct 
interpretation of other provisions in the AEDPA and, in the 
end, would frustrate an accurate, sensible, coherent 
interpretation of the Act as a whole. Second, the circuit court’s 


Unfortunately, the circuit court below acted too quickly 
in a pro se case, looked only at a single term in a single 
provision of the new Act, jumped to an unwarranted conclusion 
regarding its meaning in the abstract, and made a serious 
mistake. That error should be corrected before it does further 
damage. 


Since Congress enacted the AEDPA just over a year 
ago, the lower courts have wrestled with the meaning of the 
adjective “constitutional” —as that modifier is used by the Act 
in a variety of contexts. The pressure has mounted in recent 
weeks, as prisoners have rushed to meet the Act’s new filing 
deadlines. Widespread difficulty and injustice will result if the 
system goes much longer without this Court’s authoritative 
guidance on what is, in fact, a threshold question in many 
§2255 and habeas corpus cases. Not only petitioners’ 
entitlement to appellate review, but many other crucial matters 
are at stake: the computation of filing deadlines and 
petitioners’ right to evidentiary hearings and stays of 
execution. 


L THE CIRCUIT COURT’S CONSTRUCTION OF 
§2253(c\2) CANNOT BE RECONCILED WITH OTHER 
PROVISIONS OF THE AEDPA AND THE ACT AS A 
WHOLE 


Other key provisions of the Act also employ the 
adjective “constitutional.” If that term is understood in those 


instances to mean what the circuit court misconstrues it to 
mean in §2253(c)(2), the various working parts of the full Act 
cannot function, individually or together in an integrated 
system. 


According to unamended language in §2255, a federal 
prisoner is entitled to seek relief on the ground that he or she is 
in custody under a sentence that was imposed in violation of 
the Constitution “or laws” of the United States, a sentence “in 
excess of the maximum allowed by law,” or a sentence 
“otherwise subject to collateral attack.” And according to 
similarly unamended text in §2241(c)(3) and §2254(a), a state 
prisoner is also entitled to seek habeas corpus relief on the 
ground that he or she is in custody in violation of the “laws” of 
the United States. Clearly, then, the new Act makes no attempt 
to banish federal nonconstitutional claims from the purview of 
federal collateral remedies.’ 


By the circuit court’s account, however, the Act draws 
a clear constitutional/nonconstitutional distinction to control 
the way in which cases are processed after they enter the 
federal courthouse door. That position is untenable. The Act 
uses the “constitutional” designation in some contexts in which 
it cannot possibly mean to exclude nonconstitutional matters. 
Thus the Act plainly uses the modifier “constitutional” 
interchangeably with “federal” or, perhaps, as a synonym for 
“fundamental” or “cognizable.” 


Under a new provision in the Act, §2244(d)(1)(C), the 


: See Hill v. United States, 368 U.S. 424, 428 (1962) (explaining that 
fundamental nonconstitutional defects will suffice in 2255 cases), Reed v. 
Farley, 512 U.S. 339 (1994) (confirming that state prisoners can obtain 
habeas relief on a showing of serious violations of federal nonconstitutional 
law). 


asserted was initially recognized.” If the circuit court is correct 
in its construction of the adjective “constitutional” in 


an event that never occurs in some cases (i.e., cases in which 
en or pa ~ahy ‘ aaniin 

). That, however, i i 
§2244(d)(1)(C) cannot sensibly bear. ms = 


_ Like §2244(d)(1)(C), §2255 (as amended by the Act 
establishes a fling period for federal prisoners, which runs ip 
some cases from the date on which a “right” is “newly 
recognized.” In that instance, §2255 does not use the modifier 
constitutional.” If the appearance of the “constitutional” 
modifier in §2244(d)(1)(C) excludes nonconstitutional claims 
by implication, it follows that the omission of the same term 
from §2255 includes nonconstitutional claims. The result is 
that the filing period in §2255 cases must be geared to the 
establishment of nonconstitutional principles, while the parallel! 
deadline for habeas petitions demands a purely constitutional 
footing. Those two provisions cannot sensibly be read to 
establish such an obvious inconsistency. 


Another new provision, §2254(e), governs the federal 
courts’ authority to conduct evidentiary hearings with respect 
to habeas petitions by state prisoners. Under subparagraphs 
(AX(i) and (B) of §2254(e)(2), prisoners who failed to develop 
the facts in state court can obtain a federal hearing to pursue 
constitutional” matters—either claims that rely on new rules 
of “constitutional” law, or evidence that would have convinced 


the jury to acquit,but for “constitutional” error. 


If the circuit court’s interpretation of the 
“constitutional” adjective in §2253(c)(2) is correct, it must 
follow that the same construction must be placed on that 
identical term in §2254(e)(2). That means, however, that state 
habeas corpus petitioners who advance perfectly cognizable 
nonconstitutional claims for relief (but failed to develop the 
relevant facts in state court) are out of luck in federal 
court—no matter how “new” a claim may be, no matter how 
difficult it would have been to discover its factual predicate 
when the case was before the state courts, and, indeed, no 
matter how closely the claim is linked to innocence. This, on 
the inconsistent theory that only purely “constitutional” claims 
need apply for fact-finding in federal court. That cannot be 
what §2254(e) means. 


Any such reading of §2254(e) conflicts with settled law 
governing hearings in §2255 cases. The new Act's 
amendments to §2255 do not address hearings and thus leave 
previous law in this respect intact. Of course, federal district 
courts can conduct fact-finding hearings regarding 
nonconstitutional claims raised by federal prisoners. 


Forbidding evidentiary hearings in habeas cases 
presenting nonconstitutional claims would also conflict with 
related provisions in Chapter 154 of the Act, which governs 
death penalty cases in qualifying states. Under §2264(b) of 
that new chapter, federal courts are expressly instructed to 
adjudicate prisoners’ claims “subject to” §2254(e). Plainly, 
then, §2254(e) can bear only a construction that can be 
reconciled, in turn, with the provisions in Chapter 154. 


Under §2262(b)(3), prisoners can sustain a stay of 
execution if they make a substantial showing of the denial of “a 


Federal right.” Here again, if the appearance of the adjective 
“constitutional” in §2253(c)(2) precludes nonconstitutional 
claims, it must follow that the omission of that modifer from 
§2262(b) includes nonconstitutional claims. That means, 
however, that §2262(b) permits a prisoner to preserve a stay of 
execution on the strength of a nonconstitutional claim, even as 


a sister provision of the same Act, §2254(e), systematically 
refuses claims of that nature a federal evidentiary hearing. 


Under §2264(a)(2), prisoners who are subject to 
Chapter 154 can advance a claim based on a “new Federal 
right” (not necessarily “constitutional” in nature)}—despite 
procedural default with respect to that claim in state court. 
And, under §2264(a)(3), prisoners can press newly discovered 
facts related to any “claim” (again, not necessarily 
“constitutional” in character). By the circuit court’s account of 
the adjective “constitutional,” however, nonconstitutional 
claims are systematically denied federal evidentiary hearings. 
If that view is accepted, it must follow that §2264 excuses a 
prisoner's default with respect to a nonconstitutional claim and, 
indeed, instructs a federal habeas court to adjudicate that claim 
on the merits, but (at the same time and by virtue of the same 
Act), §2254(e) bars the court from taking evidence regarding 
the claim. That cannot be what §2264(a) means. 


Finally, under §2244(b)(2)(A), state prisoners can file 
a second or successive petition in some instances, provided 
Og ee “new tule” of “constitutional” law. Similarly, 
under §2255, federal prisoners, too, can press “new rule” 
claims described as “constitutional.” By the circuit court’s 


claims—no matter how “new” they may be. That cannot be 


what §2244(b)(2)(A) and §2255 mean.” 


Consider in this connection that, with respect to 
innocence-related claims, the successive petition provision in 
§2255 omits any reference to the “constitutional” adjective. So 
prisoners who press a nonconstitutional claim are entitled to 
procesd—provided they link that kind of claim to innocence in 
the manner prescribed.’ It is inconceivable, then, that in the 
same breath §2255 flatly bars successive petitions advancing 
“new rule” claims that are nonconstitutional in nature. 


Since prisoners are now filing successive habeas 
petitions and §2255 motions that are controlled by the new Act, 
this is where the erroneous case law is developing most 


. Together, §2262(b)(3) and (c) have it that if a prisoner's claim is 
rejected, no other federal court can thereafter issue a stay—unless the 
prisoner meets the additional standards for filing a second or successive 
petition established by §2244(b). _iIf, however, §2244(b) unilaterally 
excludes nonconstitutional claims, §2262 must mean that a stay can remain 
in place on the basis of a nonconstitutional claim, even though another court 
would be barred from issuing a new stay unless something purely 
“constitutional” is offered. That cannot be what §2262 means. 


: This is scarcely surprising, a nonconstitutional claim touching 
innocence is an excellent candidate for a second or successive §2255 
motion. Yet by allowing such a claim to be advanced im a successive 

motion, §2255 rejects any clean constitutional/nonconstitutional basis of 
dntinntion. 


Of course, the AEDPA does establish restrictions on multiple 
collateral applications. The point is that those restrictions go not to the 
substantive claims that can be advanced, but rather to the abusive manner 
in which some prisoners attempt to proceed. They bar not federal claims of 
a particular (nonconstitutional) nature, but federal claims of any nature—if 
those claims are pressed in needlessly repetitive collateral actions. 


rapidly—much of it explicitly in reliance on the Eighth 
Circuit’s misconstruction of the modifier “constitutional” in 


this case.‘ 


To sum up, the circuit’s interpretation of the adjective 
“constitutional” ascribes to the AEDPA a purpose to create a 
series of arbitrary, Byzantine snarls in the federal courts’ 
handling of claims that plainly are cognizable in collateral 
proceedings. By the circuit’s account, a petitioner who 
advances a purely “constitutional” claim is free to file a 
collateral petition, so long as he meets one of the filing 
deadlines established by the Act, and is equally entitled to 
federal fact-finding, a stay of execution, and (if necessary) 
appellate review—provided he meets the standards prescribed 
for those ordinary tools of effective adjudication. 


By contrast, according to the circuit, a petitioner who 
advances a nonconstitutional c/aim faces an entirely different 
landscape. He is free to file a petition, but he must meet a 
filing deadline that takes no account of how recently his claim 
materialized. This is so not because anyone thinks the 
petitioner should have anticipated a “new” nonconstitutional 
claim, but only because his claim is, by nature, 
nonconstitutional. If the facts supporting his claim were not 
developed in state court, the petitioner cannot obtain a federal 
hearing even if he meets the standards the Act establishes for 
determining whether procedural default in state court should be 
excused. This is so not because anyone thinks that the 
petitioner could have developed the facts previously, but only 


. E.g., Coleman v. United States, 106 F.3d 339 (10th Cir. 1997), 
United States v. Lorentsen, 106 F.3d 278 (9th Cir. 1997), In re Blackshire, 
98 F.3d 1293 (11th Cir. 1996); Nunez v. United States, 96 F.3d 990 (7th 


Cir. 1996). 


because his claim is nonconstitutional in character. If the 
petitioner needs a stay of execution, he may get one. But the 
district court may be unable to hold a hearing to determine his 
claim responsibly—again, only because the claim falls in the 
nonconstitutional category. Finally, of course, if the district 
court rules unfavorably, the petitioner is barred from seeking 
appellate review—again, not because anyone has concluded 
that his claim is frivolous and does not warrant appellate 
consideration, but because it is nonconstitutional in nature. 


Such an internally inconsistent maze of arbitrary traps 
is nothing to ascribe to the United States Congress or a new 
statute meant to bring good sense, order, and efficiency to the 
system. The AEDPA may not be perfect. But it is not Catch 
22. 


The only sensible construction that can be placed on the 
Act is that it employs the adjective “constitutional” to denote 
matters that are cognizable in federal collateral 
proceedings—not formalistically to foreclose matters that are 
cognizable, but are nonetheless nonconstitutional in character. 
The reason is plain enough. The primary purpose of the Act is 
to make federal habeas litigation more efficient, virtually all 
the claims raised by state prisoners are constitutional in nature, 
and the Act simply employs that designation because it is 
usually accurate. 


IL THE CIRCUIT’S CONSTRUCTION OF 
§2253(c)(2) RAISES SERIOUS CONSTITUTIONAL 


QUESTIONS 


Just as it makes sense to construe the AEDPA in a way 
that avoids irrational, freakish results, it equally makes sense 
to read the new Act in a way that avoids serious constitutional 
questions under Article Il. Yet the circuit court’s 


10 


interpretation bristles with constitutional difficulty. By the 
circuit court’s account of §2253(c)(2), all prisoners, state and 
federal, can press nonconstitutional claims at the district level, 
but not higher. District court judgments on nonconstitutional 
federal claims are, then, effectively final in some instances. 


Making a single district judge the sole arbiter of 
nonconstitutional federal claims would not only be novel. It 
would invite serious constitutional objections. Appellate 
review at the circuit level and, indeed, in this Court, serves 
both to correct errors below and to ensure a uniform 
interpretation of federal law. If, in habeas and §2255 cases 
invulnerable to review, both those values are sacrificed. In the 
case of §2255 motions, the claims at stake were not typically 
available at trial or on appeal, but are entirely 
fresh—cognizable for the first time in collateral proceedings. 
Making district court judgments final with respect to claims of 
that kind places the responsibility of giving federal law its 
authoritative interpretation in the hands of a single individual.* 


Moreover, the arrangement the circuit court ascribes to 
§2253(c)(2) is not outcome-neutral. For §2253 does not 
require the respondent (i.e., the state or the federal government) 
to obtain a certificate before appealing a judgment in the 
prisoner's favor. If §2253 permits government appeals in cases 


’ This Court might still reach down for a case by some extraordinary 
means—perhaps its jurisdiction to entertain habeas petitions as an 
“original” matter, see Felker v. Turpin, 116 S.Ct. 2333 (1996), or its 
authority under the All-Writs Act to issue the common law writ of certiorari. 
See Davis v. Jacobs, 454 U.S. 911 (1981). Yet the formal availability of 
that kind of avenue in an extraordinary case scarcely defuses the problems 
ee 
cases. 


11 


involving nonconstitutional claims, but precludes prisoner- 
initiated appeals, this newly amended statute skews the results 
that Article III courts can reach on federal issues. Errors that 
favor prisoners are subject to review and may be corrected, but 
errors that favor the government are insulated from review and 
may stand. That kind of legislative manipulation of judicial 
outcomes has been invalid for over a century.° 


Nothing of this sort occurred prior to the circuit court’s 
extraordinary construction of the AEDPA’s amendment to 
§2253. Before the amendment, disappointed habeas 
petitioners proceeding under §2254, but not governmental 
respondents, were required to obtain a certificate as a condition 
for appellate review. Yet at that time §2253 did not employ the 
“constitutional” adjective and thus established only a merits- 
related screening test to ensure that prisoners did not advance 
frivolous claims (of any cognizable nature) on appeal. That 
regime was one-sided, to be sure, but it insulated the appellate 


courts from meritless claims—not an entire category of — 


cognizable claims, irrespective of their merit. Gatekeeping 
devices that detect worthless claims are familiar and perfectly 


valid. The arrangement the circuit court’s judgment here 
reflects is not of that order. 


It is one thing to require prisoners to show some 
baseline substance to their appellate claims before allowing 
them to proceed, but to assume that state officials will police 
themselves. It is quite another to refuse to allow prisoners to 
advance plainly meritorious claims merely because those 


. United States v. Klein, 80 U.S. (13 Wall.) 128 (1871). See United 
States v. Sioux Nation of Indians, 448 U.S. 371, 404 (1980)(explaining that 
Klein faulted a statute that forced this Court to decide a case in the 


government's favor).

claims are nonconstitutional in nature, but to open the federal 
appellate courts’ doors in the same kind of case—if the warden, 
rather than a prisoner in gaol, comes knocking. Yet that is 


The circuit court below held that certificates cannot be 
issued with respect to nonconstitutional claims. Other courts 
have indicated agreement, yet without significant discussion 
and certainly without explicit attention *o the constitutional 
issues.’ Still other courts have hesitated to follow 
suit—sensing, perhaps, the serious Article [II problems that 
such a construction of the Act entails.* 

The situation thus invites this Court’s attention, lest 
inconsistent judgments build up in short order, many of them 
erroneous, raising even more (equally difficult) problems to 
resolve. A new statute like the AEDPA may require time for 
the bugs to be sorted out. In this instance, however, the lower 
courts will flounder over fundamental constitutional issues at 
the very threshold of many cases—unless and until they receive 
this Court’s guidance.

CONCLUSION 


For the reasons given above, the Court should grant the 
petitioner’s petition for review and should reverse the circuit 
court’s judgment.

QUESTIONS PRESENTED 


1. Whether a federal prisoner who demonstrates 
that he is incarcerated for conduct that this Court con- 
clusively determined does not constitute a federal crime 
has made “a substantial showing of the denial of a consti- 
tutional right” warranting issuance of a certificate of 
appealability under 28 U.S.C. § 2255. 


2. Whether a defendant's failure to raise an argu- 
ment on direct appeal, when that argument was fore- 
closed by binding circuit precedent and had been 
uniformly rejected by other circuits, constitutes a know- 
ing and intelligent “waiver,” precluding any showing of 
“cause and prejudice” or other excuse for the procedural 
default in a collateral review proceeding. 


3. Whether a defendant who possesses a firearm 
and drugs in close proximity to each other, but neither 
bears the firearm on his person, nor otherwise transports 
it, commits the crime under 18 U.S.C. § 924(c) of 
“carr[ying]” a firearm “during and in relation to” the 
predicate offense of possession of the drugs with intent to 
distribute them.
