18 U.S.C. § 924. Penalties. 


** * 

(c)(1) Whoever, during and in relation to any crime of 
violence or drug trafficking crime (including a crime of 
violence or drug trafficking crime which provides for an 
enhanced punishment if committed by the use of a deadly or 
dangerous weapon or device) for which he may be prosecuted 
in a court of the United States, uses or carries a firearm, 
shall, in addition to the punishment provided for such crime 
of violence or drug trafficking crime, be sentenced to 
imprisonment for five years, and if the firearm is a 
short-barreled rifle, short-barreled shotgun, or semiautomatic 
assault weapon, to imprisonment for ten years, and if the 
firearm is a machinegun, or a destructive device, or is 
equipped with a firearm silencer or firearm muffler, to 
imprisonment for thirty years. In the case of his second or 
subsequent conviction under this subsection, such person shall 
be sentenced to imprisonment for twenty years, and if the 
firearm is a machinegun, or a destructive device, or is 
equipped with a firearm silencer or firearm muffler, to life 
imprisonment without release. Notwithstanding any other 
provision of law, the court shall not place on probation or 
suspend the sentence of any person convicted of a violation 
of this subsection, nor shall the term of imprisonment 
imposed under this subsection run concurrently with any other 
term of imprisonment including that imposed for the crime 
of violence or drug trafficking crime in which the firearm 
was used or carried. 


(2) For purposes of this subsection, the term "drug 
trafficking crime" means any felony punishable under the 
Controlled Substances Act (21 U.S.C. 801 et seq.), the 
Controlled Substances Import and Export Act (21 U.S.C. 951 
et seq.), or the Maritime Drug Law Enforcement Act (46 
U.S.C. App. 1901 et seq.). 


(3) For purposes of this subsection the term "crime of 
violence” means an offense that is a felony and-- 


(A) has as an element the use, attempted use, or 
threatened use of physical force against the person or property 
of another, or 


; (B) that by its nature, involves a substantial risk that 
physical force against the person or property of another may 
be used in the course of committing the offense. 


28 U.S.C. § 1254. Courts of appeals; certiorari; 
certified questions. 


Cases in the courts of appeals may be reviewed by the 
Supreme Court by the following methods: 


(1) By writ of certiorari granted upon the petition of 
any party to any civil or criminal case, before or after 
rendition of judgment or decree; 


(2) By certification at any time by a court of appeals 
of any question of law in any civil or criminal case as to 
which instructions are desired, and upon such certification the 
Supreme Court may give binding instructions or require the 
entire record to be sent up for decision of the entire matter in 
controversy. 


28 U.S.C. § 1651. Writs. 


(a) The Supreme Court and all courts established by 
Act of Congress may issue all writs necessary or appropriate 
in aid of their respective jurisdictions and agreeable to the 
usages and principles of law. 


(b) An alternative writ or rule nisi may be issued by 
a justice or judge of a court which has jurisdiction. 


28 U.S.C. § 2244. Finality of determination. 


(a) No circuit or district judge shall be required to 
entertain an application for a writ of habeas corpus to inquire 
into the detention of a person pursuant to a judgment of a 
court of the United States if it appears that the legality of 
such detention has been determined by a judge or court of the 
United States on a prior application for a writ of habeas 
corpus, except as provided in section 2255. 


(b)(1) A claim presented in a second or successive 
habeas corpus application under section 2254 that was 
presented in a prior application shall be dismissed. 


(2) A claim presented in a second or successive 
habeas corpus application under section 2254 that was not 
presented in a prior application shall be dismissed unless-- 


(A) the applicant shows that the claim relies on 
a new rule of constitutional law, made 
retroactive to cases on collateral review by the 
Supreme Court, that was previously 
unavailable; or 


(B)(i) the factual predicate for the claim could 
not have been discovered previously through 
the exercise of due diligence; and 


(ii) the facts underlying the claim, if proven 
and viewed in light of the evidence as a whole, 
would be sufficient to establish by clear and 
error, no reasonable factfinder would have 
found the applicant guilty of the underlying 


offense. 


(3)(A) Before a second or successive application 
permitted by this section is filed in the district court, the 
applicant shall move in the appropriate court of appeals for an 
order authorizing the district court to consider the 


(B) A motion in the court of appeals for an 

order authorizing the district court to consider a second or 

inn eunfiention sheth tn a ined by a three-jud 
panel of the court of appeals. 


(C) The court of appeals may authorize the 
filing of a second or successive application only if it 
determines that the application makes a prima facie showing 
that the application satisfies the requirements of this 


(D) The court of appeals shall grant or deny 
the authorization to file a second or successive application not 
later than 30 days after the filing of the motion. 


(E) The grant or denial of an authorization by 
a court of appeals to file a second or successive application 
shall not be appealable and shall not be the subject of a 
petition for rehearing or for a writ of certiorari. 


(4) A district court shall dismiss any claim 
presented in a second or successive application that the court 
of appeals has authorized to be filed unless the applicant 
shows that the claim satisfies the requirements of this section. 


(c) In a habeas corpus proceeding brought in behalf 
of a person in custody pursuant to the judgment of a State 
court, a prior judgment of the Supreme Court of the United 
States on an appeal or review by a writ of certiorari at the 
instance of the prisoner of the decision of such State court, 
shall be conclusive as to all issues of fact or law with respect 
to an asserted denial of a Federal right which constitutes 
ground for discharge in a habeas corpus proceeding, actually 
adjudicated by the Supreme Court therein, unless the 
applicant for the writ of habeas corpus shall plead and the 
court shall find the existence of a material and controlling fact 
which did not appear in the record of the proceeding in the 
Supreme Court and the court shall further find that the 
applicant for the writ of habeas corpus could not have caused 
such fact to appear in such record by the exercise of 


(d)(1) A 1-year period of limitation shall apply to an 
application for a writ of habeas corpus by a person in custody 
pursuant to the judgment of a State court. The limitation 


period shall run from the latest of-- 


Neen ee —_ 


(A) the date on which the judgment became 
final by the conclusion of direct review or the 
expiration of the time for seeking such review; 


(B) the date on which the impediment to 
filing an application created by State action in 
violation of the Constitution or laws of the 
United States is removed, if the applicant was 
prevented from filing by such State action; 


(C) the date on which the constitutional 
right asserted was initially recognized by the 
Supreme Court, if the right has been newly 
recognized by the Supreme Court and made 
retroactively applicable to cases on collateral 
review; or 


(D) the date on which the factual predicate 
of the claim or claims presented could have 
been discovered through the exercise of due 


(2) The time during which a properly filed 
application for State post-conviction or other collateral 
review with respect to the pertinent judgment or claim 
is pending shall not be counted toward any period of 
limitation under this subsection. 


28 U.S.C.§ 2253. Appeal. 


(a) In a habeas corpus proceeding or a proceeding under 
section 2255 before a district judge, the final order shall be 
subject to review, on appeal, by the court of appeals for the 
circuit in which the proceeding is held. 


(b) There shall be no right of appeal from a final order 
in a proceeding to test the validity of a warrant to remove to 
another district or place for commitment or trial a person 
charged with a criminal offense against the United States, or 
to test the validity of such person’s detention pending removal 
proceedings. 

(c)(1) Unless a circuit justice or judge issues a certificate 


of appealability, an appeal may not be taken to the court of 
appeals from-- 


(A) the final order in a habeas corpus 


cmalaind trades dentin equabieed 
of arises out of process issued by a State court; 
or 


(B) the final order in a proceeding under 
section 2255. 


(2) A certificate of appealability may issue under 
paragraph (1) only if the applicant has made a substantial 
showing of the denial of a constitutional right. 

(3) The certificate of appealability under paragraph (1) 
shall indicate which specific issue or issues satisfy the 
showing required by paragraph (2).

QUESTION PRESENTED 


This Court granted certiorari limited to the fol- 
lowing question: 


In light of the fact that the Court of Appeals de- 
nied the petitioner’s request for a Certificate of 
Appealability, does this Court have jurisdiction to 
grant certiorari, vacate, and remand this case per 
the suggestion of the Solicitor General?

BRIEF FOR THE UNITED STATES 


OPINION BELOW 


The opinion of the court of appeals denying 
petitioner’s application for a certificate of appealabil- 
ity (J.A. 41-48) is reported at 99 F.3d 892. 


JURISDICTION 


The judgment of the court of appeals was entered on 
November 4, 1996. A petition for rehearing was de- 
nied on February 10, 1997. The petition for a writ of 
certiorari was filed on May 12, 1997, and invoked this 
Court’s jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. 1254(1). The pe- 
tition for a writ of certiorari was granted on October 
31, 1997, limited to the following question: “In light of 
the fact that the Court of Appeals denied the peti- 


(1) 


2 


tioner’s request for a Certificate of Appealability, 
does this Court have jurisdiction to grant certiorari, 
vacate, and remand this case per the suggestion of the 
* * * Solicitor General?” (118 S. Ct. 361). The juris- 
diction of this Court rests on either 28 U.S.C. 1254(1) 
or 28 U.S.C. 1651(a). 


STATUTORY PROVISIONS AND RULES INVOLVED 


Relevant statutory provisions, Federal Rules and 
Supreme Court Rules are reproduced at App., infra, 
la-8a. 


STATEMENT 


1. On June 20, 1990, police officers executed a 
search warrant at petitioner’s house. The police re- 
covered approximately 16 grams of methamphetamine, 
most of which was in a brown case on the kitchen 
counter. Within arm’s reach of the drugs on the 
kitchen counter, police found three loaded firearms, 
two of them in holsters. During the search, the police 
also found a grinder bearing methamphetamine resi- 
due, three other firearms, one of which was loaded, 
and $293 in currency. J.A. 34, 43; Gov’t C.A. Br. in 
Opp. to Pet. for Rehearing 2. Following a jury trial in 
the United States District Court for the District of 
Nebraska, petitioner was convicted of, inter alia, 
using and carrying a firearm during and in relation to 
a drug trafficking offense, in violation of 18 U.S.C. 
924(c)(1). J.A. 27. Petitioner appealed but did not 
challenge the sufficiency of the evidence to support 
his conviction under Section 924(c)(1). The court of 
appeals affirmed. J.A. 12-26. 

2.a. On December 6, 1995, this Court held in Bai- 
ley v. United States, 516 U.S. 137, that a conviction 
for use of a firearm under Section 924(c)(1) requires 
the government to “show active employment of 


3 


the firearm.” Jd. at 144. Active employment includes 
uses such as “brandishing, displaying, bartering, 
striking with, and most obviously, firing or attempt- 
ing to fire” the weapon. /d. at 148. By contrast, mere 
possession of a firearm does not constitute “use.” Jd. 
at 149. 

b. Following the decision in Bailey, petitioner 
filed a pro se motion seeking to vacate his sentence 
under 28 U.S.C. 2255 on two grounds. First, he con- 
tended that the evidence at his trial was insufficient 
under Bailey to support his conviction under Section 
924(c)(1). Petitioner contended that his failure to 
raise the insufficiency claim on direct appeal should 
be excused because his conduct “was not a crime,” and 
it would therefore be a “fundamental miscarriage of 
justice” not to review his claim. Motion to Vacate, 
Set Aside or Correct Sentence Pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 
§ 2255, at 8. Second, petitioner challenged the jury 
instructions at his trial for improperly defining the 
phrase “use a firearm” and argued that correct jury 
instructions would likely have produced an acquittal. 
Id. at 11. The government responded that petitioner 
was not entitled to relief because the evidence at trial 
was sufficient to show that he had “carried” a fire- 
arm. Noting that the police found weapons in holsters 
next to large amounts of methamphetamine on the 
counter in petitioner’s kitchen, the government ar- 
gued that the jury could infer that petitioner had 
“entered his residence and placed his belongings, 
which included the methamphetamine and firearms, 
onto the counter.” J.A. 34-35. The government ac- 
knowledged that, under Bailey, the jury instructions 
at petitioner’s trial erroneously defined the word 
“use,” but contended that the error was harmless 
because “[tJhe evidence obviously establishes ‘carry- 


4 


ing.’” J.A. 35. The district court held that petitioner 
had “waived” his Bailey claim by failing to raise it on 
direct appeal. J.A. 37-38." 

3. a. On April 24, 1996, while petitioner’s Section 
2255 motion was pending in the district court, the 
President signed into law the Anti-Terrorism and 
Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996 (AEDPA), Pub. 
L. No. 104-132, 110 Stat. 1214. Title I of the AEDPA 
made changes to the law governing collateral attacks 
for federal prisoners making Section 2255 motions 
and state prisoners seeking habeas corpus relief 
under 28 U.S.C. 2254. Before the passage of the Act, a 
state prisoner was required to secure a certificate of 
probable cause authorizing an appeal from the district 
court’s denial of his habeas petition, but no similar 
requirement was imposed on a federal prisoner seek- 
ing to appeal the denial of his Section 2255 motion. 
See 28 U.S.C. 2253 (1994). Section 102 of the AEDPA 
amended Section 2253 to require both federal and 
state prisoners to obtain a “certificate of appealabil- 
ity” as a condition of appealing. The new provision 
states that, “[uJnless a circuit justice or judge issues 
a certificate of appealability, an appeal may not be 
taken to the court of appeals from * * * (A) the final 


! The court relied on United States v. McKinney, 79 F.3d 
105, 109 (1996), in which the Eighth Circuit held that a defen- 
dant who had not raised a Bailey claim at trial or in his initial 
brief on direct appeal had waived it. This Court subsequently 
vacated and remanded McKinney for further consideration in 
light of Johnson v. United States, 117 S. Ct. 1544 (1997). 
McKinney v. United States, 117 S. Ct. 1816 (1997). On remand 
in McKinney, the Eighth Circuit applied plain error analysis 
rather than the waiver theory on which the district court in 
this case had relied. United States v. McKinney, 120 F.3d 132 
(1997). 


5 


order in a habeas corpus proceeding in which the 
detention complained of arises out of process issued 
by a State court; or (B) the final order in a proceed- 
ing under section 2255.” 110 Stat. 1217, 28 U.S.C. 
2253(c)(1) (Supp. II 1996). A certificate of appealabil- 
ity may be issued “only if the applicant has made a 
substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional 
right.” Jd. at 1218, 28 U.S.C. 2253(c)(2) (Supp. II 1996). 

b. On July 29, 1996, petitioner filed a notice of ap- 
peal from the district court’s decision denying his 
motion. J.A. 40. The court of appeals treated the 
notice of appeal as an application for a certificate of 
appealability. J.A. 1, 4,41. The case was docketed in 
the court of appeals as No. 96-3118, and the record 
was transferred to the court of appeals from the dis- 
trict court. J.A.3, 4. The court of appeals referred 
the application to a panel consisting of three circuit 
judges. J.A. 4. 

The panel of the court of appeals denied petitioner a 
certificate of appealability, concluding that he had not 
made a “substantial showing of the denial of a consti- 
tutional right.” J.A. 41-42. The court reasoned that, 
because petitioner’s claim relies on Bailey, “peti- 
tioner is not making a constitutional claim. He is 
making a claim to a federal statutory right.” J.A. 42. 
Because “Bailey did no more than interpret a statute, 
and an incorrect application of a statute by a district 
court, or any other court, does not violate the Consti- 
tution,” the court of appeals denied a certificate of 
appealability. Jbid. 

Judge McMillian dissented. J.A. 42-48. After not- 
ing that courts have granted post-conviction relief 
following a change in the law, the dissent stated that 
“a judgment in a criminal case in which the prosecu- 
tion has offered and the record discloses no proof 


6 


whatever of various elements of the crime charged 
has a fatal constitutional taint for lack of due process 
of law.” J.A. 47 (quoting United States v. Liguori, 
438 F.2d 663, 669 (2d Cir. 1971)). The dissent also 
believed that petitioner had “cause” for failing to 
challenge the jury instructions on appeal “[bJecause 
Bailey represents such a clear break with precedent 
regarding the definition of ‘use’ under § 924(c)(1).” 
J.A. 48. Accordingly, the dissent concluded that this 
case presented a “substantial showing of the denial of 
a constitutional right.” Jbid. 

c. Petitioner sought rehearing, with a suggestion 
for rehearing en banc. In opposing further review, 
the government took the position that the panel’s 
decision was correct. The full court of appeals denied 
rehearing en banc, with Judges McMillian, Beam, 
Loken, and Hansen dissenting. J.A. 49. 

4. On May 12, 1997, petitioner filed a petition for a 
writ of certiorari in this Court, invoking the Court’s 
jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. 1254(1). The petition 
sought review of the court of appeals’ holding that 
petitioner was not entitled to a certificate of ap- 
pealability because his claim—that he is presently 
incarcerated for conduct that, under Bailey, is not 
criminal—raises a statutory, not a constitutional, 
issue. The government filed a brief in response, 
agreeing that claims of the kind raised by petitioner 
in his motion under Section 2255 may fairly be char- 
acterized as “constitutional,” and that the court of 
appeals erred in concluding otherwise. The govern- 
ment suggested that the judgment of the court of 
appeals should be vacated and the case remanded for 
further consideration whether a certificate of ap- 
pealability should issue. Gov’t Br. in Resp. 7, 15. 


7 


On October 31, 1997, the Court granted certiorari 
limited to the question whether, in light of the fact 
that the court of appeals denied petitioner’s request 
for a certificate of appealability, the Court has juris- 
diction to grant certiorari, vacate, and remand this 
case per the suggestion of the Solicitor General. The 
Court invited Jeffrey S. Sutton, Esquire, of Colum- 
bus, Ohio, to brief and argue this case, as amicus 
curiae, against the Court’s jurisdiction. J.A. 50. 


SUMMARY OF ARGUMENT 


This Court has jurisdiction under the All Writs 
Act to grant a common-law writ of certiorari to 
review the court of appeals’ denial of petitioner’s 
application for a certificate of appealability, vacate the 
judgment of the court of appeals, and remand for fur- 
ther consideration whether a certificate should issue. 
The Court first held in House v. Mayo, 324 U.S. 42 
(1945) (per curiam), that it had jurisdiction to review 
the denial of an application for a certificate of probable 
cause—the predecessor and functional equivalent of a 
certificate of appealability. The Court has exercised 
certiorari jurisdiction in that context at least 14 
times since House. 

The Court’s jurisdiction was not affected by the 
Anti-Terrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 
1996 (AEDPA), Pub. L. No. 104-132, 110 Stat. 1214. In 
the AEDPA, Congress extended to Section 2255 mo- 
tions filed by federal prisoners, such as petitioner, the 
requirement that state prisoners petitioning for 
habeas corpus under 28 U.S.C. 2254 must obtain a 
certificate authorizing appeal before they may appeal 
the denial of their petitions. Congress thus elimi- 
nated prisoners’ appeals as of right from denials of 
Section 2255 motions. Congress did not, however, 


8 


limit this Court’s discretion to review by writ of 
certiorari the denial of an application for a certificate 
of appealability. 

There are two possible bases for the Court’s power 
to review denials of certificates of appealability— 
the statutory certiorari jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. 
1254(1) and the authority to issue common-law writs 
of certiorari under the All Writs Act, 28 U.S.C. 
165l(a). The Court lacks jurisdiction under Section 
1254(1) to review the merits of an underlying Section 
2255 motion until a certificate of appealability has 
issued. Absent a certificate, a court of appeals lacks 
jurisdiction to review the merits of a Section 2255 
motion, and the motion is therefore not “in” the court 
of appeals within the meaning of Section 1254(1). 
The Court arguably has jurisdiction under Section 
1254(1), however, to review the denial by a court of 
appeals of an application for a certificate of appeal- 
ability. Applications for certificates of appealability, 
which the courts of appeals have jurisdiction to decide 
under 28 U.S.C. 2253(c) (Supp. II 1996), see Jn re 
Burwell, 350 U.S. 521, 522 (1956) (per curiam), could 
be viewed as “cases in the courts of appeals.” 

Nonetheless, jurisdiction under Section 1254(1) ap- 
pears to be foreclosed by this Court’s precedent. See, 
e.g., House v. Mayo, supra. On the assumption that 
jurisdiction is unavailable under Section 1254(1), how- 
ever, this Court’s precedent makes it clear that the 
Court has jurisdiction to review the denial of a cer- 
tificate in petitioner’s case by a common-law writ of 
certiorari under the All Writs Act. 


THIS COURT HAS JURISDICTION TO REVIEW 
BY COMMON-LAW WRIT OF CERTIORARI THE 
ORDER OF THE COURT OF APPEALS DENYING 
PETITIONER'S APPLICATION FOR A CERTIFI- 
CATE OF APPEALABILITY 


This Court’s jurisdiction to review the denial by a 
court of appeals of a certificate authorizing appeal 
from a decision on collateral review was explored 
most recently in an exchange of opinions between 
Justices Rehnquist and Stevens in Davis v. Jacobs, 
454 U.S. 911 (1981). Justice Stevens contended that 
this Court’s jurisdiction under the All Writs Act, 28 
U.S.C. 1651(a), to review denials of certificates was 
settled in House v. Mayo, 324 U.S. 42 (1945) (per 
curiam). See 454 U.S. at 912-914 (Stevens, J., respect- 
ing the denial of certiorari). Justice Rehnquist main- 
tained that House was inconsistent with the princi- 
ples governing the exercise of All Writs Act jurisdic- 
tion and with Congress’s decision to make a certifi- 
cate a prerequisite to appellate consideration of 
a petition for collateral review. See id. at 915-919 
(Rehnquist, J., dissenting from the denial of certio- 
rari); see also Jeffries v. Barksdale, 453 U.S. 914 
(1981) (Rehnquist, J., dissenting from the denial of 
certiorari). 

In our view, House and later cases firmly establish 
this Court’s jurisdiction under the All Writs Act to 
review a court of appeals’ denial of a certificate grant- 
ing leave to appeal a petition for habeas corpus under 
28 U.S.C. 2254. That jurisdiction was not revoked 
when Congress in the Anti-Terrorism and Effective 
Death Penalty Act of 1996 (AEDPA), Pub. L. No. 104- 
132, 110 Stat. 1214, extended the certificate require- 


10 


ment to appeals from denials of Section 2255 motions, 
such as petitioner’s. Although 28 U.S.C. 1254(1) could 
be read to provide jurisdiction to review the denial of a 
certificate, that reading of Section 1254(1) appears 
foreclosed by this Court’s precedent, which instead 
bases jurisdiction on the All Writs Act. That use of 
the All Writs Act is fully consistent with the cases 
and principles defining the scope of this Court’s 
jurisdiction under that Act. 
A. The Court's Precedent Firmly Establishes Cer- 
tiorari Jurisdiction To Review The Denial Of 


A Certificate Of Appealability, And That Juris- 
diction Was Not Revoked By The AEDPA 


1. In House v. Mayo, 324 U.S. 42 (1945) (per cu- 
riam), this Court held that it had certiorari jurisdic- 
tion to review the denial of a certificate of probable 
cause—the predecessor and functional equivalent of 
a certificate of appealability. The Court has exer- 
cised certiorari jurisdiction in that context at least 
14 times since House. 

Since 1908, a state prisoner seeking to appeal a dis- 
trict court’s denial of a petition for habeas corpus has 
been required first to secure a certificate of probable 
cause. See 28 U.S.C. 2253 (1994). In House, the dis- 
trict court denied House’s petition for habeas corpus 


2 Before enactment of the AEDPA, Section 2253 stated: 
“An appeal may not be taken to the court of appeals from the 
final order in a habeas corpus proceeding where the detention 
complained of arises out of process issued by a State court, 
unless the justice or judge who rendered the order or a circuit 
justice or judge issues a certificate of probable cause.” That 
requirement originated in the Act of March 10, 1908, ch. 76, 35 
Stat. 40. The requirement was subsequently amended by the 
Act of February 13, 1925, ch. 229, §§ 6(d), 13, 43 Stat. 940, 942, 
and the Act of June 25, 1948, ch. 646, § 2253, 62 Stat. 967.

and declined to issue a certificate, and the court of 
appeals rejected his application to appeal in forma 
pauperis because House had not secured a certificate. 
324 U.S. at 43-44. The court of appeals did not con- 
sider whether it should issue a certificate. Jd. at 44. 
Holding that the court of appeals “erred in not con- 
sidering whether the case was an appropriate one for 
a certificate of probable cause” and that the district 
court erred in denying the petition, this Court re- 
versed both the court of appeals and the district court. 
Id. at 48. The Court stated that it did not have ju- 
risdiction to take that action under 28 U.S.C. 347(a) 
(1940), the predecessor to Section 1254(1), but had 
jurisdiction to issue a common-law writ of certiorari 
under the All Writs Act (then codified at 28 U.S.C. 
377 (1940)). 324 U.S. at 44; see page 21, infra. 

At least 14 times since House, this Court has 
granted certiorari to review the decision of a court of 
appeals denying an application for a certificate of 
probable cause. See Burwell v. Teets, 350 U.S. 808, 
809 (1955) (per curiam); Rogers v. Teets, 350 U.S. 809 
(1955) (per curiam); United States ex rel. Jennings v. 
Ragen, 358 U.S. 276, 277 (1959) (per curiam); Jn re 
Shuttlesworth, 369 U.S. 35 (1962) (per curiam); 
Maxwell v. Bishop, 385 U.S. 650 (1967) (per curiam); 
Walker v. Wainwright, 390 U.S. 335, 336-337 (1968) 
(per curiam); Conway v. California Adult Auth., 
393 U.S. 1062 (order granting petition for certiorari), 
cert. dismissed, 396 U.S. 107, 109 (1969) (per curiam); 
Humphrey v. Cady, 401 U.S. 973 (1971) (order 
granting petition for certiorari), opinion issued, 405 
U.S. 504, 506 (1972); Smith v. Digmon, 434 U.S. 332, 
333-334 (1978) (per curiam); Allen v. Hardy, 478 U.S. 
255, 257-258 (1986) (per curiam); Graham v. Lynaugh, 
492 U.S. 915 (1989) (order granting petition for 


12 


certiorari, vacating the decision of the court of ap- 
peals, and remanding); Selvage v. Lynaugh, 493 U.S. 
888, 889 (1989) (order granting petition for certiorari), 
opinion issued sub nom. Selvage v. Collins, 494 U.S. 
108, 109-110 (1990) (per curiam); Lozada v. Deeds, 498 
U.S. 430, 431 (1991) (per curiam); Lynce v. Mathis, 116 
S. Ct. 1671 (1996) (order granting petition for cer- 
tiorari), opinion issued, 117 S. Ct. 891, 893 (1997). 
Thus, this Court’s precedent firmly establishes its 
certiorari jurisdiction to review the denial by a court 
of appeals of an application for a certificate of probable 
cause."

an appeal as of right on the merits of their Section 
2255 motions. Section 102 of the AEDPA amended 
28 U.S.C. 2253 to require both federal and state 
prisoners to obtain a “certificate of appealability”

Notably absent from the revised language of Sec- 
tion 2253 is a statement restricting this Court’s cer- 
tiorari jurisdiction to review decisions by the courts 
of appeals on applications for certificates. Congress 
is presumed to legislate with a full understanding of 
existing law, including this Court’s precedents. See 
North Star Steel Co. v. Thomas, 515 U.S. 29, 34 (1995); 
Cannon v. University of Chicago, 441 U.S. 677, 699 
(1979). Thus, had Congress intended to revoke the 
firmly established jurisdiction of this Court, it would 
have done so expressly. Indeed, other provisions of 
the AEDPA demonstrate that, when Congress de- 
sires to limit the Court’s certiorari power, it knows 
how to do so. The AEDPA amended 28 U.S.C. 2244 to 
create a new requirement that a court of appeals 
authorize the filing of a second or successive ha- 
beas corpus application. See § 106, 110 Stat. 1221, 
28 U.S.C. 2244(b)(3)(A) (Supp. II 1996). Congress spe- 
cifically provided that the “grant or denial of an 
authorizarion * * * shall not be appealable and shall 
not be the subject of a petition * * * for a writ of 
certiorari.” IJbid., 28 U.S.C. 2244(b)(3)(E) (Supp. I 
1996). “[{W]here Congress includes particular lan- 
guage in one section of a statute but omits it in 
another section of the same Act, it is generally 
presumed that Congress acts intentionally and 
purposely in the disparate inclusion or exclusion.” 
Russello v. United States, 464 U.S. 16, 23 (1983). The 
logical conclusion, therefore, is that Congress did not

certificate. § 102, 110 Stat. 1218, 28 U.S.C. 2253(c)(2) (Supp. I 
1996). Second, it required that the certificate “indicate which

2253(c)(3) (Supp. II 1996).

intend to restrict this Court’s certiorari jurisdiction 
to review denials of applications for certificates of 
appealability. See Lindh v. Murphy, 117 S. Ct. 2059, 
2068 (1997) (reasoning that, because the AEDPA ex- 
pressly provides that the new amendments regarding 
habeas proceedings in capital cases apply to pending 
cases, by negative implication the amendments in the 
noncapital context apply only to cases filed after 
enactment). See also Felker v. Turpin, 116 S. Ct. 
2333, 2338-2339 (1996) (noting this Court’s hesitance 
to find that its jurisdiction has been repealed by 
implication and concluding that the AEDPA’s preclu- 
sion of certiorari jurisdiction in Section 2244(b)(3)(E) 
did not restrict this Court’s jurisdiction to entertain 
original writs of habeas corpus). 


B. The Court’s Precedent Supports Certiorari 
Jurisdiction Under The All Writs Act 


1. There are two possible bases for the Court’s 
jurisdiction to review denials of certificates of ap- 
pealability. One possible basis is the Court’s statu- 
tory certiorari power under 28 U.S.C. 1254(1), and the 
other is its power to issue common-law writs of cer- 
tiorari under the All Writs Act, 28 U.S.C. 1651(a). 
Section 1254(1) authorizes the Court to grant certio- 
rari to review “cases in the courts of appeals.” Sec- 
tion 1651(a) authorizes the Court to grant “all writs 
necessary or appropriate in aid of [its] jurisdiction[] 
and agreeable to the usages and principles of law.” 

A case is “in” the court of appeals within the mean- 
ing of Section 1254(1) only if the case is docketed and 
pending in the court of appeals. See Gay v. Ruff, 292 
U.S. 25, 30 (1934). In addition, this Court may review 
the merits of the case only if the court of appeals 
properly has jurisdiction to do so. See Nixon v. Fitz- 


16 


gerald, 457 U.S. 731, 742-743 & n.23 (1982); United 
States v. Nixon, 418 U.S. 683, 690 (1974). A court of 
appeals lacks jurisdiction to review the merits of a 
prisoner’s Section 2255 motion unless the prisoner 
first obtains a certificate authorizing an appeal. See 
§ 102, 110 Stat. 1217, 28 U.S.C. 2253(c)(1) (Supp. I 
1996) (“Unless a circuit justice or judge issues a 
certificate of appealability, an appeal may not be taken 
to the court of appeals”). Therefore, in the absence of 
a certificate of appealability, the Supreme Court lacks 
jurisdiction to issue a writ of certiorari under Sec- 
tion 1254(1) to review the merits of a Section 2255 
motion even if the court of appeals has passed on the 
merits. See McCarthy v. Harper, 449 U.S. 1309, 1310 
(1981) (Rehnquist, J., in chambers). 

The Supreme Court might nonetheless have juris- 
diction under Section 1254(1) to review the decision of 
a court of appeals on an application for a certificate of 
appealability. The Court explained in Jn re Burwell, 
350 U.S. 521 (1956) (per curiam), that a court of ap- 
peals has jurisdiction under Section 2253 to pass on 


® If there is a question whether the court of appeals has ju- 
risdiction to review the merits of a case, this Court has juris- 
diction to resolve that question. See, e.g., Nixon v. Fitzgerald, 
457 U.S. at 743 & n.23 (reversing court of appeals’ dismissal for 
lack of jurisdiction and noting that “[tJhere can be no serious 
doubt concerning our power to review a court of appeals’ deci- 
sion to dismiss for lack of jurisdiction”); Gardner v. Westing- 
house Broadcasting Co., 437 U.S. 478, 479 (1978) (affirming 
court of appeals’ dismissal for lack of jurisdiction); Firestone 
Tire & Rubber Co. v. Risjord, 449 U.S. 368, 370 (1981) (revers- 
ing court of appeals for improperly exercising jurisdiction); 
Liberty Mutual Ins. Co. v. Wetzel, 424 U.S. 737, 740 (1976) 
(same). 


17 


an application for a certificate. Jd. at 522.’ Thus, an 
application, such as petitioner’s, that has been dock- 


7 Nothing in the amendments to Section 2253 made by the 
AEDPA alters the conclusion in Jn re Burwell. The language 
that gives a court of appeals jurisdiction to issue a certificate 
was not changed. Compare § 102, 110 Stat. 1217, 28 U.S.C. 
2253(c)(1) (Supp. II 1996) (“[uJnless a circuit justice or judge 
issues a certificate of appealability”) with 28 U.S.C. 2253 (1952) 
(“unless the justice or judge who rendered the order or a 
circuit justice or judge issues a certificate of probable cause”). 

The language in both versions of Section 2253 could be read 
to give authority to issue a certificate to an individual judge 
rather than a court. In Jn re R. gers, 350 U.S. 521 n.* (1956), 
the companion case to Jn re Burwell, the Ninth Circuit con- 
tended that Section 2253 did not give jurisdiction to the court 
of appeals, “as a court,” to rule on an application for a certifi- 
cate of probable cause. See Jn re Rogers, On Certificate of 
Questions from the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Ninth Circuit at 8-10. This Court rejected the Ninth Circuit’s 
argument and explained that the Court had held in Burwell v. 
Teets, supra, and Rogers v. Teets, supra, that Section 2253 
gives the courts of appeals jurisdiction to issue certificates of 
probable cause. Jn re Burwell, 350 U.S. at 522. Despite the de- 
cision in Jn re Burwell, some confusion has persisted whether 
jurisdiction lies in a court of appeals as a “court” or as an as- 
semblage of “judges.” Compare Fed. R. App. P. 22(b) advi- 
sory committee’s note (1967 adoption) (judges have jurisdic- 
tion) with Ojeda Rios v. Wigen, 863 F.2d 196, 200 n.3 (2d Cir. 
1988) (Newman, J., in chambers) (calling Advisory Committee 
interpretation of Jn re Burwell “somewhat surprising” and 
maintaining court has jurisdiction). See also Lara v. Nelson, 
449 F.2d 323, 324 (9th Cir. 1971) (citing Rule 22(b) and suggest- 
ing that judges, rather than court, have jurisdiction); Ex parte 
Farrell, 189 F.2d 540, 543 (1st Cir.) (suggesting court has 
jurisdiction), cert. denied, 342 U.S. 839 (1951). In any event, if 
Section 2253 does not give the courts of appeals jurisdiction to 
pass on an application for a certificate, they likely have that 
authority under the All Writs Act. See Ojeda Rios, 863 F.2d at 
200 n.3. 


18 


eted in the court of appeals could be said to be prop- 
erly “in the court[] of appeals.” This Court would 
still not have jurisdiction to review the decision on 
the application, however, unless the application is a 
“case,” distinct from the underlying Section 2255 
motion.® 

The Court’s treatment of certain analogous mat- 
ters may support the proposition that an application 
for a certificate of appealability is a “case” under Sec- 
tion 1254(1). For example, this Court has considered 
appeals from interlocutory orders to be “cases in the 
courts of appeals” under Section 1254(1). See Nixon 
v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. at 742 (rejecting argument 
that the appeal of an order denying immunity claim 
“was not an appealable ‘case’ properly ‘in’ the Court of 
Appeals within the meaning of §1254”); United States 
v. Nixon, 418 U.S. at 692 (referring to the appeal of 
a denial of a motion to quash a subpoena as a “case 
* * * now properly before this Court on [a] writ of 
certiorari” under Section 1254(1)). The underlying 
damages action in Nixon v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. at 
740-741, and criminal prosecution in United States v. 
Nixon, 418 U.S. at 687-688, had not yet gone to trial in 
the district court, and, like Section 2255 proceedings

This Court has also apparently treated motions to 
intervene in cases pending in the courts of appeals as 
independent “cases” under Section 1254(1). Section 
1254(1) permits only a “party to any civil or criminal 
case” to seek certiorari review. The Court has none- 
theless allowed a litigant who has been refused inter- 
vention by the court of appeals to obtain review in this 
Court, but has stated that review extends only to the 
order denying the motion to intervene. See /nter- 
national Union, United Automobile Workers v. 
Scofield, 382 U.S. 205, 208-209 (1965). One explanation 
for that result is that the Court viewed the motion to 
intervene as a separate “case” to which the litigant 


was a “party.””

Section 2253(c). That distinction, however, does not affect the 
conclusion whether there is a “case” in the court of appeals 
under Section 1254(1). The language of Section 1254(1) does 
not limit the power of review to “cases” arising under any 
particular jurisdictional authority; and the Court has relied 
on Section 1254(1) to review a court of appeals’ decision 
denying mandamus, which a court of appeals has jurisdiction

courts of appeals, did not authorize the Court to review the 
denial by a court of appeals of a mandamus petition).

The Court’s view of what constitutes a “case” for 
purposes of Section 1254(1) thus appears quite broad. 
Under that broad view, Section 1254(1) could be inter- 
preted to authorize the Court’s review, as a separate 
“case,” of a court of appeals’ decision denying a cer- 
tificate, although that authority would not extend to 
review of the merits of the underlying motion that the 
court of appeals denied permission to appeal. 

That interpretation of Section 1254(1) may find fur- 
ther support in the acknowledged power of the Court 
under Section 1254(1) to decide whether a court of ap- 
peals has properly dismissed a case for lack of juris- 
diction. See Nixon v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. at 743 & 
n.23; note 6, supra. The decision by a court of appeals 
whether to grant a certificate of appealability is func- 
tionally equivalent to a decision whether it has juris- 
diction to consider the merits of the underlying ap- 
peal. There is, however, a distinction between the two 
situations in form, if not in function, and that distine- 
tion may justify treating the two situations differ- 
ently. By requiring a prisoner to obtain a certificate 
of appealability in order to authorize an appeal from 
denial of a habeas petition or Section 2255 motion, 
Congress has established a two-stage procedure for 
the appeal, the first stage of which is in essence a

2. In any event, this Court rejected jurisdiction 
under Section 1254(1) to review a certificate authoriz- 
ing appeal in House v. Mayo, supra. In House, the 
Court explained that it could not issue a statutory 
writ of certiorari under a predecessor to Section 
1254(1) because the case “was never ‘in’ the court of 
appeals, for want of a certificate of probable cause.” 
324 U.S. at 44. For that proposition, the Court cited 
Ferguson v. District of Columbia, 270 U.S. 633 (1926) 
(per curiam), in which the Court denied “for want of 
jurisdiction in this [C)ourt” a petition for a statutory 
writ of certiorari to review the refusal of a Justice of 
the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia to 
allow a writ of error to the Police Court of the Dis- 
trict. Jd. at 634. The Court in House noted that, de- 
spite the unavailability of a statutory writ, “[bly vir- 
tue of [the All Writs Act] we may grant a writ of 
certiorari to review the action of the court of appeals 
in declining to allow an appeal to it.” 324 U.S. at 44. 
Only at that point in its analysis did the Court go on 
to note: “And not only does our review extend to a 
determination of whether the circuit court of appeals 
abused its discretion in refusing to allow the appeal, 
but if so, it extends also to questions on the merits 
sought to be raised by the appeal.” Jd. at 44-45. Thus, 
House holds that the Court must rely on the All 
Writs Act to review the denial of a certificate. 


22 


The Court viewed House as another in a group of 
cases in which it had used the common-law writ of 
certiorari to review decisions by appellate courts 
declining to allow appeals. See 324 U.S. at 44. The 
first of those cases, Jn re 620 Church Street Bldg. 
Corp., 299 U.S. 24, 26 (1936), involved the denial of 
leave to appeal in a bankruptcy proceeding. Signifi- 
cantly, in that case, the Court reviewed only the 
decision of the court of appeals denying leave to appeal 
and not the merits of the underlying case. And, in 
that case, as in House, the Court held that it lacked 
jurisdiction to issue a statutory writ of certiorari. 
See 299 U.S. at 26. 

The other cases on which the Court relied in House 
involved decisions by courts of appeals denying re- 
quests for leave to appeal in forma pauperis (IFP). 
See Holiday v. Johnston, 313 U.S. 342, 348 n.2 (1941); 
Wells v. United States, 318 U.S. 257 (1943); Steffler v. 
United States, 319 U.S. 38 (1943). In one of those 
cases as well, the Court reviewed only the decision of 
the court of appeals denying leave to appeal and not 
the merits of the underlying case. See Wells, supra."

considered itself bound when a subsequent case fi-

C. All Writs Act Principles Justify The Use Of 
Common-Law Certiorari To Review Denials Of 
Certificates Of Appealability In Ap ~opriate 
Cases 

The All Writs Act authorizes this Court to issue 
extraordinary or common-law writs when (1) “neces- 
sary or appropriate in aid of [its] jurisdiction[]” and 
(2) “agreeable to the usages and principles of law.” 
See 28 U.S.C. 1651(a). Using a common-law writ of 
certiorari to review the court of appeals’ denial to 


27 


petitioner of a certificate of appealability is fully 
consistent with that authorization. 

Although “sparingly exercised,” Parr v. United 
States, 351 U.S. 518, 520 (1956), this Court’s power 
under the All Writs Act is quite broad. See La Buy v. 
Howes Leather Co., 352 U.S. 249, 254-255 (1957); 
Roche v. Evaporated Milk Ass’n, 319 U.S. 21, 25 
(1943) (“The common law writs, like equitable reme- 
dies, may be granted or withheld in the sound discre- 
tion of the court.”). This Court is “slow to reach a 
conclusion which would deprive the [CJourt of the 
power to issue the [common-law] writ [of certiorari] 
in proper cases to review the action of the Federal 
courts inferior in jurisdiction to this [C]ourt.” 
McClellan v. Carland, 217 U.S. 268, 279 (1910). “The 
suggestion, therefore, that this case should be dis- 
missed for want of power in this [CJourt to grant the 
writ of certiorari cannot be entertained.” Ibid. 

1. If review of the order of the court of appeals is 
unavailable under Section 1254(1), resort to a 
common-law writ of certiorari is “necessary” 
“in aid of” this Court’s appellate jurisdiction 

A common-law writ of certiorari to review the 
court of appeals’ denial of petitioner’s application for a 
certificate of appealability is necessary in aid of this 
Court’s appellate jurisdiction.” Issuance of a writ in

a constitutionally permissible exercise of this Court's appellate 
power under Article III. See, e.g., Ex parte Crane, 30 U.S. (5 
Pet.) 190, 193 (1831) (“mandamus to an inferior court of the 
United States[] is in the nature of appellate jurisdiction”). See 
also Felker v. Turpin, 116 S. Ct. at 2339 (treating consideration 
of original writ of habeas corpus in this Court as an exercise of 
appellate jurisdiction); id. at 2341 n.1 (Souter, J., concurring) 
(explaining that habeas petition, although filed in the first 


28 


this case is necessary both to prevent unauthorized 
action by the court of appeals from precluding the 
Court’s exercise of its appellate function and to 
enable the Court to supervise the courts of appeals in 
their application of Section 2253(c). 

“Where a case is within the appellate jurisdiction 
of the higher court [an extraordinary writ] may issue 
in aid of the appellate jurisdiction which might other- 
wise be defeated by the unauthorized action of the 
court below.” McClellan, 217 U.S. at 280. Thus, “this 
[Cjourt ha[s] the power to issue [an extraordinary 
writ] to compel the Circuit Courts to proceed to final 
judgment in order that this [C]ourt may exercise the 
jurisdiction of review given by law.” IJbid. See, e.g.,

damus to require a district court to issue a bench

(mandamus to require a district court to allow Japa-

the court of appeals erroneously denied petitioner 
a certificate of appealability, this Court would be 
wrongfully precluded from exercising, at a later point 
in the proceedings, its statutory certiorari power 
to review the merits of petitioner’s Section 2255 
motion.” 


instance in this Court, is “nonetheless for constitutional pur- 
poses an exercise of this Court’s appellate (rather than original) 
jurisdiction”). Cf. Marbury v. Madison, 5 U.S. (1 Cranch) 137, 
175 (1803) (the “essential criterion of appellate jurisdiction [is] 
that it revises and corrects the proceedings in a cause already

A common-law writ of certiorari is also necessary 
in cases such as petitioner’s to enable this Court 
to conduct its appellate function of supervising the 
courts of appeals. Cf. La Buy, 352 U.S. at 259-260 
(“supervisory control of the District Courts by the 
Courts of Appeals is necessary to proper judicial 
administration in the federal system”). Cf. Sup. Ct. 
R. 10(a) (noting that need for “an exercise of this 
Court’s supervisory power” is a reason for granting 
certiorari).” Extraordinary writs may be used to 
correct a “clear abuse of discretion,” Bankers Life & 
Casualty Co. v. Holland, 346 U.S. 379, 382-383 (1953), 
particularly one that involves a court’s refusal to ex- 
ercise its jurisdiction, La Buy, 352 U.S. at 257. Al- 
though this Court will not issue mandamus unless 
the petitioner shows a “clear and indisputable” right 
to the writ, see, e.g., Allied Chem. Corp. v. Daiflon, 
Inc., 449 U.S. 33, 36 (1980) (per curiam), the Court 
does not require a similar showing before issuing 
common-law certiorari.” As discussed at pages 21-23, 
supra, this Court has frequently used the common-

Cf. United States v. Dickinson, 213 U.S. 92, 93 (1909) (de-

law writ of certiorari to supervise the courts of 
appeals in cases involving the denial of leave to appeal. 
Those cases have not always involved a “clear abuse 
of discretion,” as is demonstrated by the fact that, in 
several of the cases, this Court ultimately affirmed 
the court of appeals. See, e.g., Jn re 620 Church Street 
Bldg. Corp., supra (using common-law writ of certio- 
rari to review and affirm denial of leave to appeal in 
bankruptcy); Wells v. United States, supra (same 
with respect to denial by court of appeals of leave to 
appeal in forma pauperis). See also Allen v. Hardy, 
supra (using writ of certiorari to review and affirm 
denial of certificate of probable cause). 

A common-law writ of certiorari is necessary in 
this case to correct the erroneous application by 
the court of appeals of the standard prescribed by 
Section 2253(c)(2) for issuing certificates of ap- 
pealability. If the error in this case goes uncorrected, 
the court of appeals might improperly refuse to per- 
form its appellate function to review habeas petitions 
and Section 2255 motions. And, if this Court lacks the 
power to correct the error made by the court of 
appeals here, it presumably also lacks the power to 
correct a court of appeals that interprets or applies 
Section 2253(c) in violation of equal protection or 
other fundamental constitutional principles. 

Finally, use of a common-law writ of certiorari is 
necessary to enable the Court to ensure uniformity in 
how the different circuits interpret the standard for 
issuance of certificates of appealability. Cf. Forsyth 
v. City of Hammond, 166 U.S. 506, 512 (1897) (“Nine 
separate appellate tribunals might by their differ- 
ences of opinion, unless held in check by the review- 
ing power of this court, create an unfortunate confu- 
sion in respect to the rules of Federal decision.”) 


31 


Indeed, the Court previously found it necessary to 
ensure uniformity in the standards used by the courts 
of appeals to decide applications for certificates of 
probable cause. See, e.g., Lozada v. Deeds, 498 U.S. at 
432 (concluding that the court of appeals had erred in 
applying the standards set forth in Barefoot v. Estelle, 
463 U.S. 880, 892-893 (1983), for issuance of a certifi- 
cate, and noting that two other courts of appeals 
would have decided the question differently); Lynce v. 
Mathis, 117 S. Ct. at 893 (granting certiorari to re- 
view the Eleventh Circuit’s denial of a certificate 
“(bjecause the Court of Appeals for the 10th Circuit 
reached a different conclusion on similar facts” on the 
merits of the issue petitioner sought to raise). 
Admittedly, a Circuit Justice or this Court as a 
whole could grant petitioner a certificate of ap- 
pealability. See Davis, 454 U.S. at 913 (Stevens, J., 
respecting the denial of certiorari); id. at 918 (Rehn- 
quist, J., dissenting from the denial of certiorari); 
§ 102, 110 Stat. 1217, 28 U.S.C. 2253(c)(1) (Supp. II 
1996) (authorizing “circuit justice” to grant certifi- 
cate); In re Burwell, supra (holding that authoriza- 
tion of circuit “judge” to grant certificate empowered 
court of appeals to grant one).” That authority does

Justice and referred to Court, although two Justices would

Wisconsin, 484 U.S. 1078 (1988) (denying application presented 
to individual Justice and referred to Court); Thomas v. 
Wainwright, 475 U.S. 1113 (1986) (same); In re Hunt, 348 U.S. 
968 (1955) (denying application); Rosoto v. Warden, 83 S. Ct. 


32 


not mean, however, that use of a common-law writ of 
certiorari is not “necessary” in aid of this Court’s 
appellate jurisdiction. A writ is “necessary” in aid 
of jurisdiction not only if “the [C]Jourt could not 
otherwise physically discharge its appellate duties.” 
See Adams v. United States ex rel. McCann, 317 U.S. 
269, 273 (1942). Its use must only be “reasonably nec- 
essary in the interest of justice.” Jd. at 274; Price v. 
Johnston, 334 U.S. 266, 279 (1948). Common-law cer- 
tiorari is necessary in this case because an original 
application for a certificate of appealability would be a 
clumsy and burdensome means to preserve the po- 
tential to review the merits of petitioner’s Section 
2255 motion or to supervise the interpretation by the 
courts of appeals of the standard for issuing certifi- 
cates.“ Passing on petitioner’s application would 


1788, 1789-1790 (1963) (memorandum of Harlan, J., denying 
application). 

21 Even if an extraordinary writ were not “necessary” in 
aid of the Court’s jurisdiction, a writ is certainly “appropriate” 
here. The Court’s power to use common-law writs when “ap- 
propriate” as well as when “necessary” was clarified by the Act 
of June 25, 1948, ch. 646, § 1651(a), 62 Stat. 944. That Act 
consolidated various existing provisions governing use of the 
common-law writs into Section 1651 and made “necessary 
changes in phraseology,” apparently without intending sub- 
stantive change. See H.R. Rep. No. 308, 80th Cong., 1st Sess. 
Al44 (1947). The House Report does state, however, that the 
revised section is “expressive of the construction recently 
placed upon [the All Writs Act] in U.S. Alkali Export Assn. v. 
[United States, 325 U.S. 196 (1945)], and De Beers Consol. 
Mines v. (United States, supra).” Id. at Al45. Those cases 
reaffirmed the general principle that the All Writs Act may 
not be used to allow review, particularly interlocutory review, 
in the face of plain legislative intent to preclude it. In both, 
however, the Court permitted interlocutory review even 
though not otherwise authorized by statute and even though 


33 


demand far more of this Court’s resources than using 
common-law certiorari to clarify the proper standard 
for use by the court of appeals. The Court would have 
to resolve many legal and factual issues that it would 
not otherwise face. Those issues include whether the 
certificate requirement applies to Section 2255 mo- 
tions pending in district court but not in the court of 
appeals when the AEDPA was enacted, whether peti- 
tioner procedurally defaulted on his claim and, if so, 
whether he has shown cause and prejudice to excuse 
that default, whether the jury instructions at peti- 
tioner’s trial on the meaning of “carries” under 
18 U.S.C. 924(c)(1) were correct, and whether peti- 
tioner’s conduct constituted “carr[ying]” under that 
statute.” Thus, use of the common-law writ of certio- 


appeal following final judgment was available. Thus, the 
addition of the word “appropriate” may signify congressional 
approval of review using the All Writs Act even when review 
is available in another form, such as through an original 
application in this Court for a certificate of appealability. 

As a prudential matter, this Court has also limited use of the 
extraordinary writs to cases in which “no other remedy will 
suffice,” DiBella v. United States, 369 U.S. 121, 125 n.4 (1962), 
and “‘cireumstances imperatively demand[]’ a departure from 
the ordinary remedy,” Whitney v. Dick, 202 U.S. 132, 140 
(1906). See Sup. Ct. R. 20.1. Those standards, however, are 
not limitations on this Court’s jurisdiction. In any event, the 
discussion at pages 27-31, supra, explaining why use of an 
extraordinary writ is “necessary” and the Court’s repeated use 
of certiorari to review refusals by the circuit courts to permit 
appeals, see pages 21-23, supra, demonstrate that the pruden- 
tial standards are met in petitioner’s case. 

2 Petitioner might also be able to petition for a writ of ha- 
beas corpus under 28 U.S.C. 2241. Petitioner would first have 
to demonstrate, however, that a motion under Section 2255 “is 
inadequate or ineffective to test the legality of his detention,” 
28 U.S.C. 2255. In any event, using a petition for habeas corpus 


34 


rari to review the denial by the court of appeals of the 
certificate of appealability in this case is “necessary” 
“in aid of” the Court’s appellate jurisdiction. 
2. Use of a common-law writ of certiorari is 
“agreeable to the usages and principles of law” 


Use of the common-law writ of certiorari in this 
case is not only necessary in aid of this Court’s juris- 
diction but is “agreeable to the usages and principles 
of law.” In determining whether a particular writ is 
“agreeable to the usages and principles of law,” this 
Court looks first to the common law. See United 
States v. Hayman, 342 U.S. 205, 221 n.35 (1952). The 
relief available using a particular writ is not confined 
to the precise forms or uses of the writ at common 
law before enactment of the All Writs Act but depends 
on the “usages and principles which have attached 
themselves to the writ * * * down through the years 
to the present time.” See Price v. Johnston, 334 U.S. 
at 282-283. 

As early as 1897, this Court held that it “can 
undoubtedly issue writs of certiorari in all proper 
cases” under the authority of the All Writs Act. See 
In re Chetwood, 165 U.S. 448, 462 (1897). See also 
Whitney v. Dick, 202 U.S. 132, 138 (1906) (“It may be 
said that the power of this court to issue original and 


as the vehicle to ensure that the courts of appeals are properly 
interpreting the standard for issuing certificates of appealabil- 
ity would be as cumbersome as using an original application for 
a certificate. The Court has already exhibited a preference for 
continued use of certiorari, rather than habeas corpus, to re- 
view denials of certificates authorizing an appeal. See, e.g., Jn 
re Shuttlesworth, 369 U.S. at 35 (treating application for | a- 
beas corpus as a petition for certiorari to review circuit judge’s 
denial of certificate of probable cause, granting certiorari, va- 
cating, and remanding to district court). 


35 


independent writs of certiorari has been upheld under 
the authority given by” the All Writs Act). That 
power is explicitly recognized in the current rules 
of this Court. See Sup. Ct. R. 20.6 (referring to 
common-law writs of certiorari). 

Like the other extraordinary writs, the common- 
law writ of certiorari has traditionally been used “to 
confine inferior courts to the exercise of their pre- 
scribed jurisdiction or to compel them to exercise 
their authority when it is their duty to do so.” See 
U.S. Alkali Export Ass’n v. United States, 325 U.S. 
196, 202 (1945). See, e.g., In re Chetwood, 165 U.S. at 
462 (using common-law writ of certiorari to prevent 
circuit court from requiring dismissal of writ of 
error pending in this Court); Whitney v. Dick, 202 
U.S. at 135 (using common-law certiorari to review 
the propriety of the use by a court of appeals of the 
writs of habeas corpus and certiorari to reverse a 
district court criminal conviction).” 

As discussed above, pages 21-23, supra, this Court 
has repeatedly used the common-law writ of cer- 
tiorari to require the courts of appeals to entertain 
appeals that they have refused to allow. See, ¢.g., 
House v. Mayo, supra; Holiday v. Johnston, supra. 
The Court has also used the common-law writ of cer- 
tiorari to correct legal errors in decisions of the 


® See also Ex parte Crane, 30 U.S. (5 Pet.) at 193 (Court 
had power to issue mandamus to order a circuit court to sign 
bill of exceptions); Ex parte Bradstreet, 32 U.S. (7. Pet.) 634, 
648 (1833) (mandamus to district court that improperly refused 
to take a case to trial); Insurance Co. v. Comstock, 83 U.S. (16 
Wall.) 258, 270 (1873) (mandamus may be issued to compel a 
cireuit court to decide an appeal in bankruptcy); Ez parte 
Simons, 247 U.S. 231, 240 (1918) (mandamus to require district 
court to allow jury trial of suit to enforce a promised bequest). 


36 


courts of appeals and district courts when other 
means of review would not provide an adequate 
remedy. See, e.g., De Beers Consol. Mines, Ltd. v. 
United States, 325 U.S. 212 (1945); McClellan v. 
Carland, supra; Whitney °. Dick, supra; In re 
Chetwood, supra. See also “far East Conference v. 
United States, 342 U.S. 570 (1952). Those are the 
precise functions for which the writ would be exer- 
vised in this case. 

Use of the writ to review the order by the court of 
appeals in this case does not conflict with the princi- 
ple established in United States v. Beatty, 232 U.S. 
463, 467-468 (1914), that the writ cannot be used as a 
substitute for an authorized appeal when one is avail- 
able. If the Court adheres to its precedent precluding 
review under Section 1254(1), the order of the court of 
appeals denying petitioner a certificate of appealabil- 
ity would not be reviewable except by use of an ex- 
traordinary writ. As discussed above, pages 31-34, 
supra, an original application for a certificate in this 
Court would not be an adequate substitute for direct 
review of the order. 

Nor does use of the common-law writ conflict with 
the related principle that the All Writs Act should not 
ordinarily be used to permit review in the face of a 
plain legislative purpose to preclude review. See, e.g., 
Roche, 319 U.S. at 27-28. This Court’s precedent is 
clear that the lack of statutory certiorari power 
under Section 1254(1) does not preclude the exercise 
of jurisdiction under the All Writs Act. See, e.g., Ex 
parte Republic of Peru, 318 U.S. 578, 585 n.4 (1943) 
(the express grant of statutory certiorari jurisdiction 
in the Judiciary Act of 1925, the predecessor to 
Section 1254(1), did not curtail this Court’s power to 
issue common-law writs); Jn re 620 Church Street 


37 


Bldg. Corp., 299 U.S. at 26; McClellan, 217 U.S. at 
277. In House v. Mayo, 324 U.S. at 44, the Court 
explicitly held that common-law certiorari was avail- 
able to review the denial of a certificate of probable 
cause, despite the fact that Section 2253 prevented an 
appeal on the merits absent a certificate. And, as 
discussed above, pages 12-15, supra, when Congress 
extended the certificate requirement to federal pris- 
oners like petitioner, it made no changes to Section 
2253 to alter the well established rule that this Court 
could use the common-law writ to review the denial of 
a certificate by a court of appeals. Nor is Section 
2253’s denial of appellate review as of right on the 
merits of a Section 2255 motion at all inconsistent 
with this Court’s discretionary review of the ruling 
of a court of appeals on whether to grant an appeal.” 


*% The certificate requirement has its origins in a congres- 
sional desire to limit certain frivolous habeas corpus cases. See 
Jeffries, 453 U.S. at 916 (Rehnquist, J., dissenting from denial 
of certiorari) (citing H.R. Rep. No. 23, 60th Cong., Ist Sess. 
(1908)). The continued availability of common-law certiorari, 
however, should not overburden the Court or respondents by 


writ. 
would not likely reduce the burden on the Court because peti- 
tioners could reformulate their requests for review as original 


applications for certificates of appealability or original petitions 
for habeas corpus. 


38 


This Court’s decisions in U.S. Alkali, supra, and 
De Beers, supra, are not inconsistent with use of the 
common-law writ in this case. Indeed, they support 
its use here. In those cases, the petitioners sought 
interlocutory review of questions arising under a 
statute that provided for direct appeal to this Court 
but did not provide for interlocutory appeals. The 
Court noted in U.S. Alkali that use of the common- 
law writ would generally be inappropriate under those 
circumstances because of the plain intent to preclude 
review on appeal. See 325 U.S. at 203. In both cases, 
however, the Court allowed review because the peti- 
tioners alleged that the district court had exceeded 
its jurisdiction in a manner that could not adequately 
be corrected except by use of the common-law writ. 
See id. at 203-204; De Beers, 325 U.S. at 217. Unlike 
U.S. Alkali and De Beers, this case poses no threat to 
the final judgment rule. Nor, as discussed above, is 
there any indication here of congressional intent to 
preclude this Court’s review. Finally, review is justi- 
fied for reasons parallel to those justifying review in 
the earlier cases: The court of appeals here has re- 
fused to exercise its jurisdiction in a manner that 
cannot be corrected other than by use of the common- 
law writ.” 

Finally, this Court’s jurisdiction to grant a 
common-law writ of certiorari is not defeated by peti- 


* In any event, the principle against use of extraordinary 
writs to circumvent restrictions on interlocutory appeals that 
was reaffirmed in U.S. Alkali and De Beers appears to be 
prudential rather than jurisdictional. See La Buy, 352 U.S. at 
255 (“Since the Court of Appeals could at some stage of the 
antitrust proceedings entertain appeals in these cases, it has 
power in proper circumstances, as here, to issue writs of man- 
damus reaching them.”). 


39 


tioner’s failure specifically to request such a writ. If 
the Court has jurisdiction over a case, the Court may 
review it, even if the parties incorrectly state in their 
papers filed with the Court the basis of the Court’s 
jurisdiction. Indeed, the Court recognized that prin- 
ciple in House v. Mayo, supra, and granted common- 
law certiorari even though the petitioner had invoked 
this Court’s statutory certiorari jurisdiction. See 
also In re 620 Church Street Bldg. Corp., supra 
(granting common-law writ of certiorari to review 
denial by court of appeals of leave to appeal when 
petitioner had invoked statutory certiorari jurisdic- 
tion); In re Shuttlesworth, supra (treating application 
for habeas corpus as a petition for certiorari to review 
circuit judge’s denial of certificate of probable cause, 
granting certiorari, vacating, and remanding to dis- 
trict court); note 15, supra. Indeed, this Court regu- 
larly exercises jurisdiction over cases on a different 
basis than that invoked by the party seeking review. 
See Davis, 454 U.S. at 914 n.3 (Stevens, J., respecting 
the denial of certiorari) (“We do follow the practice in 
handling our appellate docket of treating appeals that 
are improperly taken as though they had been filed as 
petitions for certiorari and then denying them”). See, 
e.g., Head v. California, 374 U.S. 509 (1963) (per 
curiam) (treating request for writ of common-law 
certiorari as request for writ of statutory certiorari, 
granting writ, and vacating and remanding for fur- 
ther consideration); Barefoot v. Estelle, 463 U.S. at 
887 (treating application for a stay of execution as a 
petition for certiorari). 


40 


CONCLUSION 


The Court has jurisdiction to grant the petition for 
certiorari, vacate the judgment of the court of 
appeals, and remand for further consideration.

Section 230 of Title 28, United States Code 


(1940), provided: 


No appeal intended to bring any judgment or de-

2. Section 347(a) of Title 28, United States Code 
(1940), provided: 


(a) In any case, civil or criminal, in a circuit 
court of appeals, or in the United States Court of 
Appeals for the District of Columbia, it shall be 
competent for the Supreme Court of the United 
States, upon the petition of any party thereto, 
whether Government or other litigant, to require 
by certiorari, either before or after a judgment or 
decree by such lower court, that the cause be 
certified to the Supreme Court for determination 
by it with the same power and authority, and with 
like effect, as if the cause had been brought there 
by unrestricted appeal.

(1940), provided: 


The Supreme Court and the district courts 
shall have power to issue writs of scire facias. 
The Supreme Court, the circuit courts of appeals, 
and the district courts shall have power to issue 
all writs not specifically provided for by statute, 
which may be necessary for the exercise of their 
respective jurisdictions, and agreeable to the 
usages and principles of law.

A prisoner in custody under sentence of a court

issue. The certificate or the statement shall be 
forwarded to the court of appeals with the notice 
of appeal and the file of the proceedings in the 
district court. If the district judge has denied the 
certificate, the applicant for the writ may then 
request issuance of the certificate by a circuit
